Arithmetic geometric model for the renormalisation of irrationally indifferent attractors
Davoud Cheraghi

Abstract
. In this paper we build a geometric model for the renormalisation of irrationally indifferent fixed points. The geometric model incorporates the fine arithmetic properties of the rotation number at the fixed point. Using this model for the renormalisation, we build a topological model for the dynamics of a holomorphic map near an irrationally indifferent fixed point. We also explain the topology of the maximal invariant set for the model, and also explain the dynamics of the map on the maximal invariant set.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
37F50 (Primary), 37F10, 46T25 (Secondary)

1.
Introduction

Let
f
be a holomorphic map defined on a neighbourhood of
0
in the complex plane
\mathbb{C}
, and assume that
0
is an

irrationally indifferent fixed point
of
f
, that is,
f
is of the form
e^{2\pi i\alpha}z+O(z^{2})
near
0
with
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
.
It is well-known that such systems exhibit highly non-trivial dynamical behaviour which depends on the
arithmetic properties of
\alpha
, see for instance
[
Sie42
,
Brj71
,
Yoc95b
,
McM98
,
GS03
,
PZ04
,
Zha11
]
.
When the system is unstable near
0
, the local dynamics remains mysterious, even for simple looking non-linear maps such as the quadratic polynomials

e^{2\pi i\alpha}z+z^{2}
.

A powerful tool for the study of irrationally indifferent fixed points is renormalisation.
A
renormalisation scheme
consists of a class of maps (dynamical systems), and an operator which preserves that class of maps.
The operator assigns a new dynamical system to a given dynamical system, using suitable iterates of the
original system. A fundamental renormalisation scheme for the systems with an irrationally indifferent fixed point is the
sector renormalisation of Yoccoz, illustrated in Figure
1
.
In this renormalisation, the new system is obtained from considering the return map to a sector landing at
0
, and the sector is
identified with a neighbourhood of
0
using a change of coordinate.
A remarkable semi-local version of the local Yoccoz renormalisation is built by Inou and Shishikura in
[
IS06
]
,
which is defined on a sector uniformly large in size, and hence captures more dynamical information about the original system.

13,4)

Figure 1.
In the left hand picture,
the (canonically defined) sector landing at
0
is bounded by a curve landing at
0
, the image of that curve by
f
, and a line segment connecting the end points of those curves.
By glueing the sides of the sector according to
f
, one obtains a Riemann surface, isomorphic to a
punctured disk.

A main technical issue with employing a renormalisation method for irrationally indifferent fixed points is the highly distorting nature of the
changes of coordinates that appear in successive applications of the renormalisation.
One is lead to analysing the local and global distorting behaviours of the successive changes of coordinates
in conjunction with the fine arithmetic features of
\alpha
.

In this paper with build a geometric model for the (semi-local version of the) renormalisation of irrationally
indifferent fixed points.

Theorem A

(Renormalisation model)
.

There exists a class of maps

\mathbb{F}=\big\{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\big\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}}

and a renormalisation operator
\mathcal{R}_{m}:\mathbb{F}\to\mathbb{F}
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
,

\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\subset\mathbb{C}
is a compact star-like set with
\{0,+1\}\subset\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
,

(ii)

for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
,

\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
is a homeomorphism which
acts as rotation by
2\pi\alpha
in the tangential direction,

(iii)

for
\alpha\in(-1/2,1/2)\setminus\mathbb{Q}
,

\mathcal{R}_{m}\left(\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\right)=\left(\mathbb{T}_{-1/\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{-1/\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{-1/\alpha}\right),

(iv)

for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
and every integer
k
with

0\leq k\leq 1/|\alpha|
,

\frac{C^{-1}}{1+\min\{k,|\alpha|^{-1}-k\}}\leq|\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{k}}(+1)|\leq\frac{C}{1+\min\{k,|\alpha|^{-1}-k\}},

for some constant
C
independent of
k
and
\alpha
.

(v)

for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
,

\mathbb{M}_{\alpha+1}=\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
,
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha+1}=\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
,

\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha}=s(\mathbb{M}_{\alpha})
, and
\mathbb{T}_{-\alpha}=s\circ\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\circ s
,
where
s
denotes the complex conjugation map,

(vi)

\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
depends continuously on
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
,
in the Hausdorff topology.

The map
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
is a topological model for the map
f(z)=e^{2\pi i\alpha}z+O(z^{2})
.
The set
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
is a topological model for the maximal invariant set of
f
at
0
on which
f
is injective.
The model for the renormalisation,
\mathcal{R}_{m}
, is defined using the return map of
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
to a cone of angle
2\pi\alpha
landing at
0
;
with a change of coordinate which preserves rays landing at 0, while exhibiting the non-linear behaviour of the actual change of coordinate for the sector
renormalisation of
f
.
As in the sector renormalisation,
\mathcal{R}_{m}
induces the Gauss map
\alpha\mapsto-1/\alpha
on the asymptotic rotation numbers at
0
.

The point
+1
is the largest real number which belongs to
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
.
It plays the role of a certain critical point of
f
, which we will explain in a moment.
The geometry of the orbit of
+1
under
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
, for one return of the dynamics, is explained in part (iv) of Theorem
A
.
This form of geometry is ubiquitous for maps with an irrationally indifferent fixed point; it requires a non-zero second derivative at
0
.
For instance, it holds for the quadratic polynomials
e^{2\pi i\alpha}z+z^{2}
.

By employing the renormalisation scheme
(\mathcal{R}_{m},\mathbb{F})
, we build a topological model for the dynamics
near an irrationally indifferent fixed point, as we explain below.

By the classic work of Fatou
[
Fat19
]
and Mane
[
Mañ87
]
, when
f
is a polynomial or a rational function of
the Riemann sphere, there is a recurrent critical point of
f
which “interacts” with the fixed point at
0
.
The topological boundary of
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
,

\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\partial\mathbb{M}_{\alpha},

is equal to the closure of the orbit of
+1
for the iterates of
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
.
The set
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is a topological model for the closure of the orbit of that recurrent critical point of
f
,
which is the measure theoretic attractor of
f
for the orbits that remain near
0
.

We explain the topology of the sets
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
in terms of the arithmetic nature of
\alpha
.

Theorem B

(Trichotomy of the maximal invariant set)
.

For every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
one of the following statements hold:

(i)

\alpha
is a Herman number and
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is a Jordan curve,

(ii)

\alpha
is a Brjuno but not a Herman number, and
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is a one-sided hairy Jordan curve,

(iii)

\alpha
is not a Brjuno number, and
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is a Cantor bouquet.

14,5)

Figure 2.
Computer simulations for the three topologies in Theorem
B
; from left to right, a Jordan curve, a hairy Jordan curve, and a Cantor bouquet.

The arithmetic class of Brjuno was discovered by Siegel-Brjuno
[
Sie42
,
Brj71
]
in their now classic work on the study of the linearisation problem for
irrationally indifferent fixed points.
It is the set of
\alpha
such that the denominators
q_{n}
of the best rational approximants of
\alpha
satisfy

\textstyle{\sum}_{n=1}^{\infty}q_{n}^{-1}\log q_{n+1}<+\infty.

The arithmetic class of Herman was discovered by Herman-Yoccoz
[
Her79
,
Yoc02
]

in their landmark study of the analytic linearisation problem for analytic diffeomorphisms of the circle.
The set of Herman numbers is more complicated to characterise in terms of the arithmetic of
\alpha
,
see Section
6
. It forms an
F_{\sigma,\delta}
subset of
{\mathbb{R}}
.
However, any Herman number is a Brjuno number, the set of Herman numbers has full measure in
{\mathbb{R}}
,
while the set of non-Brjuno numbers is topologically generic in
{\mathbb{R}}
.
The arguments presented in this paper do not rely on the optimality properties of the Brjuno and Herman
numbers for the linearisation problems.
These arithmetic conditions naturally emerge in the study of the model
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
.

A Cantor bouquet is a compact subset of
\mathbb{C}
which has empty interior, consists of a collection of Jordan arcs only meeting at
0
,
and every arc is accumulated from both sides by arcs.
A (one-sided) hairy Jordan curve is a similar object consisting of a collection of Jordan arcs all attached to (one side of) a Jordan arc.
See Section
8.1
for the precise definitions of these objects.
While one-sided hairy Jordan curves and Cantor bouquets are universal topological objects,
our construction is geometric, featuring delicate metric properties.
That is, the models for dissimilar values of non-Brjuno numbers, and also for dissimilar values of Brjuno but non-Herman numbers,
have very distinct metric properties.

The maps
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
exhibit peculiar dynamical behaviour, especially
when
\alpha
is not a Herman number.
Every point in
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is topologically recurrent.
There are points in
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
with dense orbits.
There are uncountably many distinct closed fully invariant sets.
In contrast to the linearisable examples where an invariant set consists of disjoint unions of closed analytic curves, here there is only a
one-parameter family of closed invariant sets.
The set of accumulation points of the orbit of a point
z\in\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
, denoted by
\omega(z)
, is a notable example of a closed invariant set.
It turns out that these are the only ones, as we state below.

Define
r_{\alpha}\in[0,1]
according to

[r_{\alpha},1]=\{z\in\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\mid\operatorname{Im}z=0,\operatorname{Re}z\geq 0\}.

Theorem C

(Topological dynamics)
.

For every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
the map

\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
satisfies the following properties:

(i)

the map
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is topologically recurrent.

(ii)

the map

\omega:[r_{\alpha},1]\to\{X\subseteq\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\mid X\text{ is non-empty, closed and invariant}\}

is a homeomorphism with respect to the Hausdorff metric on the range.

(iii)

the map
\omega
on
[r_{\alpha},1]
is strictly increasing with respect to
the inclusion.

(iv)

if
\alpha
is a Brjuno number, for every
t\in(r_{\alpha},1]
,
\omega(t)
is a hairy Jordan curve.

(v)

if
\alpha
is not a Brjuno number, for every
t\in(r_{\alpha},1]
,
\omega(t)
is a Cantor bouquet.

Based on the above results for the model, we propose the following conjecture.

Conjecture

(Trichotomy of the irrationally indifferent attractors)
.

Let
f
be a rational function of the Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
of degree at least
2
, with

f(0)=0
,
f^{\prime}(0)=e^{2\pi i\alpha}
, and
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
.
There exists a critical point of
f
, such that the closure of its orbit, denoted by
\Lambda(f)
, satisfies the following:

(i)

if
\alpha
is a Herman number,
\Lambda(f)
is a Jordan curve,

(ii)

if
\alpha
is a Brjuno but not a Herman number,
\Lambda(f)
is a one-sided hairy Jordan curve,

(iii)

if
\alpha
is not a Brjuno number,
\Lambda(f)
is a Cantor bouquet.

Moreover, in cases (i) and (ii),
f
preserves the connected component of
\hat{{\mathbb{C}}}\setminus\Lambda(f)

containing
0
, and in case (iii),
\Lambda(f)
contains
0
.

The above conjecture immediately implies a number of important conjectures in the study of irrationally indifferent
fixed points for rational functions.
Notably, it implies the optimality of the Brjuno condition for the linearisability of irrationally indifferent fixed
points (Douady-Yoccoz conjecture 1986), the optimality of the Herman condition for the presence of a critical
point on the boundary of the Siegel disk (Herman conjecture 1985),
the Siegel disk are Jordan domains (Douady-Sullivan conjecture 1987).
On the other hand, for rational functions, the invariant Siegel-compacta and hedgehogs introduced by
Perez-Marco
[
PM97
]
are contained in the post-critical set, and hence their topology may be
completely explained by the above conjecture.

In a counterpart paper
[
Che17
]
, we employ the topological model in Theorem
A
,
and the near-parabolic renormalisation scheme of Inou and Shishikura
[
IS06
]
,
to prove the above conjecture for a class of maps and rotation numbers.
Indeed, we present some sufficient condition for a renormalisation scheme in order to conclude the above
conjecture.
In order to explain this relation, we briefly outline the construction of the model.
Here we take an upside-down approach to the renormalisation.
In contrast to defining renormalisation for a given map defined on a domain, we start by building
the changes of coordinates for the renormalisation, one for each rotation number.
Repeatedly applying the Gauss map to a fixed
\alpha
, one obtains a sequence of rotation numbers,
and the corresponding changes of coordinates.
Then, there is a maximal set on which the infinite chain of the changes of coordinates is defined.
Finally we build the map
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
on the maximal set so that its return map via the change of
coordinate becomes
\mathbb{T}_{G(\alpha)}
.
The advantage of this approach is that any given renormalisation scheme for irrationally indifferent fixed
points may be compared to the model
(\mathcal{R}_{m},\mathbb{F})
by comparing the corresponding
changes of coordinates.
This provides a streamlined approach to the study of the dynamics of irrationally indifferent fixed points.
It is also unified, in the sense that one does not need to reconsider the role of the arithmetic properties in
a given renormalisation scheme.
In the counterpart paper
[
Che17
]
, we show that if a renormalisation scheme consists of a change of
coordinate which is sufficiently close to the change of coordinate for the renormalisation model,
then the corresponding maps are topologically conjugate on the corresponding maximal domain of
renormalisations.
See also
[
SY18
]
for partial progress towards the above conjecture.

An alternative construction for
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
was suggested by Buff and Chéritat in 2009
[
BC09
]
.
Our thought in this direction was influenced and motivated by their construction based on employing
toy models for the changes of coordinates.
In contrast to the conformal changes of coordinates considered by those authors, the models for the changes
of coordinates presented here are not conformal, but preserve straight rays landing at
0
, while maintaining
the correct nonlinear behaviour in the radial directions.
This flexibility allows us to incorporate some remarkable functional relations, which in turn allow us to avoid
taking Hausdorff limits in the construction.
The explicit construction presented here allow us to promote the models for
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
to build
the maps
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
, and a renormalisation scheme on those maps.

2.
The changes of coordinates for the renormalisation
\mathcal{R}_{m}

In this section we introduce a one-parameter (
r\in(0,1/2]
) family of real analytic diffeomorphisms.
When we set the parameter as a rotation number, the diffeomorphism becomes the change of coordinate for the
renormalisation of a map with that asymptotic rotation number at
0
.
These are the toy models for the change of coordinates in the toy model for the renormalisation scheme.

2.1.
Explicit formula for the changes of coordinates

Consider the set

{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}=\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid\operatorname{Im}w>-1\}.

For
r\in(0,1/2]
, we define the map
Y_{r}:\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}\to{\mathbb{C}}

as
1
1

1

\overline{X}
denotes the topological closure of a given set
X
.

Y_{r}(w)=r\operatorname{Re}w+\frac{i}{2\pi}\log\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi riw}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\Big|.

Evidently,
Y_{r}
maps a vertical line in
{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}
to a vertical line.
Also, one can see that
Y_{r}(0)=0
and that
Y_{r}(1/(2r)-i)
is uniformly close to
1/2+i(\log 1/r)/(2\pi)
.
Figure
3
shows the behaviour of
Y_{r}
on horizontal and vertical lines.

Figure 3.
The nearly horizontal curves are the images of the horizontal lines
y=-1,0,1,\dots,8

under
Y_{r}
.
The vertical lines, from left to right, are the images of the vertical lines
\operatorname{Re}w=-1
,
\operatorname{Re}w=0
,
and
\operatorname{Re}w=1/\alpha
, under
Y_{r}
. Here,
r=1/(10+1/(1+1/(1+1/(1+\dots))))
.

Lemma 2.1
.

For every
r\in(0,1/2]
,
Y_{r}:\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}\to{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}
is well-defined, real-analytic in
\operatorname{Re}w
and
\operatorname{Im}w
, and is
injective.
Moreover, for every
r\in(0,1/2)
and every
y\geq 0
, we have

H_{r}\left(\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid\operatorname{Im}w\geq y-1\}\right)\subset\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid\operatorname{Im}w\geq\operatorname{Im}H_{r}(iy)-1\}.

Proof.

The proof is based on some elementary calculations. Note that

10\leq 1+(12/5)+(12/5)^{2}/2!+\dots+(12/5)^{5}/5!\leq e^{12/5}.

Using
0\leq r\leq 1/2
,
3\leq\pi\leq 4
, and the above inequality, we obtain

\log(4+3\pi r)+2\pi r\leq\log(e^{12/5})+\pi\leq\log e^{4\pi/5}+\pi\leq 9\pi/5.

This implies that for all
r\in[0,1/2]
,
e^{9\pi/5}\geq(4+3\pi r)e^{2\pi r}
, and therefore,

\pi e^{\pi r}(e^{9\pi/5}-(4+3\pi r)e^{2\pi r}))\geq 0.

Fix
r\in[0,1/2]
. Integrating the above inequality from
0
to
r
, we conclude that

e^{9\pi/5}(e^{\pi r}-1)-(\pi r+1)e^{3\pi r}+1\geq 0.

This implies that for all
r\in(0,1/2]
, we have

(2.1)

\frac{e^{\pi r}-1}{(\pi r+1)e^{3\pi r}-1}\geq e^{-9\pi/5}.

On the other hand, for
w\in\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}
, by the triangle inequality,

|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi riw}|\geq|e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi riw}|-|e^{-3\pi r}|\geq e^{-2\pi r}-e^{-3\pi r},

and

|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}|\leq|e^{-3\pi r}-1|+|1-e^{\pi ri}|\leq(1-e^{-3\pi r})+\pi r.

Combining the above two inequalities with Equation (
2.1
) we obtain

\left|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi riw}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\right|\geq\frac{e^{-2\pi r}-e^{-3\pi r}}{\pi r+1-e^{-3\pi r}}=\frac{e^{\pi r}-1}{(\pi r+1)e^{3\pi r}-1}\geq e^{-9\pi/5}.

The above inequality implies that for all
w\in{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}
we have

\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(w)\geq\frac{1}{2\pi}\log e^{-9\pi/5}>-1.

In particular,
Y_{r}
is well-defined, and maps
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}
into
{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}
. This also implies that

Y_{r}
is real-analytic in
\operatorname{Re}w
and
\operatorname{Im}w
, for
w
in
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}
.

To prove that
Y_{r}
is injective, assume that
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
are two distinct points in
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}
.
If
\operatorname{Re}w_{1}\neq\operatorname{Re}w_{2}
, then
\operatorname{Re}Y_{r}(w_{1})\neq\operatorname{Re}Y_{r}(w_{2})
.
If
\operatorname{Re}w_{1}=\operatorname{Re}w_{2}
but
\operatorname{Im}w_{1}\neq\operatorname{Im}w_{2}
, then

|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi riw_{1}}|\neq|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi riw_{2}}|.

This implies that
\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(w_{1})\neq\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(w_{2})
.

One may employ similar elementary calculations to derive the latter part of the lemma.
∎

2.2.
Uniform contraction of the changes of coordinates

A key property of the maps
Y_{r}
is stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2
.

For every
r\in(0,1/2]
, and every
w_{1},w_{2}
in
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}
, we have

|Y_{r}(w_{1})-Y_{r}(w_{2})|\leq 0.9|w_{1}-w_{2}|.

The precise contraction factor
0.9
in the above lemma is not crucial, any constant strictly less than
1
suffices.

Proof.

Let
g(w)=(e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi riw})(e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}e^{2\pi ri\overline{w}})
.
Then,
g(w)
is of the form
\zeta\overline{\zeta}
, for some
\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}
, and hence it produces positive real values for

w\in\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}
.
We have

\partial g(w)/\partial w=2\pi rie^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi riw}(e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}e^{2\pi ri\overline{w}}),

and

\partial g(w)/\partial\overline{w}=-2\pi rie^{\pi ri}e^{2\pi ri\overline{w}}(e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi riw}).

Therefore, by the complex chain rule,

\frac{\partial}{\partial w}\big(\log g(w)\big)=\frac{1}{g(w)}\frac{\partial g}{\partial w}=\frac{2\pi rie^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi riw}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi riw}}=\frac{2\pi ri}{e^{-3\pi r}e^{\pi ri}e^{2\pi riw}-1},

and

\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{w}}\big(\log g(w)\big)=\frac{1}{g(w)}\frac{\partial g}{\partial\overline{w}}=\frac{-2\pi rie^{\pi ri}e^{2\pi ri\overline{w}}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}e^{2\pi ri\overline{w}}}=\frac{-2\pi ri}{e^{-3\pi r}e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi ri\overline{w}}-1}.

We rewrite
Y_{r}
in the following form

Y_{r}(w)=r\cdot\frac{w+\overline{w}}{2}+\frac{i}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\log g(w)-\frac{i}{2\pi}\log|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}|.

Then, by the above calculations,

\frac{\partial Y_{r}}{\partial w}(w)=\frac{r}{2}+\frac{i}{4\pi}\cdot\frac{2\pi ri}{e^{-3\pi r}e^{\pi ri}e^{2\pi riw}-1}=\frac{r}{2}\Big(1-\frac{1}{e^{-3\pi r}e^{\pi ri}e^{2\pi riw}-1}\Big),

and

\frac{\partial Y_{r}}{\partial\overline{w}}(w)=\frac{r}{2}+\frac{i}{4\pi}\cdot\frac{-2\pi ri}{e^{-3\pi r}e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi ri\overline{w}}-1}=\frac{r}{2}\Big(1+\frac{1}{e^{-3\pi r}e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi ri\overline{w}}-1}\Big).

Let
\xi=e^{-3\pi r}e^{\pi ri}e^{2\pi riw}
. For
w\in\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}
,
|\xi|\leq e^{-\pi r}
.
For the maximum size of the directional derivative of
Y_{r}
we have

\displaystyle\max_{\theta\in[0,2\pi]}\big|\operatorname{D}Y_{r}(w)\cdot e^{i\theta}\big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{\partial Y_{r}}{\partial w}(w)\Big|+\Big|\frac{\partial Y_{r}}{\partial\overline{w}}(w)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{r}{2}\cdot\Big|1-\frac{1}{\xi-1}\Big|+\frac{r}{2}\cdot\Big|1+\frac{1}{\overline{\xi}-1}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{r}{2}\cdot\frac{2+e^{-\pi r}}{1-e^{-\pi r}}+\frac{r}{2}\cdot\frac{e^{-\pi r}}{1-e^{-\pi r}}=r\cdot\frac{e^{\pi r}+1}{e^{\pi r}-1}.

For
r\geq 0
,
e^{\pi r}-1\geq\pi r+\pi^{2}r^{2}/2
, (the first two terms of the Taylor series with positive terms).
This gives us

r\cdot\frac{e^{\pi r}+1}{e^{\pi r}-1}=r\left(1+\frac{2}{e^{\pi r}-1}\right)\leq r\left(1+\frac{2}{\pi r+\pi^{2}r^{2}/2}\right)=\frac{2\pi r+\pi^{2}r^{2}+4}{2\pi+\pi^{2}r}.

The last function in the above equation is increasing on
(0,1/2)
,
because it has a non-negative derivative
(4\pi r+\pi^{2}r^{2})/(2+\pi r)^{2}
.
Then, it is bounded by its value at
1/2
, which, using
\pi\geq 3
, gives us

\frac{2\pi r+\pi^{2}r^{2}+4}{2\pi+\pi^{2}r}\leq\frac{\pi+\pi^{2}/4+4}{2\pi+\pi^{2}/2}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{4}{2\pi+\pi^{2}/2}\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{4}{6+4}=\frac{9}{10}.\qed

2.3.
Remarkable functional relations for the changes of coordinates

The maps
Y_{r}
satisfy two crucial functional relations, one at a large scale, and one at a small scale,
both of which are illustrated in Figure
3
. We present these properties in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.3
.

For every
r\in(0,1/2]
, we have

(i)

for every
w\in\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}
,

Y_{r}(w+1/r)=Y_{r}(w)+1

(ii)

for every
t\geq-1
,

Y_{r}(it+1/r-1)=Y_{r}(it)+1-r.

Proof.

Part (i) of the lemma readily follows from the formula defining
Y_{r}
.

To prove part (ii) of the lemma, first note that

\displaystyle\big|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi ri(it+1/r-1)}\big|

\displaystyle=\big|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{2\pi rt}e^{2\pi ri}\big|

\displaystyle=\big|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}e^{2\pi rt}\big|=\big|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{2\pi rt}\big|.

Above, the first and second “
=
” are simple multiplications of complex numbers, while for the third “
=
” we have
used that
|x-z|=|x-\overline{z}|
, for real numbers
x
and complex numbers
z
. Thus,

Y_{r}(it+1/r-1)=r(1/r-1)+\frac{i}{2\pi}\log\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{2\pi rt}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\Big|=(1-r)+Y_{r}(it).\qed

3.
The sets
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}

In this section we build the sets
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
introduced in Section
1
.
These sets are defined by successively applying the changes of coordinates
Y_{r}
, for a sequence of parameters
r
.

3.1.
Successive rotation numbers
\alpha_{n}
and signs
\varepsilon_{n}

For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, define
d(x,{\mathbb{Z}})=\min_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}|x-k|
. Let us fix an irrational number
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Define the numbers
\alpha_{n}\in(0,1/2)
, for
n\geq 0
, according to

(3.1)

\alpha_{0}=d(\alpha,{\mathbb{Z}}),\quad\alpha_{n+1}=d(1/\alpha_{n},{\mathbb{Z}}).

Then, there are unique integers
a_{n}
, for
n\geq-1
, and
\varepsilon_{n}\in\{+1,-1\}
, for
n\geq 0
, such that

(3.2)

\alpha=a_{-1}+\varepsilon_{0}\alpha_{0},\quad 1/\alpha_{n}=a_{n}+\varepsilon_{n+1}\alpha_{n+1}.

Evidently, for all
n\geq 0
,

(3.3)

1/\alpha_{n}\in(a_{n}-1/2,a_{n}+1/2),\quad a_{n}\geq 2,

and

(3.4)

\varepsilon_{n+1}=\begin{cases}+1&\text{if }1/\alpha_{n}\in(a_{n},a_{n}+1/2),\\
-1&\text{if }1/\alpha_{n}\in(a_{n}-1/2,a_{n}).\end{cases}

In order to streamline our notations in this paper, we let
\alpha_{-1}=+1
.
See Section
6
, in particular Section
6.2
, for more details about
this notion of continued fraction algorithm (known as nearest integer continued fraction).

3.2.
Successive changes of coordinates

Recall that
s(w)=\overline{w}
denotes the complex conjugation map.
For
n\geq 0
we define

(3.5)

\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w)=\begin{cases}Y_{\alpha_{n}}(w)&\text{if }\varepsilon_{n}=-1\\
-s\circ Y_{\alpha_{n}}(w)&\text{if }\varepsilon_{n}=+1.\end{cases}

Each
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
is either orientation preserving or reversing, depending on the sing of
\varepsilon_{n}
.
For
n\geq 0
, we have
2
2

2

For a given set
X\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
, we define
iX=\{ix\mid x\in X\}
.

(3.6)

\mathbb{Y}_{n}(i[-1,+\infty))\subset i(-1,+\infty),\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbb{Y}_{n}(0)=0.

Lemma
2.2
implies that for all
n\geq 0
and all
w_{1},w_{2}
in
\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}
, we have

(3.7)

|\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w_{1})-\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w_{2})|\leq 0.9|w_{1}-w_{2}|.

It follows from Lemma
2.3
that for all
n\geq 0
and all
w\in\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}
,

(3.8)

\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w+1/\alpha_{n})=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w)+1&\text{if }\varepsilon_{n}=-1,\\
\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w)-1&\text{if }\varepsilon_{n}=+1.\end{cases}

Also, by the same lemma, for all
n\geq 0
, and all
t\geq-1
,

(3.9)

\mathbb{Y}_{n}(it+1/\alpha_{n}-1)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(it)+(1-\alpha_{n})&\text{if }\varepsilon_{n}=-1,\\
\mathbb{Y}_{n}(it)+(\alpha_{n}-1)&\text{if }\varepsilon_{n}=+1.\end{cases}

3.3.
Equivariant tiling of the tower

For
n\geq 0
let

(3.10)

\begin{gathered}I_{n}^{0}=\{w\in\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\},\\
J_{n}^{0}=\{w\in I_{n}^{0}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[1/\alpha_{n}-1,1/\alpha_{n}]\},\\
K_{n}^{0}=\{w\in I_{n}^{0}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}-1]\}.\end{gathered}

We inductively defined the sets
I_{n}^{j}
,
J_{n}^{j}
, and
K_{n}^{j}
, for
j\geq 1
and
n\geq 0
.
Assume that for some
j
and all
n\geq 0
,
I_{n}^{j}
,
J_{n}^{j}
and
K_{n}^{j}
are defined.
We define
I_{n}^{j+1}
,
J_{n}^{j+1}
and
K_{n}^{j+1}
for
n\geq 0
as follows.
Fix an arbitrary
n\geq 0
.
If
\varepsilon_{n+1}=-1
, let

(3.11)

I_{n}^{j+1}=\bigcup_{l=0}^{a_{n}-2}\big(\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(I_{n+1}^{j})+l\big)\bigcup\big(\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(K_{n+1}^{j})+a_{n}-1\big).

If
\varepsilon_{n+1}=+1
, let

(3.12)

I_{n}^{j+1}=\bigcup_{l=1}^{a_{n}}\big(\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(I_{n+1}^{j})+l\big)\bigcup\big(\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(J_{n+1}^{j})+a_{n}+1\big).

Regardless of the sign of
\varepsilon_{n+1}
, define

J_{n}^{j+1}=\{w\in I_{n}^{j+1}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[1/\alpha_{n}-1,1/\alpha_{n}]\},

K_{n}^{j+1}=\{w\in I_{n}^{j+1}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}-1]\}.

Figure
4
presents two generations of these domains.
We summarise the basic features of these sets in the following lemma.

14,11)

Figure 4.
The left hand picture is for
\varepsilon_{n}=-1
and the right hand picture is for
\varepsilon_{n}=+1
.
The sets
K_{n}^{0}
and
J_{n}^{0}
are on the lower row, and the set
I_{n-1}^{1}
is on the upper row.

Lemma 3.1
.

For all
n\geq 0
and
j\geq 0
, the sets
I_{n}^{j}
,
J_{n}^{j}
and
K_{n}^{j}
are closed and connected subsets of

{\mathbb{C}}
, and each of
\partial I_{n}^{j}
,
\partial J_{n}^{j}
and
\partial K_{n}^{j}
is a piece-wise analytic curve.
Moreover,

(i)

\{\operatorname{Re}w\mid w\in I_{n}^{j}\}=[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
;

(ii)

if
\varepsilon_{n+1}=-1
, we have

(3.13)

\begin{gathered}\{w\in I_{n}^{j}\mid\operatorname{Re}w=0\}\subseteq\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(i[-1,\infty)),\\
\{w\in I_{n}^{j}\mid\operatorname{Re}w=1/\alpha_{n}\}\subseteq\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(i[-1,\infty)+1/\alpha_{n+1}-1)+a_{n}-1;\end{gathered}

(iii)

if
\varepsilon_{n+1}=+1
, we have

(3.14)

\begin{gathered}\{w\in I_{n}^{j}\mid\operatorname{Re}w=0\}\subseteq\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(i[-1,\infty)+1/\alpha_{n})+1,\\
\{w\in I_{n}^{j}\mid\operatorname{Re}w=1/\alpha_{n}\}\subseteq\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(i[-1,\infty)+1/\alpha_{n+1}-1)+a_{n}+1.\end{gathered}

Proof.

The proof is elementary and is left to the reader. One only needs to follow the basic arithmetic relations among

\alpha_{n}
,
a_{n}
and
\varepsilon_{n+1}
in (
3.1
)-(
3.3
),
and use the functional relations in (
3.8
) and (
3.9
).
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

For every
n\geq 0
and
j\geq 0
, the following hold:

(i)

for all
w\in\overline{\mathbb{H}^{\prime}}
, we have
w\in I_{n}^{j}
if and only if
w+1\in I_{n}^{j}
;

(ii)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
it\in I_{n}^{j}
if and only if
it+1/\alpha_{n}\in I_{n}^{j}
.

Proof.

We shall prove both parts at once by an inductive argument on
j
.
Clearly, both statements hold for
j=0
and all
n\geq 0
.
Assume that both parts of the lemma hold for some
j\geq 0
and all
n\geq 0
.

Part (ii) for
j
and Equation (
3.8
) together imply that for real values of
t
,
it\in I_{n}^{j+1}
if and only
if
it+1\in I_{n}^{j+1}
. Then, by the definition of
I_{n}^{j+1}
in (
3.11
) and (
3.12
),
one concludes part (i) for
j+1
and all
n\geq 0
.

To prove part (ii) of the lemma for
j+1
and all
n\geq 0
we need to consider two cases.
First assume that
\varepsilon_{n+1}=-1
. By (
3.9
) and (
3.2
),
for
t
and
t^{\prime}
in
[-1,+\infty)
satisfying
\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(it^{\prime})=it
, we have

\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(it^{\prime}+1/\alpha_{n+1}-1)+a_{n}-1=\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(it^{\prime})+(1-\alpha_{n+1})+a_{n}-1=it+1/\alpha_{n}.

If
it\in I_{n}^{j+1}
, then by Equation (
3.11
), there is
it^{\prime}\in I_{n+1}^{j}
with
\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(it^{\prime})=it
.
By the induction hypotheses (part (i) and (ii) for
j
and all
n\geq 0
),
it^{\prime}+1/\alpha_{n+1}-1\in I_{n+1}^{j}
.
Then,
it^{\prime}+1/\alpha_{n+1}-1\in K_{n+1}^{j}
. Therefore, by the above equation and (
3.11
),

it+1/\alpha_{n}\in I_{n}^{j+1}
.

On the other hand, if
it+1/\alpha_{n}\in I_{n}^{j+1}
, then by (
3.11
) and the induction hypotheses,
there is
it^{\prime}\in I_{n+1}^{j}
such that
it^{\prime}+1/\alpha_{n+1}-1\in K_{n+1}^{j}
and

\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(it^{\prime}+1/\alpha_{n+1}-1)+(a_{n}-1)=it+1/\alpha_{n}.

Again, by (
3.9
) and (
3.2
), this implies that

it+1/\alpha_{n}=\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(it^{\prime})+1/\alpha_{n}
.
Hence,
it=\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(it^{\prime})
, which implies that
it\in I_{n}^{j+1}
.

The proof when
\varepsilon_{n+1}=+1
is similar, using (
3.12
) and (
3.9
).
∎

Recall that
\alpha_{-1}=+1
. Let
I_{-1}^{0}=\{w\in\overline{{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[0,1/\alpha_{-1}]\}
, and for
j\geq 1
,
consider the sets

(3.15)

I_{-1}^{j}=\mathbb{Y}_{0}(I_{0}^{j-1})+(\varepsilon_{0}+1)/2.

By Lemma
2.1
,
I_{n}^{1}\subset I_{n}^{0}
, for
n\geq-1
. By an inductive argument, this implies that for all

n\geq-1
and all
j\geq 0
,

(3.16)

I_{n}^{j+1}\subset I_{n}^{j}.

3.4.
The sets
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}

For
n\geq-1
, we define

I_{n}=\cap_{j\geq 1}I_{n}^{j}.

Each
I_{n}
consists of closed half-infinite vertical lines with unbounded imaginary parts.
However,
I_{n}
may or may not be connected.
We note that
\operatorname{Re}I_{-1}\subset[0,1]
.
Indeed, by Lemma
3.2
, for real
t
,
it\in I_{-1}
if and only if
(it+1)\in I_{-1}
.
Thus, we may define

(3.17)

\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}=\{s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in I_{-1}\}\cup\{0\}.

Remark 3.3
.

There may be alternative (simpler) approaches to build the set
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
using the maps
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
.
For the sake of applications, here we have selected an approach which closely mimics a construction
in the renormalisation scheme.
However, it is worth noting that one cannot simply define the domains
I_{n}^{j}
, and subsequently

\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
, by first extending the maps
\mathbb{Y}_{n}

1/\alpha_{n}
-periodically onto
{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}
,
and then iterating them on
{\mathbb{H}}^{\prime}
.
That is because, such a construction would lead to a set in the limit which is periodic under translations
by
+1
and some irrational number.
It would follow that the interior of that set must be the region above a horizontal line, which cannot be the case for
arbitrary
\alpha
.

Proposition 3.4
.

For every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
we have the following:
3
3

3

A set
M\subseteq\mathbb{C}
is called
star-like about

0
if for every
z\in M
and every
r\in[0,1]
,
rz\in M
.

(i)

\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
is a compact set which is star-like about
0
,
+1\in\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
,
and
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\cap(1,\infty)=\emptyset
;

(ii)

\mathbb{M}_{\alpha+1}=\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
and
s(\mathbb{M}_{\alpha})=\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha}
.

Proof.

Since every map
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
sends vertical lines to vertical lines, each of
I_{n}^{j}
is the region above the
graph of a function.
This implies that for every
n\geq-1
,
I_{n}
consists of some half-infinite vertical lines.
Thus,
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
is star-like about
0
.
Also,
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
is bounded and closed, hence a compact set.
On the other hand, for all
n\geq-1
,
0\in I_{n}
, which implies that
+1\in\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
.
Indeed, by the uniform contraction of the maps
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
, Equation (
3.7
),
+1
is an
end point of a ray in
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
, that is, for every
\delta>0
,
1+\delta\notin\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
.

Recall that in order to define
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
, which only depends on
\alpha
,
we first define the sequences
(\alpha_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}
, and
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 0}
.
These fully determine the sequence of maps
(\mathbb{Y}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
, and hence the set
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
.
The irrational numbers
\alpha
and
\alpha+1
produce the same sequences
(\alpha_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}

and
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 0}
.
Therefore, the sequence of the maps
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
are the same for both
\alpha
and
\alpha+1
.
This implies that
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha+1}=\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
.

To determine
\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha}
, we need to compare the corresponding sequences
(\alpha_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,

(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 0}
for
\alpha
and
-\alpha
.
Let us denote the corresponding objects for
-\alpha
using the same notations as the ones for
\alpha
but with a prime,
that is,
\alpha_{n}^{\prime}
,
a_{n}^{\prime}
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{n}
,
\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}_{n}
,
I^{\prime}_{n}
,
K^{\prime}_{n}
,
J^{\prime}_{n}
, etc.
Using
\alpha=a_{-1}+\varepsilon_{0}\alpha_{0}
, we note that
a^{\prime}_{-1}=-a_{-1}
,
\alpha_{0}^{\prime}=\alpha_{0}
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{0}=-\varepsilon_{0}
.
These imply that
\mathbb{Y}_{0}^{\prime}=-s\circ\mathbb{Y}_{0}
.
However, since
\alpha_{0}^{\prime}=\alpha_{0}
, we conclude that for all
n\geq 1
we have
a^{\prime}_{n-1}=a_{n-1}
,

\alpha^{\prime}_{n}=\alpha_{n}
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{n}=\varepsilon_{n}
.
Thus, for all
n\geq 1
,
\mathbb{Y}_{n}^{\prime}=\mathbb{Y}_{n}
.
These imply that
I_{0}=I_{0}^{\prime}
.
In particular, in the definition of
\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha}
, the only difference with the definition of

\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
is that
\mathbb{Y}_{0}
changes to
-s\circ\mathbb{Y}_{0}
.
Therefore, using Equation (
3.15
),

\displaystyle I^{\prime}_{-1}=\mathbb{Y}_{0}^{\prime}(I^{\prime}_{0})+\frac{1+\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}}{2}

\displaystyle=-s\circ\mathbb{Y}_{0}(I_{0}^{\prime})+\frac{1-\epsilon_{0}}{2}

\displaystyle=-s\left(\mathbb{Y}_{0}(I_{0})+\frac{1+\epsilon_{0}}{2}\right)+1=-s(I_{-1})+1.

Comparing to Equation (
3.17
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha}

\displaystyle=\{s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in I^{\prime}_{-1}\}\cup\{0\}

\displaystyle=\{s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in-s(I_{-1})\}\cup\{0\}

\displaystyle=\{s\circ s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in I_{-1}\}\cup\{0\}

\displaystyle=s\left(\{s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in I_{-1}\}\right)\cup\{0\}=s(\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}).\qed

Lemma 3.5
.

For every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
we have the following:

(i)

if
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
,
\mathbb{M}_{1/\alpha}=\{s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in I_{0}\}\cup\{0\}
,

(ii)

if
\alpha\in(-1/2,0)
,
\mathbb{M}_{-1/\alpha}=\{s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in I_{0}\}\cup\{0\}
.

Proof.

For
\alpha
, let
(\alpha_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 0}
denote the sequences defined in
Section
3.1
.
Define
\alpha^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}
.
Let us use the notations
(\alpha^{\prime}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
(a^{\prime}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
(\varepsilon^{\prime}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
for the sequences

(\alpha_{n})_{n\geq 0}
,
(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 0}
corresponding to
\alpha^{\prime}
.
We have
\alpha^{\prime}_{0}=\alpha_{1}
, and then

\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}=\alpha^{\prime}=a^{\prime}_{-1}+\varepsilon^{\prime}_{0}\alpha^{\prime}_{0}=a^{\prime}_{-1}+\varepsilon^{\prime}_{0}\alpha_{1}.

As
\alpha^{\prime}\in(-1/2,1/2)
, we must have
a^{\prime}_{-1}=0
, and hence
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{0}=\varepsilon_{1}
.
To determine
\mathbb{Y}_{0}^{\prime}
, we consider two cases.
If
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{0}=\varepsilon_{1}=-1
, we have
\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}_{0}=Y_{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}}=\mathbb{Y}_{\alpha_{1}}=\mathbb{Y}_{1}
,
and if
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{0}=\varepsilon_{1}=+1
, we have
\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}_{0}=-s\circ Y_{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}}=-s\circ Y_{\alpha_{1}}=\mathbb{Y}_{1}
.

The relation
\alpha^{\prime}_{0}=\alpha_{1}
implies that for all
n\geq 1
we have
\alpha^{\prime}_{n}=\alpha_{n+1}
,
a^{\prime}_{n}=a_{n+1}
and

\varepsilon^{\prime}_{n}=\varepsilon_{n+1}
.
Hence, for all
n\geq 1
,
\mathbb{Y}^{\prime}_{n}=\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}
.
These imply that
I^{\prime}_{-1}=I_{0}
.
Therefore, according to Equation (
3.17
), we must have

(3.18)

\mathbb{M}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}=\mathbb{M}_{\alpha^{\prime}}=\{s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in I^{\prime}_{-1}\}\cup\{0\}=\{s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in I_{0}\}\cup\{0\}.

If
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
, we have
1/\alpha=1/\alpha_{0}=a_{-1}+\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}
, which using Lemma
3.4
-(ii),
we obtain

\mathbb{M}_{1/\alpha}=\mathbb{M}_{a_{-1}+\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}=\mathbb{M}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}.

If
\alpha\in(-1/2,0)
, we have
-1/\alpha=1/\alpha_{0}=a_{-1}+\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}
, and hence, using Lemma
3.4
-(ii),
we get

\mathbb{M}_{-1/\alpha}=\mathbb{M}_{a_{-1}+\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}=\mathbb{M}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}.

Combining the above equations, we obtain the desired properties in parts (i) and (ii).
∎

4.
The map
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}

In this section we define the map

(4.1)

\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha},

where
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
is the topological model defined in Section
3
.

The topological description of
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
, which is presented in Section
8
, does not employ
the map
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
in any ways.
However, the topological description can be used to identify the map
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
as follows.
When
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is a Jordan curve, it is the graph of a function of the argument.
Thus, there is a unique homeomorphism of
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
which acts as rotation
by
2\pi\alpha
in the tangential direction.
Similarly, when,
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is a hairy Jordan curve, there is a unique homeomorphism
of the base Jordan curve which acts as rotation by
2\pi\alpha
.
This map can be extended onto the end points of the Jordan arcs attached to the Jordan curve by matching the
corresponding end points.
Since the set of end points of those arcs is dense in
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
, there may be a
unique homeomorphism of
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
which acts as rotation by
2\pi\alpha
on the
base Jordan curve.
However, it is not clear if this map continuously extents to the whole hairy Cantor set.
Similarly, there may be a unique homeomorphism of a Cantor bouquet
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}

which acts as rotation by
2\pi\alpha
in the tangential direction.
Here we take a different approach to build
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
.
We give a presentation which is aligned with the action of the map on the renormalisation tower; compare with

[
Che13
,
AC18
]
.
This helps us later when describing the dynamics of
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
,
and may also be employed to link the toy renormalisation scheme we build here to an actual renormalisation scheme.

4.1.
Definition of the lift of
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}

Let us fix
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
, and let
I_{n}
, for
n\geq-1
, be the sets in Section
3.3
.
Given
w_{-1}\in I_{-1}
, we inductively identify the integers
l_{i}
and then the points
w_{i+1}\in I_{i+1}
so that

0\leq\operatorname{Re}(w_{i}-l_{i})<1,\ \text{if }\varepsilon_{i+1}=-1;\quad-1<\operatorname{Re}(w_{i}-l_{i})\leq 0,\quad\text{if }\varepsilon_{i+1}=+1;

and

\mathbb{Y}_{i+1}(w_{i+1})+l_{i}=w_{i}.

It follows that for all
n\geq 0
, we have

(4.2)

w_{-1}=(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+l_{-1})\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{1}+l_{0})\circ\dots\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{n}+l_{n-1})(w_{n}).

Also, by the definition of
I_{i}
in (
3.11
) and (
3.12
), for all
i\geq 0
,

(4.3)

0\leq l_{i}\leq a_{i}+\varepsilon_{i+1},\quad\text{ and }\quad 0\leq\operatorname{Re}w_{i}<1/\alpha_{i}.

We refer to the sequence
(w_{i};l_{i})_{i\geq-1}
as the
trajectory
of
w_{-1}
, with respect to
\alpha
, or simply, as
the trajectory of
w_{-1}
, when it is clear from the context what irrational number is used.

We define the map

(4.4)

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:I_{-1}\to I_{-1},

as follows.
Let
w_{-1}
be an arbitrary point in
I_{-1}
, and let
(w_{i};l_{i})_{i\geq-1}
denote the trajectory of
w_{-1}
.
Then,

(i)

if there is
n\geq 0
such that
w_{n}\in K_{n}
, and for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
,
w_{i}\in I_{i}\setminus K_{i}
, then

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w_{-1})=\left(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}+1}{2}\right)\circ\left(\mathbb{Y}_{1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}+1}{2}\right)\circ\cdots\circ\left(\mathbb{Y}_{n}+\frac{\varepsilon_{n}+1}{2}\right)(w_{n}+1);

(ii)

if for all
n\geq 0
,
w_{n}\in I_{n}\setminus K_{n}
, then

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w_{-1})=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\left(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}+1}{2}\right)\circ\left(\mathbb{Y}_{1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}+1}{2}\right)\circ\cdots\circ\left(\mathbb{Y}_{n}+\frac{\varepsilon_{n}+1}{2}\right)(w_{n}+1-1/\alpha_{n}).

It might not be clear that the limit in case (ii) exists. We look into this within the proof of Lemma
4.1
.

4.2.
The continuity

Lemma 4.1
.

For every
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
, the map
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:I_{-1}\to I_{-1}
induces a well-defined,
continuous and injective map
4
4

4

Here,
{\mathbb{Z}}
acts on
I_{-1}
by horizontal translations by integers.

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}\to I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}.

The main idea of the proof for the above statement is to partition the set
I_{-1}
into infinitely many pieces,
where the map is continuous on each piece. Then, we show that the maps on the pieces match at the boundary points.
Below we introduce the partition pieces.

For
w_{-1}\in I_{-1}
, let
(w_{i};l_{i})_{i\geq-1}
denote the trajectory of
w_{-1}
.
For each
n\geq 0
, let

W^{n}=\{w_{-1}\in I_{-1}\mid\text{ for all }0\leq i\leq n-1,w_{i}\in I_{i}\setminus K_{i},\text{ and }w_{n}\in K_{n}\},

V^{n}=\{w_{-1}\in I_{-1}\mid\text{ for all }0\leq i\leq n,w_{i}\in I_{i}\setminus K_{i}\}.

We set
V^{\infty}=\cap_{n\geq 0}V^{n}
.
Evidently, we have

(4.5)

I_{-1}=\cup_{n\geq 0}W^{n}\cup V^{\infty}.

It is also convenient to use some simplified notations for the compositions of the maps which appear in the definition
of
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
.
That is, for
m\geq n
, let
5
5

5

id denotes the identity map.

X_{n}^{n-1}=\textrm{id},\qquad X_{n}^{m}=\left(\mathbb{Y}_{n}+\frac{\varepsilon_{n}+1}{2}\right)\circ\left(\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{n+1}+1}{2}\right)\circ\cdots\circ\left(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+\frac{\varepsilon_{m}+1}{2}\right).

We break the proof of Lemma
4.1
into several lemmas.

Lemma 4.2
.

For all
n\geq 1
, the map
w_{-1}\mapsto w_{n}
is continuous and injective on the sets
W^{n}
and
V^{n}
.
In particular, for all
n\geq 1
,
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:W^{n}\to I_{-1}
is continuous and injective.

Proof.

For each
i\geq 0
, if
w_{i}\in I_{i}\setminus K_{i}
and
w_{i+1}\in K_{i+1}
, then
l_{i}=a_{i}+(\varepsilon_{i+1}-1)/2
.
Similarly, if
w_{i}\in I_{i}\setminus K_{i}
and
w_{i+1}\in I_{i+1}\setminus K_{i+1}
, then
l_{i}=a_{i}+(3\varepsilon_{i+1}-1)/2
.
These imply that for all
n\geq 1
and all
w_{-1}\in W^{n}
, the entries
(l_{i})_{i=-1}^{n-1}
in the trajectory of
w_{-1}

is independent of
w_{-1}
.
Similarly, for all
n\geq 1
the entries
(l_{i})_{i=-1}^{n-1}
in the trajectory of
w_{-1}\in V^{n}
is independent of
w_{-1}
.
In particular, the map
w_{-1}\mapsto w_{n}
is continuous and injective on each of
W^{n}
and
V^{n}
.

As each
\mathbb{Y}_{j}
is continuous and injective, we conclude that
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
is continuous and injective on
W^{n}
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

The map
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
is well-defined, continuous and injective on
V^{\infty}
.

Proof.

We know from Lemma
4.2
that for every
n\geq 0
, the map
w_{-1}\mapsto w_{n}
is continuous and
injective on
V^{n}
.
The image of this map covers
(1/\alpha_{n}-1,1/\alpha_{n})\cap I_{n}
, due to the choice we made in Equation (
4.3
).
Since the inverse map
w_{n}\mapsto w_{-1}
is also continuous and injective on
(1/\alpha_{n}-1,1/\alpha_{n})\cap I_{n}
, it follows that

V^{n}
is relatively open in
I_{-1}
. It is possible that for some values of
\alpha
, the nest
\cap_{n\geq 0}V^{n}
is empty
(for instance when
\varepsilon_{i}=-1
, for all
i\geq 0
).
Below we assume that
V^{\infty}
is not empty.

For each
n\geq 0
and
0\leq i\leq n
, we define the sets
V^{n}_{i}
as the set of
w_{i}
, for
w_{-1}\in V^{n}
.
Then, define
V^{\infty}_{i}=\cap_{n\geq i}V^{n}_{i}
. It follows that
w_{-1}\mapsto w_{i}
is continuous and injective
from
V^{\infty}
to
V^{\infty}_{i}
, for all
i\geq 0
.
Moreover, by the uniform contraction of the map
\mathbb{Y}_{j}
, each
V^{\infty}_{i}
is a closed half-infinite vertical line.

For
n\geq 0
and
m\geq n
, we define the map
E_{n}^{m}:V_{n}^{m}\to I_{n}
as follows

(4.6)

E_{n}^{m}(w_{n})=X_{n+1}^{m}(w_{m}+1-1/\alpha_{m}).

By the above paragraphs, this is a continuous and injective map on
V_{n}^{m}
.

Note that for
w_{n}\in I_{n}\setminus K_{n}
we have

|\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(w_{n+1})+(\varepsilon_{n+1}+1)/2-(w_{n}+1-1/\alpha_{n})|\leq 1.

This is because,
\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(w_{n+1})=\operatorname{Im}w_{n}
,
\operatorname{Re}w_{n}+1-1/\alpha_{n}\in[0,1]
, and

\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(w_{n+1})+(\varepsilon_{n+1}+1)/2
belongs to
[0,+1]
.

By Equation (
3.8
),
\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(w-1/\alpha_{n+1})=\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(w)+\varepsilon_{n+1}
, and
by Lemma
2.2
,

|\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(w+1)-\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(w)|\leq 0.9
.
Combining with the above inequality, we conclude that for all
w_{n}\in V_{n}^{n+1}
,

\displaystyle|E_{n}^{n+1}(w_{n})-E_{n}^{n}(w_{n})|

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(w_{n+1}+1-1/\alpha_{n+1})+(\varepsilon_{n+1}+1)/2-(w_{n}+1-1/\alpha_{n})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(w_{n+1})+\varepsilon_{n+1}+(\varepsilon_{n+1}+1)/2-(w_{n}+1-1/\alpha_{n})\right|+0.9

\displaystyle=|\varepsilon_{n+1}|+1+0.9\leq 3.

Therefore, using the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{j}
in Lemma
2.2
, we conclude that for all

m\geq n+2
, and all
w_{n}\in V^{m+1}_{n}
, we have

(4.7)

|E_{n}^{m+1}(w_{n})-E_{n}^{m}(w_{n})|=|X_{n+1}^{m}\circ E_{m}^{m+1}(w_{m})-X_{n+1}^{m}\circ E_{m}^{m}(w_{m})|\leq(0.9)^{m-n}\cdot 3.

In particular, for
w_{n}\in V_{n}^{\infty}
, the above inequality holds for all
m\geq n
, and hence
E_{n}^{m}
forms a
uniformly Cauchy sequence. Thus, the map

(4.8)

E_{n}=\lim_{m\to\infty}E_{n}^{m}:V^{\infty}_{n}\to I_{n}

is well-define, and continuous.
In particular, since
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w_{-1})=(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(\varepsilon_{0}+1)/2)\circ E_{0}(w_{0})
, we conclude that
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
is
continuous on
V^{\infty}
.

In order to show that
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
is injective, it is enough to show that
E_{0}
is injective.
To this end, we first note that for all
n\geq 0
and all
w_{n}\in V_{n}^{\infty}
, we have

(4.9)

|E_{n}(w_{n})-(w_{n}+1-1/\alpha_{n})|\leq\sum_{j=n}^{\infty}|E_{n}^{j+1}(w_{n})-E_{n}^{j}(w_{n})|\leq 3\sum_{j=n}^{\infty}0.9^{j-n}\leq 30.

Also, for
m\geq n+1
, we may rewrite Equation (
4.6
) as

(4.10)

E_{n}^{m}(w_{n})=(\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}+(\varepsilon_{n+1}+1)/2)\circ E_{n+1}^{m}(w_{n+1}),

and then take limits as
m\to\infty
to obtain

(4.11)

E_{n}(w_{n})=(\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}+(\varepsilon_{n+1}+1)/2)\circ E_{n+1}(w_{n+1}).

The above relation holds for all
n\geq 0
and all
w_{n+1}\in V_{n+1}^{\infty}
.

Let
w_{-1}
and
w^{\prime}_{-1}
be distinct elements in
V^{\infty}
. Let
(w_{i};l_{i})_{i\geq-1}
and
(w_{i}^{\prime};l_{i})_{i\geq-1}
denote the
trajectories of
w_{-1}
and
w^{\prime}_{-1}
, respectively.
Then, for each
n\geq 0
, both
w_{n}
and
w^{\prime}_{n}
belong to
V_{n}^{\infty}
.
By the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{j}
, there is
n\geq 0
such that
|w_{n}-w_{n}^{\prime}|\geq 61
.
By virtue of the uniform bound in Equation (
4.9
),
E_{n}(w_{n})\neq E_{n}(w_{n}^{\prime})
.
Inductive using (
4.11
), and the injectivity of
\mathbb{Y}_{j}
, we conclude that

E_{n}(w_{n})\neq E_{n}(w_{n}^{\prime})
, for all
n\geq 0
.
In particular,
E_{0}
is injective.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

For all
n\geq 1
,
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:\overline{W^{n}}\to I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}
is continuous.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary
n\geq 1
.
By definition,
W^{n}\subset V^{n-1}
.
Recall from the proof of Lemma
4.3
that the map
w_{-1}\mapsto w_{n-1}
is a homeomorphism from

V^{n-1}
onto
(1/\alpha_{n-1}-1,1/\alpha_{n-1})\cap I_{n-1}
. Thus,
V^{n-1}
is relatively open in
I_{-1}
.
Indeed, for
w_{-1}\in V^{n-1}
, the map
\operatorname{Re}w_{-1}\to\operatorname{Re}w_{n-1}
is a translation independent of
w_{-1}
.

We have

(4.12)

\displaystyle\{w_{n-1}\mid w_{-1}\in W^{n}\}=\{w\in I_{n-1}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[a_{n-1}-1,1/\alpha_{n-1})\},\quad\text{if }\varepsilon_{n}=-1;

\displaystyle\{w_{n-1}\mid w_{-1}\in W^{n}\}=\{w\in I_{n-1}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in(1/\alpha_{n-1}-1,a_{n-1}]\},\quad\text{if }\varepsilon_{n}=+1.

Combining with the above paragraph, we conclude that there are real numbers
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
such that
either
W^{n}=\{w\in I_{-1}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in(x_{n},y_{n}]\}
or
W^{n}=\{w\in I_{-1}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[x_{n},y_{n})\}
.

Let
(w^{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence in
W^{n}
converging to some
w\in\overline{W^{n}}
.
If
w\in W^{n}
, the continuity at
w
follows from Lemma
4.2
.
So, below we assume that
w\in\overline{W_{n}}\setminus W^{n}
.
Note that either
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}<\operatorname{Re}w
or
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}>\operatorname{Re}w
holds for all
i\geq 0
.

Let
(w_{j};l_{j})_{j\geq-1}
denote the trajectory of
w
, and for each
i\geq 0
, let
(w^{i}_{j},l^{i}_{j})_{j\geq-1}
denote
the trajectory of
w^{i}
. By (
4.12
), either
\varepsilon_{n}=+1
and

\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{n-1}\searrow 1/\alpha_{n-1}-1
, or
\varepsilon_{n}=-1
and
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{n-1}\nearrow 1/\alpha_{n-1}
.
6
6

6

We use the
notation
x_{i}\searrow x
to mean that the sequence
x_{i}
converges to
x
and
\operatorname{Re}x_{i}>x
. Similarly,
x_{i}\nearrow x

means that the sequence
x_{i}
converges to
x
and
\operatorname{Re}x_{i}<x
.

We consider two cases based on these scenarios.

(i)
\varepsilon_{n}=-1
and
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{n-1}\nearrow 1/\alpha_{n-1}
, as
i\to\infty
.
For integers
j
with
-1\leq j\leq n-3
,
\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{j}\notin\mathbb{Z}
.
That is because, if there is
j
with
0\leq j\leq n-3
and
\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
then we must have

\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{j+1}=1/\alpha_{j+1}
, then
\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{j+2}=1/\alpha_{j+2}-1
,
and then
\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{n-1}=1/\alpha_{n-1}-1
.
The last property is not possible in this case.
Similarly, if
\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{-1}\in\mathbb{Z}
then we must have

\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{0}=1/\alpha_{0}
, then
\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{1}=1/\alpha_{1}-1
,
and then
\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{n-1}=1/\alpha_{n-1}-1
.
The same contradiction.

By the previous paragraph,
l^{i}_{j}=l_{j}
, for all
-1\leq j\leq n-3
.
This implies that for
-1\leq j\leq n-2
,
\lim_{i\to\infty}w^{i}_{j}=w_{j}
.

The integer
n-1
is the smallest integer with
w_{n-1}\in K_{n-1}
.
To see this, first note that either
\varepsilon_{n-1}=-1
and
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{n-2}\nearrow a_{n-2}-1
, or
\varepsilon_{n-1}=+1
and

\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{n-2}\searrow a_{n-2}
.
Thus,
\operatorname{Re}w_{n-2}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Re}w_{n-2}^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Hence,
\operatorname{Re}w_{n-1}=0
.
On the other hand, for
0\leq j\leq n-3
,
\operatorname{Re}w_{j}\neq 1/\alpha_{j}-1
, since otherwise
\operatorname{Re}w_{n-1}=1/\alpha_{n-1}-1
.
With this paragraph, we conclude that

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w)=X_{0}^{n-1}(w_{n-1}+1).

Let
w_{n-1}^{\prime}=\lim_{i\to\infty}w^{i}_{n-1}
and
w_{n}^{\prime}=\lim_{i\to\infty}w^{i}_{n}
.
We must have
\operatorname{Re}w^{\prime}_{n-1}=1/\alpha_{n-1}
and
\operatorname{Re}w^{\prime}_{n}=1/\alpha_{n}-1
.
By Equation (
3.8
), we have
w^{\prime}_{n-1}=w_{n-1}+1/\alpha_{n-1}
, and then using Equation (
3.9
), we get

w^{\prime}_{n}+1=w_{n}+1/\alpha_{n}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w^{i})=\lim_{i\to\infty}X_{0}^{n}(w^{i}_{n}+1)=X_{0}^{n}(w^{\prime}_{n}+1)

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{n-1}\circ\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w_{n}^{\prime}+1)

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{n-1}\circ\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w_{n}+1/\alpha_{n})

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{n-1}(\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w_{n})+1)=X_{0}^{n-1}(w_{n-1}+1).

This completes the proof in this case.

(ii)
\varepsilon_{n}=+1
and
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{n-1}\searrow 1/\alpha_{n-1}-1
, as
i\to\infty
.
Let us first assume that for all
1\leq j\leq n-1
we have
\varepsilon_{j}=-1
.
This implies that
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{j}\searrow 1/\alpha_{j}-1
, for all
0\leq j\leq n-1
.
In particular,
\operatorname{Re}w_{j}=1/\alpha_{j}-1
, for all
j\geq 0
.

For
0\leq j\leq n
, choose
t_{j}\geq-1
such that
w_{j}=1/\alpha_{j}-1+it_{j}
.
Then, by Equation (
3.8
),

\displaystyle\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w)=\left(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}+1}{2}\right)(w_{0}+1)=\mathbb{Y}_{0}(1/\alpha_{0}+it_{0})+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}+1}{2}=\mathbb{Y}_{0}(it_{0})-\varepsilon_{0}+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}+1}{2}.

On the other hand, by (
3.9
),

\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{j}(it_{j})=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{j}(it_{j}+1/\alpha_{j}-1)=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{j}(w_{j})=\operatorname{Im}w_{j-1}=t_{j-1},

and hence
\mathbb{Y}_{j}(it_{j})=it_{j-1}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w^{i})=\lim_{i\to\infty}X_{0}^{n}(w^{i}_{n}+1)

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{n}(w_{n}+1)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{n-1}\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{n}+1)(1/\alpha_{n}+it_{n})+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}+1}{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{n-1}\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{n}(it_{n}))+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}+1}{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Y}_{0}(it_{0})+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}+1}{2}.

By the above equations,
\lim_{i\to\infty}\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w^{i})/\mathbb{Z}=\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w)/\mathbb{Z}
.

Now assume that there is
1\leq m\leq n-1
with
\varepsilon_{m}=+1
.
Assume that
m
is the largest integer with
1\leq m\leq n-1
and
\varepsilon_{m}=+1
.
As in the above paragraph, we note that for all
j
with
m\leq j\leq n-1
we have
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{j}\searrow 1/\alpha_{j}-1
.
Since
\varepsilon_{m}=+1
, we must have
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{m-1}\nearrow 1/\alpha_{m-1}
.
As in case (i), this implies that for
0\leq j\leq m-3
,
\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{j}\notin\mathbb{Z}
.
Hence, for
-1\leq j\leq m-2
,
w_{j}=\lim_{i\to\infty}w^{i}_{j}
.
Moreover,
\lim_{i\to\infty}\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{m-2}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

By the above paragraph,
\operatorname{Re}w_{m-1}=0
, and
m-1
is the smallest positive integer with
w_{m-1}\in K_{m-1}
.
Then,

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w)=X_{0}^{m-1}(w_{m-1}+1).

For
m-1\leq j\leq n
, let
w^{\prime}_{j}=\lim_{i\to\infty}w^{i}_{j}
.
We have
w_{j}=w_{j}^{\prime}+1-1/\alpha_{j}
.
Then, as in the previous cases, we note that

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w^{i})

\displaystyle=\lim_{i\to\infty}X_{0}^{n}(w^{i}_{n}+1)

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{n}(w^{\prime}_{n}+1)

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{m-1}\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+1)\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{n-1}\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{n}+1)(w_{n}^{\prime}+1)

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{m-1}\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+1)\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{n-1}\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{n}+1)(w_{n}+1/\alpha_{n})

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{m-1}\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+1)\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{n-1}(w_{n-1})

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{m-1}(w_{m-1}+1).

This completes the proof in this case.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

The set
W^{0}
is closed, and
\tilde{T}:W^{0}/{\mathbb{Z}}\to I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}
is well-defined, continuous, and injective.

Proof.

This is straightforward and is left to the reader.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

First we prove the injectivity.
For
n\geq 1
, each equivalence class in
W^{n}/{\mathbb{Z}}
consists of a single element, because the horizontal width of
W^{n}

is at most
\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n-1}\leq 1/2
.
Also, the width of
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(W^{n})
is at most
1/2
, which implies that each equivalence class in
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(W^{n})/{\mathbb{Z}}

consists of a single element.
Then, by Lemma
4.2
,
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:W^{n}/{\mathbb{Z}}\to I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}
is injective.
By Lemma
4.5
,
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:W^{0}/{\mathbb{Z}}\to I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}
is injective.
Similarly,
V^{\infty}
consists of a single half-infinite vertical line which is mapped to a single half-infinite vertical
line by
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
.
Thus, by Lemma
4.3
,
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:V^{\infty}/{\mathbb{Z}}\to V^{\infty}/{\mathbb{Z}}
is injective.
on the other hand, for
0\leq n<m
, the sets
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(W^{n})
,
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(W^{m})
and

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(V^{\infty})
, are pairwise disjoint. Therefore,
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}\to I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}
is injective.

Now we prove the continuity.
Let
(w^{i})_{i\geq 0}
be a convergent sequence in
I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that

(w^{i})_{i\geq 0}
converges in
I_{-1}
.
By Lemmas
4.3
and
4.4
,
if a subsequence of this sequence lies in some
W^{n}
or in
V^{\infty}
, then
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
is continuous along that subsequence.
Therefore, it is enough to deal with the case of
w^{i}\in W^{j_{i}}
, with
j_{i}\to+\infty
as
i\to+\infty
.
Without loss of generality (in order to simplify the notations) we may assume that
w^{i}\in W^{i+1}
, for
i\geq 0
.

Let
w=\lim_{i\to\infty}w^{i}
. Below, we consider three cases, based on the location of
w
.

Case 1:
w\in V^{\infty}
.

Let
(w_{j};l_{j})_{j\geq-1}
denote the trajectory of
w
, and for
i\geq 0
, let
(w^{i}_{j};l^{i}_{j})_{j\geq-1}

denote the trajectory of
w^{i}
.
Recall from the proof of Lemma
4.3
that

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w_{-1})=(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(\varepsilon_{0}+1)/2)\circ E_{0}(w_{0}),

and
E_{0}^{m}\to E_{0}
, uniformly on
V^{\infty}_{0}
.

Fix an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
.
Let us choose an integer
m\geq 5
such that for all
i\geq m
we have

|E_{0}(w_{0})-E_{0}^{i}(w_{0})|\leq\varepsilon/2,\qquad 63\cdot(0.9)^{m}\leq\varepsilon/2.

For
i\geq m
,
w^{i}\in W^{i+1}\subset V^{i}\subset V^{m}
, and
w\in V^{\infty}\subset V^{m}
.
Then, by Lemma
4.2
,
w^{i}_{m}\to w_{m}
, as
i\to\infty
.
In particular, there is
N\geq 0
such that for all
i\geq N
we have
|w_{m}-w^{i}_{m}|\leq 1
.
Below we assume that
i\geq\max\{N,m\}
, and we aim to show Equation (
4.14
).

Since both
w^{i}_{m}
and
w_{m}
belong to
V^{i}_{m}
, we may employ the estimate in Equation (
4.7
), to obtain

(4.13)

\displaystyle|E_{m}^{i}(w_{m})

\displaystyle-E_{m}^{i}(w_{m}^{i})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=m}^{i-1}|E_{m}^{l+1}(w_{m})-E_{m}^{l}(w_{m})|+|w_{m}-w_{m}^{i}|+\sum_{l=m}^{i-1}|E_{m}^{l+1}(w^{i}_{m})-E_{m}^{l}(w^{i}_{m})|

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot 3\sum_{l=m}^{\infty}(0.9)^{l-m}+|w_{m}-w^{i}_{m}|\leq 60+1=61.

Note that

\left|(w^{i}_{i}+1-1/\alpha_{i})-\left(\mathbb{Y}_{i+1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{i+1}+1}{2}\right)(w^{i}_{i+1})\right|\leq 1.

Combining the above inequality with the uniform contraction in Lemma
2.2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg|E_{i}^{i}

\displaystyle(w^{i}_{i})-\left(\mathbb{Y}_{i+1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{i+1}+1}{2}\right)(w^{i}_{i+1}+1)\bigg|

\displaystyle=\left|(w^{i}_{i}+1-1/\alpha_{i})-\left(\mathbb{Y}_{i+1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{i+1}+1}{2}\right)(w^{i}_{i+1})\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad\quad+\left(\mathbb{Y}_{i+1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{i+1}+1}{2}\right)(w^{i}_{i+1})-\left(\mathbb{Y}_{i+1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{i+1}+1}{2}\right)(w^{i}_{i+1}+1)\right|\leq 1+0.9\cdot 1\leq 2.

Using the uniform contraction one more time, this give us

\displaystyle\left|E_{m}^{i}(w_{m}^{i})-X_{m+1}^{i+1}(w^{i}_{i+1}\!+\!1)\right|=\left|X_{m+1}^{i}\circ E_{i}^{i}(w^{i}_{i})-\!X_{m+1}^{i}\circ\left(\mathbb{Y}_{i+1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{i}+1}{2}\right)(w^{i}_{i+1}+1)\right|\leq 2.

Combining the above bound with the bound in Equation (
4.13
), we conclude that

\left|E_{m}^{i}(w_{m})-X_{m+1}^{i+1}(w^{i}_{i+1}+1)\right|\leq 61+2=63.

Using the relation in Equation (
4.10
) several times, we note that

(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(\varepsilon_{0}+1)/2)\circ E_{0}^{i}(w_{0})=X_{0}^{m-1}\circ E_{m}^{i}(w_{m})
.
Thus, using the above equation with
m=0
, we obtain

(4.14)

\displaystyle|\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w)-\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w^{i})|

\displaystyle=\left|(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(\varepsilon_{0}+1)/2)\circ E_{0}(w_{0})-X_{0}^{m-1}\circ X_{m}^{i+1}(w^{i}_{i+1}+1)\right|

\displaystyle\leq|(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(\varepsilon_{0}+1)/2)\circ E_{0}(w_{0})-(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(\varepsilon_{0}+1)/2)\circ E_{0}^{i}(w_{0})|

\displaystyle+|X_{0}^{m-1}\circ E_{m}^{i}(w_{m})-X_{0}^{m-1}\circ X_{m}^{i+1}(w^{i}_{i+1}+1)|

\displaystyle\leq(0.9)\cdot\varepsilon/2+(0.9)^{m}\cdot(63)\leq\varepsilon.

This completes the proof in Case 1.

Case 2:
w\in W^{n}
for some
n\geq 1
.

Recall from the proof of Lemma
4.3
, that there are real numbers
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
such that either

W^{n}=\{w\in I_{-1}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[x_{n},y_{n})\}
or
W^{n}=\{w\in I_{-1}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in(y_{n},x_{n}]\}
.
Since,
W^{n}
and
W^{i+1}
are pairwise disjoint, for
i+1>n
, and
w_{i}\in W^{i+1}
, we must have
\operatorname{Re}w=x_{n}
.

Note that for integers
j
with
0\leq j\leq n-2
,
\operatorname{Re}w_{j}\notin{\mathbb{Z}}
.
This implies that
w^{i}_{n-1}\to w_{n-1}
, as
i\to\infty
.
It follows from Equation (
4.12
) that
\operatorname{Re}w_{n-1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Hence,
\operatorname{Re}w_{n}=0
.
Below we consider two scenarios.

Case 2-i:
\varepsilon_{n}=-1
.

Since
w^{i}_{n}\notin K_{n}
, for large
i
, we must have
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{n-1}\nearrow\operatorname{Re}w_{n-1}
.
It follows that
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{n}\nearrow 1/\alpha_{n}+w_{n}
.
Let us define
w_{n}^{\prime}=1/\alpha_{n}+w_{n}
.
For
i\geq n
, let
(w^{\prime}_{i};l_{i}^{\prime})_{i\geq n}
denote the trajectory of
w_{n}^{\prime}
, defined in the same fashion according to
Equation (
4.3
).
We must have
\operatorname{Re}w^{\prime}_{i}=1/\alpha_{i}-1
, for all
i\geq n+1
, and by Equation (
3.9
),
w_{i}=w_{i}^{\prime}+1-1/\alpha_{i}
, for all
i\geq n
.

If
\varepsilon_{n+1}=-1
, then we must have
\operatorname{Re}w_{n+1}^{i}\nearrow 1/\alpha_{n+1}-1
.
This is a contradiction, since this implies that
w^{i}\in W^{n+1}
, for sufficiently large
i
.
Therefore, we have
\varepsilon_{n+1}=+1
and
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{n+1}\searrow 1/\alpha_{n+1}-1
.
Indeed, for the same reason, we must have
\varepsilon_{i}=-1
, for all
i\geq n+2
.

Assume that
i\geq n
. We have

\left|(w_{i}-\left(\mathbb{Y}_{i+1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{i+1}+1}{2}\right)(w^{i}_{i+1})\right|\leq 1.

Therefore, applying
X_{n+1}^{i}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|w_{n}-X_{n+1}^{i+1}(w^{i}_{i+1}+1)\right|\leq 1\cdot(0.9)^{i-n}.

Then,

\displaystyle\left|\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w)-\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w^{i})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|X_{0}^{n}(w_{n})-X_{0}^{i+1}(w^{i}_{i+1}+1)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|X_{0}^{n}(w_{n})-X_{0}^{n}\circ X_{n+1}^{i+1}(w^{i}_{i+1}+1)\right|\leq 1\cdot(0.9)^{i+1}.

This completes the proof in this case.

Case 2-ii:
\varepsilon_{n}=+1
.

Here, we must have
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{n-1}\searrow\operatorname{Re}w_{n-1}
.
It follows that
w^{i}_{n}\nearrow 1/\alpha_{n}+w_{n}
. Let
w_{n}^{\prime}=1/\alpha_{n}+w_{n}
.
Let
(w^{\prime}_{i};l^{\prime}_{i})_{i\geq n}
denote the trajectory of
w^{\prime}_{n}
.
We must have
\varepsilon_{n+1}=+1
, otherwise,
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{n+1}\nearrow 1/\alpha_{n+1}-1
and hence
w^{i}\in W^{n}

for sufficiently large
i
. For the same reason, we must have
\varepsilon_{i}=-1
, for all
i\geq n+2
.
As in the previous case, we obtain
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w^{i})\to\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w)
.

Case 3:
w\in W_{0}
.

If
\varepsilon_{0}=-1
, either
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}\nearrow 1
and hence
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{0}\nearrow 1/\alpha_{0}
, or
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}\searrow 1-\alpha_{0}

and hence
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{0}\searrow 1/\alpha_{0}-1
.
Similarly, if
\varepsilon_{0}=+1
, either
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}\nearrow\alpha_{0}
and hence
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{0}\searrow 1/\alpha_{0}-1
,
or
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}\searrow 0
and hence
\operatorname{Re}w^{i}_{0}\nearrow 1/\alpha_{0}
.
All these scenarios may be dealt with as in Case 2.
∎

4.3.
The map
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
, and its properties

Proposition 4.6
.

For every
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
, the map
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:I_{-1}\to I_{-1}
induces
a homeomorphism

\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha},

via the projection
w\mapsto s(e^{2\pi iw})
, that is,

s(e^{2\pi i\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w)})=\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}(s(e^{2\pi iw}))
for all
w\in I_{-1}
.
Moreover,
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
acts as rotation by
2\pi\alpha
in the tangential direction,
that is, there is a function
g_{\alpha}
such that

\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}(re^{2\pi i\theta})=g_{\alpha}(r,\theta)e^{2\pi i(\theta+\alpha)}

for every
re^{2\pi i\theta}\in\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.1
,
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}\to I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}
induces a continuous and injective map

\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
of the set
\{s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in I_{-1}\}
.
From the construction, we note that as
\operatorname{Im}z\to\infty
in
I_{-1}/\mathbb{Z}
,

\operatorname{Im}\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(z)\to+\infty
.
This implies that we may continuously extend
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
onto
0
by setting
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}(0)=0
.
Since
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
is compact, and
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
is continuous
and injective, it must be a homeomorphism.

In order to show that
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
acts as rotation by
2\pi\alpha
in the tangential direction, it is enough to show
that
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
acts as translation by
-\alpha
on
\cup_{n\geq 0}W^{n}\cup V^{\infty}
.
However, because
V^{\infty}
has empty interior, by the continuity of
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
, it is enough to prove this
on the sets
W^{n}
.
The latter property follows from the definition of
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
and the functional relation in Equation (
3.8
).
We present the details below.

For
w_{-1}\in W^{0}
, we have

w_{-1}=(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(1+\epsilon_{0})/2)(w_{0}),\qquad\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w_{1})=(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(1+\epsilon_{0})/2)(w_{0}+1).

Thus, by the definition of
\mathbb{Y}_{0}
,

\operatorname{Re}(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(1+\epsilon_{0})/2)(w_{0}+1)=\operatorname{Re}w_{-1}-\epsilon_{0}\alpha_{0}.

Therefore,

\arg\left(\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}(s(e^{2\pi iw_{-1}}))\right)=\arg(s(e^{2\pi iw_{-1}}))+\epsilon_{0}\alpha_{0}=\arg(s(e^{2\pi iw_{-1}}))+\alpha.

Now, fix an arbitrary
n\geq 1
, and let
w_{-1}\in W^{n}
be an arbitrary point with trajectory
(w_{i},l_{i})_{i\geq-1}
.
For
j=n,n-1,n-2,\dots,-1
, let us define the points
\xi_{j}=X_{j+1}^{n}(w_{n}+1)
.
We note that in this case we must have
\varepsilon_{n}=-1
, and
\varepsilon_{i}=+1
for all
i=n-1,n-2,\dots,1
.
Then, using
1/\alpha_{n-1}=a_{n-1}-\alpha_{n}
and Equation (
3.11
), we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}w_{n-1}-\operatorname{Re}\xi_{n-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\operatorname{Re}(\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w_{n}))+a_{n-1}-1\right)-\operatorname{Re}(\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w_{n}+1))

\displaystyle=(a_{n-1}-1)-\alpha_{n}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\alpha_{n-1}}+\alpha_{n}-1\right)-\alpha_{n}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{n-1}}-1.

For the next step, we have
1/\alpha_{n-2}=a_{n-2}+\alpha_{n-1}
, and use Equation (
3.12
), to obtain

\operatorname{Re}w_{n-2}-\operatorname{Re}\xi_{n-2}=-\alpha_{n-1}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha_{n-1}}-1\right)+a_{n-2}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{n-2}}-1.

Repeating the above process for levels
n-3,n-4,\dots,0
, we end up with

\operatorname{Re}w_{0}-\operatorname{Re}\xi_{0}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}-1.

In the last stage we apply the map
\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(1+\varepsilon_{0})/2
, to obtain,

\operatorname{Re}w_{-1}-\operatorname{Re}\xi_{-1}=-\varepsilon_{0}\alpha_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}-1\right)=-\varepsilon_{0}+\varepsilon_{0}\alpha_{0}.

Thus,
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
acts as translation by
-\varepsilon_{0}\alpha_{0}
which is the same as
-\alpha
modulo
\mathbb{Z}
.
This completes the argument for the set
W^{n}
.
∎

Proposition 4.7
.

For every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
, we have
s\circ\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\circ s=\mathbb{T}_{-\alpha}

on
\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha}
.

Proof.

Recall that by Proposition
3.4
, we have
s(\mathbb{M}_{\alpha})=\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha}
.
Thus both of the maps
\mathbb{T}_{-\alpha}
and
s\circ\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\circ s
are defined on
\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha}

and map into
\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha}
.

When
\alpha
changes to
-\alpha
,
\varepsilon_{0}
changes to
-\varepsilon_{0}
, but all the remaining numbers
\alpha_{i}

and
\varepsilon_{i+1}
, for
i\geq 0
, remain the same.
Let
I_{-1}
and
I^{\prime}_{-1}
denote the corresponding sets for
\alpha
and
-\alpha
, respectively.
We have
-s(I^{\prime}_{-1})=I_{-1}
; see proof of Proposition
3.4
.
From the definition of the maps
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
and
\tilde{T}_{-\alpha}
, we can see that

(-s)\circ\tilde{T}_{\alpha}\circ(-s)=\tilde{T}_{-\alpha}
on
I^{\prime}_{-1}
.
Projecting onto
\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha}
via
w\mapsto s(e^{2\pi iw})
we obtain the desired relation.
∎

Proposition 4.8
.

There is a constant
C
such that for every
\alpha\in(-1/2,1/2)\setminus\mathbb{Q}
and every integer

k
satisfying
0\leq k<1/|\alpha|
, we have

\frac{C^{-1}}{1+\min\{k,|\alpha|^{-1}-k\}}\leq|\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{k}}(+1)|\leq\frac{C}{1+\min\{k,|\alpha|^{-1}-k\}}.

Proof.

By virtue of Proposition
4.7
, it is enough to show this for
\alpha\in(0,1/2)\setminus\mathbb{Q}
.
In that case, we have
\alpha_{0}=\alpha
,
\varepsilon_{0}=+1
, and
\mathbb{Y}_{0}=-s\circ Y_{\alpha_{0}}
.
The point
+1
may be lifted under the projection
w\mapsto s(e^{2\pi iw})
to the point
+1
in
I_{-1}
.
In the trajectory of
+1
, we have
l_{-1}=+1
and
w_{0}=0
.
Therefore, by the definition of
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
, for all integers
k
satisfying
0\leq k<1/\alpha
, we have

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{k}}(+1)=\mathbb{Y}_{0}(k)+(1+\varepsilon_{0})/2=\mathbb{Y}_{0}(k)+1
.
This implies that

|\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{k}}(+1)|=\left|s\left(e^{2\pi i\mathbb{Y}_{0}(k)}\right)\right|=\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi\alpha}-e^{\pi\alpha i}}{e^{-3\pi\alpha}-e^{-\pi\alpha i}e^{-2\pi\alpha ik}}\Big|.

We need to estimate the right hand side of the above equation.
The numerator of that formula is proportional to
\alpha
;
see Equations (
7.1
) and (
7.2
) for a more precise estimate on this.
For the denominator we have,

\displaystyle\left|e^{-3\pi\alpha}-e^{-\pi\alpha i}e^{-2\pi\alpha ik}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|e^{-3\pi\alpha}-1\right|+\left|1-e^{-\pi\alpha i}\right|+\left|e^{-\pi\alpha i}-e^{-\pi\alpha i}e^{-2\pi\alpha ik}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 3\pi\alpha+\pi\alpha+2\pi\alpha\min\{k,\alpha^{-1}-k\}

\displaystyle\leq 4\pi\alpha\left(1+\min\{k,\alpha^{-1}-k\}\right).

To give a lower bound on the left hand side of the above equation, let us consider two cases.
If
k\leq 5
, or
\alpha^{-1}-k\leq 5
, we have

\displaystyle\left|e^{-3\pi\alpha}-e^{-\pi\alpha i}e^{-2\pi\alpha ik}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|e^{-3\pi\alpha}-1\right|

\displaystyle\geq\alpha

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\alpha}{6}\left(1+\min\{k,\alpha^{-1}-k\}\right).

If
k
satisfies
5\leq k\leq\alpha^{-1}-5
, by the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\left|e^{-3\pi\alpha}-e^{-\pi\alpha i}e^{-2\pi\alpha ik}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|e^{-\pi\alpha i}e^{-2\pi\alpha ik}-e^{-\pi\alpha i}\right|-\left|e^{-\pi\alpha i}-e^{-3\pi\alpha}\right|

\displaystyle\geq 2\pi\alpha\min\{k,\alpha^{-1}-k\}-(\pi\alpha+3\pi\alpha)

\displaystyle\geq 2\pi\alpha\left(\min\{k,\alpha^{-1}-k\}-2\right)

\displaystyle\geq\pi\alpha\left(\min\{k,\alpha^{-1}-k\}+1\right).

Combining the above inequalities, we obtain the desired estimate in the proposition for a suitable constant
C
.
∎

The estimate of the sizes of the orbit presented in Proposition
4.8
widely holds for holomorphic map of the
from
e^{2\pi i\alpha}z+a_{2}z^{2}+\dots
, with
a_{2}\neq 0
.
More precisely, for a fixed nonlinearity (higher order terms), and a choice of a point close enough to the fixed point
at
0
, there is a constant
C
such that the upper and lower bounds in the estimate
hold for those integers.
The constant
C
is independent of the rotation number
\alpha
and the number of iterates
k
.
For large values of
\alpha
, this mostly follows from continuity of the map.
For smaller values of
\alpha
, one employs the existence of perturbed Fatou-coordinates, and elementary estimates on
their behaviour. See
[
Shi00
]
for more details on this.

5.
The renormalisation operator
\mathcal{R}_{m}

In this section we define the renormalisation of
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}

using a suitable return map to a fundamental set in
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
.
This construction is in the spirit of the sector renormalisation, qualitatively presented by Douady and
Ghys
[
Dou87
]
, and quantitatively employed by Yoccoz in the study of the small divisors problem in
complex dimension one
[
Yoc95b
]
.
Our construction of renormalisation relies on the model for the changes of coordinates we defined
in Section
2
.

5.1.
Definition of
\mathcal{R}_{m}

Fix an arbitrary
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
.
Since
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha+1}=\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha+1}=\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
, we may assume that

\alpha\in(-1/2,1/2)
.
Let us first assume that
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
.

Consider the set

S_{\alpha}=\{z\in\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\setminus\{0\}\mid\arg z\in([0,2\pi\alpha)+2\pi\mathbb{Z})\}.

This consists of all the non-zero points in
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
which lie in a “sector” of angle
2\pi\alpha
at
0
.
Because
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
acts as rotation by
2\pi\alpha
in the tangential direction, Proposition
4.6
,
for every
z\in S_{\alpha}
, there is the smallest integer
k_{z}\geq 2
such that
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ k_{z}}(z)\in S_{\alpha}
.
Indeed, for every
z\in S
, either
k_{z}
is equal to the integer part of
1/\alpha
or
k_{z}
is equal to the
integer part of
1/\alpha
plus
1
.
The map
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ k_{z}}
is the first return map to
S_{\alpha}
in the dynamical system

\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
.

Recall that
\mathbb{Y}_{0}(w+1/\alpha_{0})=\mathbb{Y}_{0}(w)+(1+\varepsilon_{0})/2=\mathbb{Y}_{0}+1
.
Let us consider the map

\psi_{\alpha}:\mathbb{H}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}

defined as

\psi_{\alpha}(w)=s\left(e^{2\pi i\mathbb{Y}_{0}(w)}\right)=s\left(e^{2\pi i(-s\circ Y_{\alpha_{0}}(w))}\right)=\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi\alpha}-e^{\pi\alpha i}}{e^{-3\pi\alpha}-e^{-\pi\alpha i}e^{-2\pi\alpha iw}}\Big|e^{2\pi i\alpha\operatorname{Re}w}.

We note that

(5.1)

S_{\alpha}\subset\psi_{\alpha}\left(\{w\in\mathbb{H}^{\prime}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[0,1)\}\right).

The map
\psi_{\alpha}
is continuous and injective on
\{w\in\mathbb{H}^{\prime}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[0,1)\}
, and
it sends half-infinite vertical lines in that set to straight rays landing at
0
.

There is a continuous inverse branch of
\psi_{\alpha}
defined on
S_{\alpha}
, that is,

(5.2)

\phi_{\alpha}:S_{\alpha}\to\{w\in\mathbb{H}^{\prime}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[0,1)\}.

The above map is continuous and injective.
(This map is the analogue of the perturbed Fatou coordinate for
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
)

The return map
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{k_{z}}}:S_{\alpha}\to S_{\alpha}
induces a map

h_{\alpha}:\phi_{\alpha}(S_{\alpha})\to\phi_{\alpha}(S_{\alpha})

via
\psi_{\alpha}
and
\phi_{\alpha}
, that is,

h_{\alpha}(w)=\phi_{\alpha}\circ\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ k_{\psi_{\alpha}(w)}}\circ\psi_{\alpha}(w).

Using the projection
w\mapsto e^{2\pi iw}
, the map
h_{\alpha}
projects to a map
E_{\alpha}
, defined on

e^{2\pi i\phi_{\alpha}(S_{\alpha})}\subset\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
.
That is,
e^{2\pi ih_{\alpha}(w)}=E_{\alpha}(e^{2\pi iw})
for all
w\in\phi_{\alpha}(S_{\alpha})
.
We may extend
E_{\alpha}
onto
0
by letting it map
0
to
0
.
We call the map
E_{\alpha}
, and its domain of definition, the renormalisation of

\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
, and denote it by

\mathcal{R}_{m}(\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha})
.

Remark 5.1
.

In the above definition, one may replace the set
S_{\alpha}
by any set of the form

S_{\alpha}^{\eta}=\{z\in\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\setminus\{0\}\mid\arg z\in\left([2\pi\eta,2\pi\eta+2\pi\alpha)+2\pi\mathbb{Z}\right)\}

for a fixed
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
, and use the return map of
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
on
S_{\alpha}^{\eta}
to
define the renormalisation of
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
in the same fashion.
The definition of renormalisation is independent of the choice of
\eta
.
That is, one obtains the same map defined on the same set of points.
We avoid considering this generality for the sake of simplicity, although, the independence from
\eta
will
become evident from the proof of Proposition
5.2
.

Also, when projecting the map
h_{\alpha}
to obtain
E_{\alpha}
we have used the projection map
w\mapsto e^{2\pi iw}

for the sake of consistency with how the sector renormalisation is defined.
This may appear in contrast to the projection map
w\mapsto s\left(e^{2\pi iw}\right)
used in the definition of the
sets
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
.
Due to the properties in Proposition
3.4
and Lemma
3.5
, this does not cause any problems.
See the proof of
5.2
for more details.

The above process defines the renormalisation of
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}

when
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
.
For
\alpha\in(-1/2,0)
, we first use dynamical conjugation via the complex conjugation map so that the
rotation number of the map becomes
-\alpha\in(0,1/2)
and then repeat the above process.
By Proposition
3.4
, we have
s(\mathbb{M}_{\alpha})=\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha}
and by Proposition
4.7

we have
s\circ\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\circ s=\mathbb{T}_{-\alpha}
.
Therefore, for
\alpha\in(-1/2,0)
, we define

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{m}(\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=s\Big(\mathcal{R}_{m}\big(s\circ\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\circ s:s\left(\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\right)\to s\left(\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\right)\big)\Big)
