\displaystyle=s\big(\mathcal{R}_{m}(\mathbb{T}_{-\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha})\big),

where the complex conjugation
s
on the right hand side of the above equation means that we consider the
dynamical conjugate of the map and its domain of definition using the complex conjugation map.
More precisely, in terms of our notations, that means

s(f:X\to Y)=\left(s\circ f\circ s:s(X)\to s(Y)\right).

5.2.
Invariance of the class of maps under the renormalisation operator

Proposition 5.2
.

For every
\alpha\in(-1/2,1/2)\setminus\mathbb{Q}
we have

\mathcal{R}_{m}(\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha})=(\mathbb{T}_{-1/\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{-1/\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{-1/\alpha}).

Proof.

Let us first assume that
\alpha\in(0,1/2)\setminus\mathbb{Q}
.
Recall the numbers
(\alpha_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 0}
defined in Section
3.1
.
We shall also use the notations
I_{n}
,
K_{n}
and
J_{n}
introduced in Section
3.3
.

Let us consider the set

\hat{S}_{\alpha}=\{w\in I_{0}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[0,1)\}.

By the definition of
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
in Section
3
, in particular Equations (
3.15
) and (
3.17
),
we have
\psi_{\alpha}(I_{0})\cup\{0\}=\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
, and

\psi_{\alpha}(\hat{S}_{\alpha})=S_{\alpha}.

Indeed,
\psi_{\alpha}:\hat{S}_{\alpha}\to S_{\alpha}
is a homeomorphism.
This implies that

\phi_{\alpha}:S_{\alpha}\to\hat{S}_{\alpha}

is a homeomorphism as well.
Therefore, we have

h_{\alpha}:\hat{S}_{\alpha}\to\hat{S}_{\alpha}.

Because
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
and
I_{0}
is periodic of period
+1
, by Proposition
3.4

and Lemma
3.5
, we have

\{e^{2\pi iw}\mid w\in\hat{S}_{\alpha}\}\cup\{0\}=s(\mathbb{M}_{1/\alpha})=\mathbb{M}_{-1/\alpha}.

This shows that the map
h_{\alpha}
projects to the map
E_{\alpha}
defined on
\mathbb{M}_{-1/\alpha}
, via the projection

w\mapsto e^{2\pi iw}
.
In other words, the domain of definition of

\mathcal{R}_{m}(\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha})
is
\mathbb{M}_{-1/\alpha}
.
Now, we need to show that
E_{\alpha}=\mathbb{T}_{-1/\alpha}
.

We continue to assume that
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
.
Let us consider the set

\tilde{S}_{\alpha}=\{w\in I_{-1}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in(1-\alpha,1].

The map
w\mapsto s(e^{2\pi iw})
is a homeomorphism from
\tilde{S}_{\alpha}
to
S_{\alpha}
.
We also have the homeomorphism

\mathbb{Y}_{0}+1:\hat{S}_{\alpha}\to\tilde{S}_{\alpha}.

Recall from Section
4
, that
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}

is induced from
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
on
I_{-1}
, via the projection
w\mapsto s(e^{2\pi iw})
.
Therefore, the return map of
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
on
S_{\alpha}

corresponds to the return map of
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:I_{-1}\to I_{-1}
on
\tilde{S}_{\alpha}
.

The map
\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(1+\varepsilon_{0})/2=\mathbb{Y}_{0}+1:I_{0}\to I_{-1}
is a homeomorphism.
Let us consider the map

\hat{T}_{\alpha}=(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+1)^{-1}\circ\tilde{T}_{\alpha}\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+1):I_{0}\to I_{0}.

Then, through the conjugacy
\mathbb{Y}_{0}+1
, the return map of
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:I_{-1}\to I_{-1}
on
\tilde{S}_{\alpha}

corresponds to the return map of
\hat{T}_{\alpha}:I_{0}\to I_{0}
on
\hat{S}_{\alpha}
.
Because
\hat{S}_{\alpha}=\phi_{\alpha}(S_{\alpha})
, by the definition of renormalisation, the return map of

\hat{T}_{\alpha}:I_{0}\to I_{0}
on
\hat{S}_{\alpha}
projects via
w\mapsto e^{2\pi iw}
to the map
E_{\alpha}
.
Below, we investigate this return map in more details.

Let
w\in I_{-1}
be an arbitrary point, and let
(w_{i};l_{i})_{i\geq-1}
denote the trajectory of
w
defined in
Section
4.1
.
By the definition of
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
, if
w_{0}\in K_{0}
we have

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w_{-1})=(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+1)((\mathbb{Y}_{0}+1)^{-1}(w_{-1})+1)
.
This implies that for
w\in K_{0}
,
\hat{T}_{\alpha}(w)=w+1
.
On the other hand, if
w_{0}\in I_{0}\setminus K_{0}
,
\hat{T}_{\alpha}(w_{0})
is defined as follows:

(i)

if there is
n\geq 1
such that
w_{n}\in K_{n}
, and for all
1\leq i\leq n-1
,
w_{i}\in I_{i}\setminus K_{i}
, then

\hat{T}_{\alpha}(w_{0})=\left(\mathbb{Y}_{1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}+1}{2}\right)\circ\left(\mathbb{Y}_{2}+\frac{\varepsilon_{2}+1}{2}\right)\circ\cdots\circ\left(\mathbb{Y}_{n}+\frac{\varepsilon_{n}+1}{2}\right)(w_{n}+1);

(ii)

if for all
i\geq 1
,
w_{i}\in I_{i}\setminus K_{i}
, then

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w_{0})=\lim_{n\to+\infty}\left(\mathbb{Y}_{1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}+1}{2}\right)\circ\left(\mathbb{Y}_{2}+\frac{\varepsilon_{2}+1}{2}\right)\circ\cdots\circ\left(\mathbb{Y}_{n}+\frac{\varepsilon_{n}+1}{2}\right)(w_{n}+1-1/\alpha_{n}).

Combining the above together, we conclude that the return map of
\hat{T}_{\alpha}:I_{0}\to I_{0}
on the set

\hat{S}_{\alpha}
consists of a finite number of translations by
+1
which take a point in

\hat{S}_{\alpha}
to a point in
I_{0}\setminus K_{0}
, and then one iterate of either the map in
item (i) or the the map in item (ii), depending on which scenario takes place.

Since any point in
I_{0}
and its integer translations are sent by
w\mapsto s(e^{2\pi iw})
to the same point,
each translation by +1 on
K_{0}
induce the identity map via the projection
w\mapsto s(e^{2\pi iw})
.
Thus, by the above paragraph, the rerun map of
\hat{T}_{\alpha}:I_{0}\to I_{0}
on the set
\hat{S}_{\alpha}

and the map
\hat{T}_{\alpha}:I_{0}\setminus K_{0}\to\hat{S}_{\alpha}
induce the same map via the
projection
w\mapsto s(e^{2\pi iw})
.
On the other hand, by the definition in Section
4.1
, the map specified in items (i) and (ii) is

\tilde{T}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}:I_{0}/\mathbb{Z}\to I_{0}/\mathbb{Z}
.
Therefore, the rerun map of
\hat{T}_{\alpha}:I_{0}\to I_{0}
on the set
\hat{S}_{\alpha}
, and the map

\tilde{T}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}:I_{0}/\mathbb{Z}\to I_{0}/\mathbb{Z}
induce the same map via the projection

w\mapsto s(e^{2\pi iw})
.

By the definition in Section
4.1
,
\tilde{T}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}:I_{0}/\mathbb{Z}\to I_{0}/\mathbb{Z}

induces
\mathbb{T}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}:\mathbb{M}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}\to\mathbb{M}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}

via the projection
w\mapsto s(e^{2\pi iw})
.
If we project via
w\mapsto e^{2\pi iw}
,
\tilde{T}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}
induces

s\circ\mathbb{T}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}\circ s
on
s(\mathbb{M}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}})
.
By Proposition
3.4
,

s(\mathbb{M}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}})=\mathbb{M}_{-\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}=\mathbb{M}_{-1/\alpha_{0}}=\mathbb{M}_{-1/\alpha}

and by Proposition
4.7
,
s\circ\mathbb{T}_{\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}\circ s=\mathbb{T}_{-\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1}}=\mathbb{T}_{-1/\alpha}
.
Note that here we have used
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
, which implies that
\alpha_{0}=\alpha
and

1/\alpha_{0}=-\varepsilon_{1}\alpha_{1},\mod\mathbb{Z}
.

Now assume that
\alpha\in(-1/2,0)
.
By the definition of renormalisation for
\alpha\in(-1/2,0)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{m}(\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=s\left(\mathcal{R}_{m}(\mathbb{T}_{-\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{-\alpha})\right)

\displaystyle=s\left(\mathbb{T}_{1/\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{1/\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{1/\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=s\circ\mathbb{T}_{1/\alpha}\circ:s(\mathbb{M}_{1/\alpha})\to s(\mathbb{M}_{1/\alpha})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{T}_{-1/\alpha}:\mathbb{M}_{-1/\alpha}\to\mathbb{M}_{-1/\alpha}.\qed

6.
Arithmetic classes of Brjuno and Herman

In this section we define the arithmetic classes of Brjuno and Herman.
This requires the action of the modular group
\mathrm{PGL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
on the real line, which produces
continued fraction type representation of irrational numbers.
To study the action of this group, one may choose a fundamental interval for the action of
z\mapsto z+1

and study the action of
z\mapsto 1/z
on that interval.
When the interval
(0,1)
is chosen, one obtains the standard representation (continued fraction).

Because of the nature of the renormalisation, we work with the fundamental interval
(-1/2,1/2)
for the translation.
This is partly due to the symmetry of the renormalisation scheme

\{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha},\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}\}

with respect to the rotation stated in part (v) of Theorem
A
.
This choice of the fundamental interval leads to a modified representation (continued fraction) of irrationals,
also known as nearest integer continued fraction.

The modified continued fraction was used by Yoccoz in
[
Yoc88
,
Yoc95b
]
to
characterise the Brjuno numbers.
He also studied the relation between the Brjuno functions (see below) in terms of the standard and the
modified continued fractions.
A systematic in-depth study of the Brjuno condition, its properties, and its dependence on the choice of the continued
fraction is carried out by Marmi, Moussa and Yoccoz in
[
MMY97
,
MMY01
,
MMY06
]
.
A key point in those papers is that the Brjuno function is a cocycle under the action of
\mathrm{PGL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

In
[
Yoc02
]
, Yoccoz uses the standard continued fraction to identify the arithmetic class
{\mathscr{H}}

(see Definition
6.2
).
The main aim of this section is to identify the equivalent form of the Herman condition in terms of the modified
(nearest integer) continued fraction.
Some of our technical arguments may be found in, or may follow from,
[
MMY97
,
MMY01
,
MMY06
]
.
We present a quick route to the equivalent form of the Herman condition in terms of the modified
continued fractions.

We shall only use the modified representation beyond this section.
For basic properties of continued fractions one may consult
[
Khi64
]
.

6.1.
Standard continued fraction

For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let
\langle x\rangle\in(0,1)
denote the fractional part of
x
, that is,
x\in{\mathbb{Z}}+\langle x\rangle
.
For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
, we may define the numbers
\tilde{\alpha}_{n}\in(0,1)
as
\tilde{\alpha}_{0}=\langle\alpha\rangle
and

\tilde{\alpha}_{n+1}=\langle 1/\tilde{\alpha}_{n}\rangle
, for
n\geq 0
.
Then we identify the unique integers
\tilde{a}_{n}
, for
n\geq-1
, according to

(6.1)

\alpha=\tilde{a}_{-1}+\tilde{\alpha}_{0},\quad 1/\tilde{\alpha}_{n}=\tilde{a}_{n}+\tilde{\alpha}_{n+1}.

These may be combined to obtain

\alpha=\tilde{a}_{-1}+\cfrac{1}{\tilde{a}_{0}+\cfrac{1}{\ddots+\cfrac{1}{\tilde{a}_{n}+\tilde{\alpha}_{n+1}}}},\;\text{for }n\geq-1.

The
n
-th convergent of
\alpha
is define as

\frac{\tilde{p}_{n}}{\tilde{q}_{n}}=\tilde{a}_{-1}+\cfrac{1}{\tilde{a}_{0}+\cfrac{1}{\ddots+\cfrac{1}{\tilde{a}_{n}}}},\;\text{for }n\geq-1.

Then,

\alpha=(\tilde{p}_{n}+\tilde{p}_{n-1}\tilde{\alpha}_{n+1})/(\tilde{q}_{n}+\tilde{q}_{n-1}\tilde{\alpha}_{n+1}),\;\text{for }n\geq-1,

which implies

(6.2)

\alpha_{n+1}=-(\alpha\tilde{q}_{n}-\tilde{p}_{n})/(\alpha\tilde{q}_{n-1}-\tilde{p}_{n-1}),\;\text{for }n\geq-1.

In
[
Brj71
]
, see also
[
Che64
]
, Brjuno introduced the important series
\sum_{n=-1}^{+\infty}\tilde{q}_{n}^{-1}\log\tilde{q}_{n+1}
.
In
[
Yoc95b
]
, Yoccoz defines a closely related series which enjoys remarkable equivariant properties
with respect to the action of
\mathrm{PGL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
.
To define that, we need to introduce the numbers

\tilde{\beta}_{-2}=\alpha,\quad\tilde{\beta}_{-1}=+1,\quad\tilde{\beta}_{n}=\prod_{i=0}^{n}\tilde{\alpha}_{i},\;\text{for }n\geq 0.

In terms of the convergents, Equation (
6.2
) gives us

(6.3)

\tilde{\beta}_{n}=(-1)^{n}(\alpha\tilde{q}_{n-1}-\tilde{p}_{n-1}),\;\text{for }n\geq-1.

Define the (standard Brjuno) function
\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}:{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}\to(0,+\infty)\cup\{+\infty\}
as

(6.4)

\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\alpha)=\sum_{n=-1}^{+\infty}\tilde{\beta}_{n}\log\frac{1}{\tilde{\alpha}_{n+1}}=\sum_{n=-1}^{+\infty}(-1)^{n}(\alpha\tilde{q}_{n-1}-\tilde{p}_{n-1})\log\frac{\alpha\tilde{q}_{n-1}-\tilde{p}_{n-1}}{\tilde{p}_{n}-\alpha\tilde{q}_{n}}.

This is a highly irregular function;
\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\alpha)=+\infty
for generic choice of
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
One may refer to
[
MMY97
,
MMY01
,
JM18
]
, and the extensive list of references therein,
for detailed analysis of the regularity properties of this function.
In this paper we are not concerned with the regularity, but only exploit the equivariant properties of the Brjuno
function with respect to the action of
PGL(2,\mathbb{Z})
.

Definition 6.1
.

An irrational number
\alpha
is called a
Brjuno number
if
\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\alpha)<+\infty
.

The function
\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}
satisfies the remarkable relations

(6.5)

\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\alpha)=\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\alpha+n),\;\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\alpha)=\alpha\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(1/\alpha)+\log(1/\alpha),

for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
and all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. These show that the set of Brjuno numbers is

\mathrm{PGL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
-invariant.

Let
{\mathscr{B}}
denote the set of Brjuno numbers.
The definition of the Brjuno numbers given in the introduction is consistent with the one given in
Definition
6.1
.
That is because,
|\sum_{n=-1}^{+\infty}\tilde{q}_{n}^{-1}\log\tilde{q}_{n+1}-\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\alpha)|
is uniformly
bounded from above.

The set of Herman numbers is defined in a different fashion.
To that end, we need to consider the diffeomorphisms
h_{r}:{\mathbb{R}}\to(0,+\infty)
, for
r\in(0,1)
:

h_{r}(y)=\begin{cases}r^{-1}(y-\log r^{-1}+1)&\text{if }y\geq\log r^{-1},\\
e^{y}&\text{if }y\leq\log r^{-1}.\end{cases}

Each
h_{r}
satisfies

(6.6)

\begin{gathered}h_{r}(\log r^{-1})=h_{r}^{\prime}(\log r^{-1})=r^{-1},\\
e^{y}\geq h_{r}(y)\geq y+1,\forall y\in{\mathbb{R}},\\
h_{r}^{\prime}(y)\geq 1,\forall y\geq 0.\end{gathered}

Following Yoccoz
[
Yoc02
]
, we give the following definition.

Definition 6.2
.

An irrational number
\alpha
is of
Herman type
, if for all
n\geq 0
there is
m\geq n
such that

h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}(0)\geq\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{m}).

In the above definition, the composition
h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}
is understood as the
identity map when
m=n
, and as
h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}
when
m=n+1
.
The set of Herman numbers is denoted by
{\mathscr{H}}
.
It follows from the definition that
7
7

7

In this paper we do not make a distinction between
“
\subset
” and “
\subseteq
”. If strict inclusion is meant, we use “
\subsetneq
”.

{\mathscr{H}}\subset{\mathscr{B}}.

That is because, if
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
, then
\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha})=\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{0})=+\infty
.
Repeatedly using the functional equations in Equation (
6.5
), one concludes that

\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{m})=+\infty
for all
m\geq 0
.
In particular, the inequality in the above definition never holds.

In Definition
6.2
, one may only require that for large
n
there is
m
such that the inequality
holds. That is because, if
m^{\prime}
works for some
n^{\prime}
, then the same
m^{\prime}
works for all
n\leq n^{\prime}
.
This shows that
\tilde{\alpha}_{0}\in{\mathscr{H}}
if and only if
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\in{\mathscr{H}}
.
On the other hand, since
\alpha
and
\alpha+1
produce the same sequence of
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}
, we see that
{\mathscr{H}}
is
invariant under
z\mapsto z+1
. These show that
{\mathscr{H}}
is invariant under the action of

\mathrm{PGL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Recall that
\alpha
is a Diophantine number, if there are
\tau\geq 0
and
c>0
such that
for all
p/q\in{\mathbb{Q}}
with
q\geq 1
we have
|\alpha-p/q|\geq c/q^{2+\tau}
.
Any Diophantine number is of Herman type. Since the set of Diophantine numbers has full Lebesgue measure in

{\mathbb{R}}
, the sets
{\mathscr{H}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}
have full Lebesgue measure in
{\mathbb{R}}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

The set
{\mathscr{B}}\setminus{\mathscr{H}}
is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha
be an irrational number such that there is an integer
k\geq 0
such that

\tilde{a}_{k}=1
, and for all
i\geq k
we have
e^{\tilde{a}_{i}}\leq\tilde{a}_{i+1}\leq e^{2\tilde{a}_{i}}-1
.
Evidently, the set of such irrational numbers is dense in
{\mathbb{R}}
. Below we show that any such
\alpha
belongs to

{\mathscr{B}}\setminus{\mathscr{H}}
.

For integers
i\geq k
,

\tilde{\beta}_{i}\log(1/\tilde{\alpha}_{i+1})\leq\tilde{\beta}_{i}\log(\tilde{a}_{i+1}+1)\leq\tilde{\beta}_{i}2\tilde{a}_{i}\leq 2\tilde{\beta}_{i-1}.

Then,

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\sum_{i=-1}^{k-1}\tilde{\beta}_{i}\log(1/\tilde{\alpha}_{i+1})+\sum_{i=k}^{+\infty}\tilde{\beta}_{i}\log(1/\tilde{\alpha}_{i+1})}

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\sum_{i=-1}^{k-1}\tilde{\beta}_{i}\log(1/\tilde{\alpha}_{i+1})+2\sum_{i=k}^{+\infty}\tilde{\beta}_{i-1}}\leq\textstyle{\sum_{i=-1}^{k-1}\tilde{\beta}_{i}\log(1/\tilde{\alpha}_{i+1})+8.}

This proves that
\alpha
belongs to
{\mathscr{B}}
.

On the other hand, for all
i\geq k+1
,

\tilde{a}_{i-1}\leq\log\tilde{a}_{i}\leq\log(1/\tilde{\alpha}_{i})\leq\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{i}).

Then, by an inductive argument, for all

i\geq k+1
,
h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{i-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{k}}(0)\leq\tilde{a}_{i-1}
.
Therefore, for all integers
m\geq n\geq k+1
,

h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}(0)\leq h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}(h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{k}}(0))\leq\tilde{a}_{m-1}\leq\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{m}).

This shows that
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{H}}
.
∎

By classical results of Siegel
[
Sie42
]
and Brjuno
[
Brj71
]
, if
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}
, then every germ of
holomorphic map
f(z)=e^{2\pi i\alpha}z+O(z^{2})
is locally conformally conjugate to the rotation by
2\pi\alpha

near
0
.
On the other hand, Yoccoz in
[
Yoc88
,
Yoc95b
]
proved that this condition is optimal in the quadratic family

e^{2\pi i\alpha}z+z^{2}
, i.e. if
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
then
e^{2\pi i\alpha}z+z^{2}
is not linearisable near
0
.
His approach is geometric, and avoids formidable calculations one encounters in the study of
small-divisors.
The optimality of this condition has been (re)confirmed for several classes of maps

[
PM93
,
Gey01
,
BC04
,
Oku04
,
Oku05
,
FMS18
,
Che19
]
, but in its general form for rational functions remains a
significant challenge in the field of holomorphic dynamics.
In this paper we do not rely on the optimality of this condition in any class of maps.

In
[
Her79
]
, Herman carried out a comprehensive study of the problem of linearisation of orientation-preserving
diffeomorphisms of the circle
{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
with irrational rotation number.
In particular, he presented a rather technical arithmetic condition which guaranteed the linearisation of such analytic
diffeomorphisms.
Although the linearisation problem for analytic circle diffeomorphisms close to rotations was successfully studied
earlier by Arnold
[
Arn61
]
, no progress had been made in between.
Enhancing the work of Herman, Yoccoz identified the optimal arithmetic condition
{\mathscr{H}}
for the analytic
linearisation of analytic diffeomorphisms of the circle,
[
Yoc95a
,
Yoc02
]
.
The name, Herman numbers, for the class
{\mathscr{H}}
was suggested by Yoccoz in honour of the work of Herman on this problem.
Similarly, in this paper we do not use this form of the optimality of the condition
{\mathscr{H}}
.

6.2.
Modified continued fraction, and the equivalent form of Herman numbers

Let us recall the modified continued fraction algorithm we mentioned in Section
3.1
.
For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, define
d(x,{\mathbb{Z}})=\min_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}|x-k|
. Let us fix an irrational number
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Define the numbers
\alpha_{n}\in(0,1/2)
, for
n\geq 0
, according to

(6.7)

\alpha_{0}=d(\alpha,{\mathbb{Z}}),\quad\alpha_{n+1}=d(1/\alpha_{n},{\mathbb{Z}}),

Then, there are unique integers
a_{n}
, for
n\geq-1
, and
\varepsilon_{n}\in\{+1,-1\}
, for
n\geq 0
, such that

(6.8)

\alpha=a_{-1}+\varepsilon_{0}\alpha_{0},\quad 1/\alpha_{n}=a_{n}+\varepsilon_{n+1}\alpha_{n+1}.

Evidently, for all
n\geq 0
,

(6.9)

1/\alpha_{n}\in(a_{n}-1/2,a_{n}+1/2),\quad a_{n}\geq 2,

and

(6.10)

\varepsilon_{n+1}=\begin{cases}+1&\text{if }1/\alpha_{n}\in(a_{n},a_{n}+1/2),\\
-1&\text{if }1/\alpha_{n}\in(a_{n}-1/2,a_{n}).\end{cases}

We also defined
\alpha_{-1}=+1
.

The sequences
\{a_{n}\}
and
\{\varepsilon_{n}\}
provide us with the infinite continued fraction

\alpha=a_{-1}+\cfrac{\varepsilon_{0}}{a_{0}+\cfrac{\varepsilon_{1}}{a_{1}+\cfrac{\varepsilon_{2}}{a_{2}+\dots}}}.

Consider the numbers

\beta_{-2}=\alpha,\;\beta_{-1}=+1,\;\beta_{n}=\beta_{n}(\alpha)=\textstyle{\prod_{i=0}^{n}\alpha_{i},\;\text{for }n\geq 0}.

In
[
Yoc95b
]
, Yoccoz defines the arithmetic series

(6.11)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\beta_{n-1}\log\alpha_{n}^{-1},}

and calls it the Brjuno function. This function is defined on the set of irrational numbers, and takes values in

(0,+\infty]
. One may extend
{\mathcal{B}}
onto
{\mathbb{Q}}
, by setting
{\mathcal{B}}(p/q)=+\infty
, for all
p/q\in{\mathbb{Q}}
.

The Brjuno function satisfies the remarkable relations

(6.12)

\begin{gathered}{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)={\mathcal{B}}(\alpha+1)={\mathcal{B}}(-\alpha),\;\text{for }\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}},\\
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)=\alpha{\mathcal{B}}(1/\alpha)+\log(1/\alpha),\;\text{for }\alpha\in(0,1/2).\end{gathered}

These show that one may think of the Brjuno function as a
\mathrm{PGL}(2,{\mathbb{Z}})
-cocycle.
This point of view drives some of the technical arguments we present later in the paper,
notably in Section
8.5
.
See
[
MMY97
,
MMY01
]
for a systematic approach to employing this mechanism.

One may formally define the arithmetic classes of
{\mathscr{B}}
and
{\mathscr{H}}
using the modified continued fraction and the
modified function
{\mathcal{B}}
in the same fashion. The following two propositions guarantee that through this
we identify the same classes of irrational numbers.

Proposition 6.4
.

For all
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
we have
|{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)-\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\alpha)|\leq 29
.
In particular,
\alpha
is a Brjuno number if and only if
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)<+\infty
.

In
[
MMY97
]
, the authors go very far in this direction, by showing the remarkable property that
the difference
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha)-\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\alpha)
extends to a
1/2
-holder continuous function over all of
\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition 6.5
.

An irrational number
\alpha
is a Herman number if and only if for all
n\geq 0
there is
m\geq n
such that

h_{\alpha_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0)\geq{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m}).

Although the criterion in the above proposition appears identical to the one given in
Definition
6.2
, the value of
m
for a given
n
may be different.
As before, here
h_{\alpha_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}
is understood as the identity map when
m=n
,
and as
h_{\alpha_{n}}
when
m=n+1
.

The remaining of this section is devoted to the proof of the above two propositions.
The main reason here is that the sequences
\{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}\}
and
\{\alpha_{n}\}
are closely related, and it is possible to
identify one from the other using an algorithm.

Let us define the sequence

\textstyle{c(-1)=-1,\;c(n)=-1+\sum_{i=0}^{n}(3-\varepsilon_{i})/2,\;\text{for }n\geq 0}.

That is,
c(n)
is obtained from
c(n-1)
by adding
+2
if
\varepsilon_{n}=-1
or adding
+1
if
\varepsilon_{n}=+1
.
Clearly,
n\mapsto c(n)
is strictly monotone with
c(n)\to+\infty
as
n\to+\infty
.
For more general properties of
c(n)
, and its dependence on the choice of the continued fraction, one may refer to
[
MMY97
]
(where
c(n)
is denoted as
k^{1/2}(n)
).

Lemma 6.6
.

For all
n\geq-1
, the following hold:

(i)

if
\varepsilon_{n+1}=-1
,
\alpha_{n+1}=1-\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}
,
and if
\varepsilon_{n+1}=+1
,
\alpha_{n+1}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}
;

(ii)

\alpha_{n+1}=\prod_{c(n)+1}^{c(n+1)}\tilde{\alpha}_{i}
;

(iii)

\tilde{\beta}_{c(n)}=\beta_{n}
;

(iv)

{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1})={\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{n+1})
.

Proof.

We prove (i) by induction on
n
.
We start with
n=-1
. If
\varepsilon_{0}=+1
,
\alpha_{0}=\tilde{\alpha}_{0}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(-1)+1}
.
If
\varepsilon_{0}=-1
,
\alpha_{0}=1-\tilde{\alpha}_{0}=1-\tilde{\alpha}_{c(-1)+1}
.
Now assume that the assertion in (i) is true for
n-1
. To prove it for
n
, we consider two cases:

First assume that
\varepsilon_{n}=+1
. By the induction hypothesis for
n-1
,
\alpha_{n}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n-1)+1}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)}
.
Hence,
1/\alpha_{n}=1/\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)}
.
Now, if
\varepsilon_{n+1}=+1
,
1/\alpha_{n}=1/\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)}
leads to
\alpha_{n+1}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}
.
If
\varepsilon_{n+1}=-1
,
1/\alpha_{n}=1/\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)}
leads to
\alpha_{n+1}=1-\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}
.

Now assume that
\varepsilon_{n}=-1
. By the induction hypothesis,
\alpha_{n}=1-\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n-1)+1}=1-\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)-1}
.
As
\alpha_{n}\in(0,1/2)
,
\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)-1}\in(1/2,1)
.
Then,
\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)}=1/\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)-1}-1=1/(1-\alpha_{n})-1=\alpha_{n}/(1-\alpha_{n})
.
Hence,
1/\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)}=1/\alpha_{n}-1
.
Now, if
\varepsilon_{n+1}=+1
,
1/\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)}=1/\alpha_{n}-1
leads to
\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}=\alpha_{n+1}
.
If
\varepsilon_{n+1}=-1
,
1/\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)}=1/\alpha_{n}-1
leads to
1-\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}=\alpha_{n+1}
.

Part (ii): If
\varepsilon_{n+1}=+1
, by Part (i),
\alpha_{n+1}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n+1)}
.
If
\varepsilon_{n+1}=-1
, by Part (i),
\alpha_{n+1}=1-\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}
. As

\alpha_{n+1}\in(0,1/2)
we conclude that
\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}\in(1/2,1)
, which implies that

\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+2}=1/\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}-1
.
Therefore,

\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n+1)}\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+2}\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}=1-\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}=\alpha_{n+1}.

Part (iii): By the formula in Part (ii), and the definition of
c(n)
,

\textstyle{\beta_{n}=\prod_{m=0}^{n}\alpha_{m}=\prod_{m=0}^{n}\Big(\prod_{i=c(m-1)+1}^{c(m)}\tilde{\alpha}_{i}\Big)=\prod_{i=0}^{c(n)}\tilde{\alpha}_{i}=\tilde{\beta}_{c(n)}.}

Part (iv): If
\varepsilon_{n+1}=+1
, by Part (i),
\alpha_{n+1}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}
, and hence

{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1})={\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{n+1})
.
If
\varepsilon_{n+1}=-1
, by Part (i),
\alpha_{n+1}=1-\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}
. Using Equation (
6.12
),

{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{n+1})={\mathcal{B}}(1-\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1})={\mathcal{B}}(-\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1})={\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n)+1}).\qed

Proof of Proposition
6.4
.

Fix
n\geq 0
.
If
\varepsilon_{n}=+1
then
c(n)=c(n-1)+1
, and by Lemma
6.6
-(iii),

(6.13)

\beta_{n-1}\log\beta_{n}=\tilde{\beta}_{c(n-1)}\log\tilde{\beta}_{c(n-1)+1}=\tilde{\beta}_{c(n)-1}\log\tilde{\beta}_{c(n)}.

If
\varepsilon_{n}=-1
then
c(n)=c(n-1)+2
, and by Lemma
6.6
-(iii),

\tilde{\beta}_{c(n)-1}=\tilde{\beta}_{c(n-1)+1}=\tilde{\beta}_{c(n-1)}\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n-1)+1}=\beta_{n-1}(1-\alpha_{n})=\beta_{n-1}-\beta_{n}.

and therefore

(6.14)

\displaystyle\big(\tilde{\beta}_{c(n-1)}\log\tilde{\beta}_{c(n-1)+1}

\displaystyle+\tilde{\beta}_{c(n)-1}\log\tilde{\beta}_{c(n)}\big)-\beta_{n-1}\log\beta_{n}

\displaystyle=\big(\beta_{n-1}\log(\beta_{n-1}-\beta_{n})+(\beta_{n-1}-\beta_{n})\log\beta_{n}\big)-\beta_{n-1}\log\beta_{n}

\displaystyle=\beta_{n-1}\log(\beta_{n-1}-\beta_{n})-\beta_{n}\log\beta_{n}

Combing (
6.13
) and (
6.14
),
and using
\beta_{n-1}-\beta_{n}=\beta_{n-1}(1-\alpha_{n})
, we conclude that for all
m\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle{\sum_{i=0}^{c(m)}\tilde{\beta}_{i-1}\log\tilde{\beta}_{i}}

\displaystyle-\textstyle{\sum_{n=0}^{m}\beta_{n-1}\log\beta_{n}}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\sum_{n=0}^{m}\left(\sum_{i=c(n-1)+1}^{c(n)}(\tilde{\beta}_{i-1}\log\tilde{\beta}_{i})-\beta_{n-1}\log\beta_{n}\right)}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\sum_{n=0\,;\,\varepsilon_{n}=-1}^{m}\left(\beta_{n-1}\log\beta_{n-1}+\beta_{n-1}\log(1-\alpha_{n})-\beta_{n}\log\beta_{n}\right).}

On the other hand,

\textstyle{\sum_{n=0}^{m}\beta_{n-1}\log(1/\alpha_{n})+\sum_{n=0}^{m}\beta_{n-1}\log\beta_{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{m}\beta_{n-1}\log\beta_{n-1},}

and similarly,

\textstyle{-\sum_{i=0}^{c(m)}\tilde{\beta}_{i-1}\log\tilde{\beta}_{i}-\sum_{i=0}^{c(m)}\tilde{\beta}_{i-1}\log(1/\tilde{\alpha}_{i})=-\sum_{i=0}^{c(m)}\tilde{\beta}_{i-1}\log\tilde{\beta}_{i-1}.}

Recall that
\tilde{\beta}_{-1}=\beta_{-1}=1
. Adding the above three equations, we conclude that

\displaystyle\textstyle{\left|\sum_{n=0}^{m}\beta_{n-1}\log(1/\alpha_{n})\right.}

\displaystyle{\textstyle\displaystyle\left.-\sum_{i=0}^{c(m)}\tilde{\beta}_{i-1}\log(1/\tilde{\alpha}_{i})\right|}

\displaystyle{\textstyle\displaystyle\leq 3\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}|\beta_{n}\log\beta_{n}|+\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}|\beta_{n-1}\log(1-\alpha_{n})|}+\textstyle{\sum_{i=0}^{+\infty}|\tilde{\beta}_{i}\log\tilde{\beta}_{i}|}

Since
|x\log x|\leq 2\sqrt{x}
for
x\in(0,1)
,

(6.15)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}|\tilde{\beta}_{n}\log\tilde{\beta}_{n}|}\textstyle{\leq 2\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}(\tilde{\beta}_{n})^{1/2}}

\displaystyle{\textstyle\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}(\tilde{\beta}_{2n})^{1/2}+2\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}(\tilde{\beta}_{2n+1})^{1/2}}

\displaystyle{\textstyle\displaystyle\leq 2(\tilde{\beta}_{0})^{1/2}\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}2^{-n/2}+2\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}2^{-n/2}}

\displaystyle\leq 6+4\cdot 2^{1/2}.

On the other hand,
\beta_{n}\leq 2^{-n-1}
, for all
n\geq 0
.
Using
|x\log x|\geq 2\sqrt{x}
, for
x\in(0,1)
,
\alpha_{j}\in(0,1/2)
, we obtain

\textstyle{\sum_{n=0}^{k}|\beta_{n}\log\beta_{n}|\leq 2\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\sqrt{\beta_{n}}\leq 2\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}1/2^{(n+1)/2}=2+2\sqrt{2},}

and

\textstyle{\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}|\beta_{n-1}\log(1-\alpha_{n})|\leq\log 2\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\beta_{n-1}=2\log 2.}

This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

We have,

(i)

for all
r\in(0,1)
,

h_{r}({\mathcal{B}}(r))\geq{\mathcal{B}}(1/r)+1,\quad h_{r}(\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(r))\geq\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(1/r)+1.

(ii)

if there are
m\geq n\geq 0
satisfying

h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}(0)\geq\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{m})
,
then

\lim_{m\to+\infty}h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}(0)-\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{m})=+\infty.

(iii)

if there are
m\geq n\geq 0
satisfying

h_{\alpha_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0)\geq{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})
, then

\lim_{m\to+\infty}h_{\alpha_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0)-{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})=+\infty.

Proof.

Note that for all
r\in(0,1)
and all
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

h_{r}(y)\geq r^{-1}y+r^{-1}\log r+1.

If
y\geq\log r^{-1}
,
h_{r}(y)=r^{-1}y+r^{-1}\log r+r^{-1}\geq r^{-1}y+r^{-1}\log r+1
.
Using the inequality
x\geq 1+\log x
, for
x>0
, we note that

re^{y}\geq 1+\log(re^{y})=y+\log r+1\geq y+\log r+r
.
This implies the above inequality for
y<\log r^{-1}
.

By the above inequality, as well as (
6.5
) and (
6.12
), we obtain

h_{r}({\mathcal{B}}(r))\geq r^{-1}{\mathcal{B}}(r)+\log r+1={\mathcal{B}}(1/r)+1,\quad h_{r}(\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(r))\geq r^{-1}\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(r)+\log r+1=\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(1/r)+1.

If the inequalities in (ii) and (iii) hold, we may use the inequality in (i) and
h_{r}(y+1)\geq h_{r}(y)+1
in
Equation (
6.6
), to obtain

h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{m+j-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}(0)\geq\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{m+j})+j,\quad h_{\alpha_{m+j-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0)\geq{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+j})+j.\qed

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
r_{1}\in(1/2,1)
,
r_{2}=1/r_{1}-1\in(0,1)
, and
r=r_{1}r_{2}\in(0,1/2)
. Then, for all
y\geq e^{2}
we have

\big|h^{-1}_{r}(y)-h^{-1}_{r_{1}}\circ h^{-1}_{r_{2}}(y)\big|\leq 1+e^{-1}.

Proof.

The inverse map
h_{r}^{-1}:(0,+\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}
is given by the formula

h_{r}^{-1}(y)=\begin{cases}ry+\log r^{-1}-1&\text{if }y\geq 1/r,\\
\log y&\text{if }0<y\leq 1/r.\end{cases}

Since
y\geq e^{2}
, one can see that
h_{r_{2}}^{-1}(y)\geq 1+r_{2}=1/r_{1}
. Thus,

h^{-1}_{r_{1}}\circ h^{-1}_{r_{2}}(y)=r_{1}h_{r_{2}}^{-1}(y)+\log r_{1}^{-1}-1
.
Using
r_{2}=1/r_{1}-1
and the elementary inequality
|x\log x|\leq 1/e
, for
x\in(0,1)
, we have

(1-r_{1})\log(1/r_{2})=(r_{2}/(1+r_{2}))\log r^{-1}_{2}\leq e^{-1}/(1+r_{2})\leq e^{-1}.

We consider three cases:

(1)
y\leq 1/r_{2}
:
Since
1/r_{2}\leq 1/r
, we get

\displaystyle\big|h^{-1}_{r}(y)-h^{-1}_{r_{1}}\circ h^{-1}_{r_{2}}(y)\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\log y-(r_{1}\log y+\log r_{1}^{-1}-1)\big|

\displaystyle\leq(1-r_{1})\log y+\big|1-\log r^{-1}_{1}\big|

\displaystyle\leq(1-r_{1})\log r_{2}^{-1}+\big|1-\log r^{-1}_{1}\big|\leq e^{-1}+1.

(2)
1/r_{2}\leq y\leq 1/r
:
Then,

\displaystyle h^{-1}_{r}(y)-h^{-1}_{r_{1}}\circ h^{-1}_{r_{2}}(y)

\displaystyle=\log y-(r_{1}(r_{2}y+\log r_{2}^{-1}-1)+\log r_{1}^{-1}-1)

\displaystyle=\log(yr_{1})-r_{1}\log r_{2}^{-1}+r_{1}(1-r_{2}y)+1.

On the other hand, we have

-1/2\leq r_{1}-1\leq r_{1}-ry=r_{1}-r_{1}r_{2}y=r_{1}(1-r_{2}y)\leq 0,

and, using
yr_{1}r_{2}=yr\leq 1
, we get

\log yr_{1}-r_{1}\log r_{2}^{-1}\leq\log r^{-1}_{2}-r_{1}\log r_{2}^{-1}=(1-r_{1})\log r_{2}^{-1}\leq e^{-1},

and, using
y\geq 1/r_{2}
,

\log yr_{1}-r_{1}\log r_{2}^{-1}\geq\log(r_{1}/r_{2})-r_{1}\log r_{2}^{-1}=\log r_{1}+(1-r_{1})\log r_{2}^{-1}\geq\log r_{1}\geq-\log 2.

Combining the above inequalities we get

-1-e^{-1}\leq 1-1/2-\log 2\leq h^{-1}_{r}(y)-h^{-1}_{r_{1}}\circ h^{-1}_{r_{2}}(y)\leq 1+e^{-1}.

(3)
y\geq 1/r
:
Using
r_{1}r_{2}=r
, we get

\displaystyle\big|h^{-1}_{r}(y)-h^{-1}_{r_{1}}\circ h^{-1}_{r_{2}}(y)\big|

\displaystyle=\big|ry+\log r^{-1}-1-(r_{1}(r_{2}y+\log r_{2}^{-1}-1)+\log r_{1}^{-1}-1)\big|

\displaystyle=(1-r_{1})\log r_{2}^{-1}+r_{1}\leq e^{-1}+1.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
m>n\geq 0
and
y\geq e^{2}
. Assume that at least one of the following holds:

(i)

h^{-1}_{\alpha_{n}}\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{n+1}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{m}}(y)
is defined and is at least
e^{2}
,

(ii)

h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n-1)+1}}\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n-1)+2}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}(y)

is defined and is at least
e^{2}
.

Then,

\big|h^{-1}_{\alpha_{n}}\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{n+1}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{m}}(y)-h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n-1)+1}}\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n-1)+2}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}(y)\big|\leq 2(1+e^{-1}).

In the above lemma, it is part of the conclusion that if one of the compositions in items (i) and (ii) is defined and
is at least
e^{2}
, then the composition in the other item is defined as well.

Proof.

First assume that item (i) holds.
By Equation (
6.6
),
h_{r}(t)\geq t
for all
t\geq 0
and
r\in(0,1)
.
This implies that for all
j
with
n\leq j\leq m
,

h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j}}\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j+1}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{m}}(y)\geq e^{2}
.
Let

y_{j}=h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j}}\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j+1}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{m}}(y),\;\text{for }n\leq j\leq m.

By an inductive argument, we show that for all
j
with
n\leq j\leq m
,

\tilde{y}_{j}=h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j-1)+1}}\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(n-1)+2}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}(y),

is defined, and satisfies

|y_{j}-\tilde{y}_{j}|\leq 2(1+e^{-1}).

We start with
j=m
.
If
\varepsilon_{m}=+1
,
c(m-1)+1=c(m)
, and since
y\geq e^{2}>0
,
\tilde{y}_{m}=h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}(y)
is
defined. Moreover, by Lemma
6.6
,
\alpha_{m}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m-1)+1}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}
, and hence,

y_{m}=\tilde{y}_{m}
.

If
\varepsilon_{m}=-1
,
c(m-1)+1=c(m)-1
. Since
y\geq e^{2}>1
,

h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}(y)>h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}(1)=0
, and hence

\tilde{y}_{m}=h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m-1)+1}}\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}(y)
is defined.
Moreover, by Lemma
6.6
, we have
\alpha_{m}=1-\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m-1)+1}
and

\alpha_{m}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m-1)+1}\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}
.
As
\alpha_{m}\in(0,1/2)
,
\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m-1)+1}\in(1/2,1)
, and hence

\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m-1)+2}=1/\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m-1)+1}-1
.
We use Lemma
6.8
with
r=\alpha_{m}
,
r_{1}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m-1)+1}
and
r_{2}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}

to conclude that

|y_{m}-\tilde{y}_{m}|=|h_{\alpha_{m}}^{-1}(y)-h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c}(m-1)+1}\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}(y)|\leq 1+e^{-1}
.

Now assume that the assertion holds for
j+1
, with
n<j+1\leq m
. To prove it for
j
we consider two cases.

If
\varepsilon_{j}=+1
,
c(j-1)+1=c(j)
. By the induction hypothesis for
j+1
,
|y_{j+1}-\tilde{y}_{j+1}|\leq 2(1+e^{-1})
.
Combining with
y_{j+1}\geq e^{2}
, we obtain
\tilde{y}_{j+1}\geq e^{2}-2(1+e^{-1})>4
. This implies that

\tilde{y}_{j}=h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)}}(\tilde{y}_{j+1})
is defined.
On the other hand, by Lemma
6.6
,
\alpha_{j}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j-1)+1}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)}
.
Moreover, for all
t\geq 4
and all
r\in(0,1)
,
(h_{r}^{-1})^{\prime}(t)\leq 1/2
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle|y_{j}-\tilde{y}_{j}|=|h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j}}(y_{j+1})-h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)}}(\tilde{y}_{j+1})|

\displaystyle=|h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j}}(y_{j+1})-h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j}}(\tilde{y}_{j+1})|

\displaystyle\leq(1/2)|y_{j+1}-\tilde{y}_{j+1}|\leq(1/2)2(1+e^{-1})\leq 2(1+e^{-1}).

If
\varepsilon_{j}=-1
,
c(j-1)+1=c(j)-1
. As in the previous case, the induction hypothesis for
j+1
gives us

|y_{j+1}-\tilde{y}_{j+1}|\leq 2(1+e^{-1})
.
Combining with
y_{j+1}\geq e^{2}
we obtain
\tilde{y}_{j+1}\geq e^{2}-2(1+e^{-1})>4
.
This implies that
\tilde{y}_{j}=h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)-1}}\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)}}(\tilde{y}_{j+1})
is defined.

On the other hand, by Lemma
6.6
,
\alpha_{j}=1-\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j-1)+1}
, and

\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j-1)+2}=1/\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j-1)+1}-1
.
Using Lemma
6.8
with
r=\alpha_{j}
,
r_{1}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j-1)+1}
and
r_{2}=\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)}

we get

|h_{\alpha_{j}}(y_{j})-h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)-1}}\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)}}(y_{j})|\leq 1+e^{-1}.

By elementary calculations one may see that for all
t\geq 4
,

\big(h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)-1}}\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)}}\big)^{\prime}(t)\leq 1/2
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle|y_{j}-\tilde{y}_{j}|

\displaystyle=|h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j}}(y_{j+1})-h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)-1}}\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)}}(\tilde{y}_{j+1})|

\displaystyle\leq|h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j}}(y_{j+1})-h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)-1}}\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)}}(y_{j+1})|

\displaystyle\quad\quad+|h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)-1}}\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)}}(y_{j+1})-h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)-1}}\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j)}}(\tilde{y}_{j+1})|

\displaystyle\leq(1+e^{-1})+(1/2)\cdot|y_{j+1}-\tilde{y}_{j+1}|

\displaystyle\leq(1+e^{-1})+(1/2)\cdot 2(1+e^{-1})=2(1+e^{-1}).

This completes the proof of the induction.

The proof of the lemma when item (ii) holds is similar, indeed easier, and is left to the reader.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.5
.

Let
\exp^{\circ i}
denote the
i
-fold composition of the exponential map
x\mapsto e^{x}
.

Assume that
\alpha
satisfies the criterion in Proposition
6.5
.
Fix an arbitrary
n\geq 0
.
Let
n^{\prime}
be an integer with

(6.16)

n^{\prime}\geq n+\exp^{\circ 2}(2)+2(1+e^{-1}).

Applying the criterion in Proposition
6.5
with
n^{\prime}
, there is
m^{\prime}\geq n^{\prime}
such that

h_{\alpha_{m^{\prime}-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n^{\prime}}}(0)\geq{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m^{\prime}}).

By Lemma
6.7
-(iii), there is
m\geq n^{\prime}
such that

h_{\alpha_{m}}\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n^{\prime}}}(0)\geq{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1})+29.

We are going to show that the pair
c(m)+1,n
satisfies the inequality in Definition
6.2
.

By Proposition
6.4
and Lemma
6.6
-(iv),

{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1})+29={\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1})+29\geq\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1}).

Combining with the previous inequality, we obtain

h_{\alpha_{m}}\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n^{\prime}}}(0)\geq\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1}).

This implies that there is an integer
j
, with
n^{\prime}\leq j\leq m
such that

(6.17)

h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{m}}(\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1}))\leq 0.

Now we consider two cases based on the size of
\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1})
.

First assume that
\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1})<e^{2}
.
Using
h_{r}(y)\geq y+1
several times, we obtain

e^{2}\leq n^{\prime}-n\leq m-n\leq c(m)-n\leq h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}(0).

Thus, we have the desired inequality

\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1})\leq h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}(0)
.

Now assume that
\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1})\geq e^{2}
.
Combining with Equation (
6.17
), there is an integer
j^{\prime}
, with
j\leq j^{\prime}\leq m
, such that

h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j^{\prime}}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{m}}(\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1}))\in[e^{2},\exp^{\circ 1}(e^{2})].

This is because any such orbit must pass through the interval
[e^{2},\exp^{\circ 1}(e^{2})]
.
We may now apply Lemma
6.9
, to conclude that

h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j^{\prime}-1)+1}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}(\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1}))

is defined and

\displaystyle h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j^{\prime}-1)+1}}\circ\dots\circ

\displaystyle h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}(\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1}))

\displaystyle\leq h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j^{\prime}-1)+1}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}(\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1}))-h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j}^{\prime}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{m}}(\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1}))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j^{\prime}}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{m}}(\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1}))

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+e^{-1})+\exp^{\circ 1}(e^{2})=2(1+e^{-1})+\exp^{\circ 2}(2).

On the other hand, for all
j^{\prime}\geq 0
,
j^{\prime}\leq c(j^{\prime})
.
Then, combining with Equation (
6.16
), we have

c(j^{\prime}-1)-n+1\geq j^{\prime}-1-n+1\geq j-n\geq n^{\prime}-n\geq 2(1+e^{-1})+\exp^{\circ 2}(2).

Now, using
h_{r}(y)\geq y+1
several times, we obtain

2(1+e^{-1})+\exp^{\circ 2}(2)\leq c(j^{\prime}-1)-n+1\leq h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j^{\prime}-1)}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}(0).

Combining the above inequalities we obtain

h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j^{\prime}-1)+1}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}(\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1}))\leq h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j^{\prime}-1)}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}(0),

which gives the desired relation

\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1})\leq h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}}(0).

This completes the proof of one side of the proposition.

The other side of the proposition may be proved along the same lines, but there are some technical
differences.

Assume that
\alpha
satisfies Definition
6.2
.
Fix an arbitrary
n\geq 0
.
Let
n^{\prime}
be an integer with

(6.18)

n^{\prime}\geq 2n+2\exp^{\circ 3}(2)+4(1+e^{-1}).

Applying Definition
6.2
with
n^{\prime}
, there is
m^{\prime}\geq n^{\prime}
such that

h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{m^{\prime}-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n^{\prime}}}(0)\geq\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{m^{\prime}}).

Recall that
c(m)\to+\infty
as
m\to+\infty
. By Lemma
6.7
-(ii), there is
m\geq n^{\prime}
such that

h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n^{\prime}}}(0)\geq\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1})+29.

Note that
c(m)\geq m\geq n^{\prime}
.
We are going to show that the pair
m+1,n
satisfies the inequality in Proposition
6.5
.

By Proposition
6.4
and Lemma
6.6
-(iv),

\tilde{{\mathcal{B}}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1})+29\geq{\mathcal{B}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)+1})={\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1}).

Combining with the previous inequality, we obtain

h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\tilde{\alpha}_{n^{\prime}}}(0)\geq{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1}).

This implies that there is an integer
j
, with
n^{\prime}\leq j\leq c(m)
such that

(6.19)

h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{j}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1}))\leq 0.

Now we consider two cases based on the size of
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1})
.

First assume that
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1})<\exp^{\circ 3}(2)
.
Using
h_{r}(y)\geq y+1
several times, we obtain

\exp^{\circ 3}(2)\leq n^{\prime}-n\leq m-n\leq h_{\alpha_{m}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0).

Thus, we have the desired inequality
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1})\leq h_{\alpha_{m}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0)
.

Now assume that
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1})\geq\exp^{\circ 3}(2)=\exp^{\circ 2}(e^{2})
.
Combining with Equation (
6.19
), there is an integer
j^{\prime}
, with
j\leq c(j^{\prime})-1\leq c(m)
, such that

h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j^{\prime})-1}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1}))\in[e^{2},\exp^{\circ 2}(e^{2})].

This is because any such orbit has two consecutive elements in the interval
[e^{2},\exp^{\circ 3}(2)]
,
and the image of the map
i\mapsto c(i)
covers at least one element of any pair of consecutive integers.
We may now apply Lemma
6.9
, to conclude that

h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j^{\prime}}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{m}}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1}))
is defined and

\displaystyle h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j^{\prime}}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{m}}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1}))

\displaystyle\leq h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j^{\prime}}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{m}}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1}))-h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j^{\prime})-1}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1}))

\displaystyle\qquad+h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(j^{\prime})-1}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\tilde{\alpha}_{c(m)}}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1}))

\displaystyle\leq 2(1+e^{-1})+\exp^{\circ 3}(2).

On the other hand, for all
j^{\prime}\geq 0
,
1+2j^{\prime}\geq c(j^{\prime})
.
Then, combining with our choice of
n^{\prime}
in Equation (
6.18
), we have

j^{\prime}-n\geq(c(j^{\prime})-1)/2-n\geq j/2-n\geq n^{\prime}/2-n\geq\exp^{\circ 3}(2)+2(1+e^{-1}).

Now, using
h_{r}(y)\geq y+1
several times, we obtain

2(1+e^{-1})+\exp^{\circ 3}(2)\leq j^{\prime}-n\leq h_{\alpha_{j^{\prime}-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0).

Combining the above inequalities we obtain

h^{-1}_{\alpha_{j^{\prime}}}\circ\dots\circ h^{-1}_{\alpha_{m}}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1}))\leq h_{\alpha_{j^{\prime}-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0),

which gives the desired relation
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m+1})\leq h_{\alpha_{m}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0)
.
This completes the proof of the other side of the proposition.
∎

7.
Elementary properties of the change of coordinates

In this section we establish some basic properties of the changes of coordinates
Y_{r}
introduced in
Section
2
.
The important properties are the relation between
Y_{r}
and the function
h_{r}
employed in the definition of the
Herman numbers in Section
6
, and the relation between
Y_{r}
and the functional relation
for the Brjuno function in Equation (
6.12
).
In Proposition
7.1
we show that a suitable rescaling of the restriction of
Y_{r}
to the

y
-axis is uniformly close to
h_{r}
.
In Lemma
7.2
we relate the behaviour of
Y_{r}
to the main functional relation
for the Brjuno function.
In Lemma
7.3
we list some geometric properties of the mapping
Y_{r}:\mathbb{H}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
.

Recall from Section
6.1
that
h_{r}
is a diffeomorphism from
{\mathbb{R}}
onto
(0,+\infty)
.

Proposition 7.1
.

For all
r\in(0,1/2]
and all
y\geq 1
,

|2\pi\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy/(2\pi))-h_{r}^{-1}(y)|\leq\pi.

Proof.

The map
h_{r}^{-1}:(0,+\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}
is given by the formula

h_{r}^{-1}(y)=\begin{cases}ry+\log r^{-1}-1&\text{if }y\geq 1/r,\\
\log y&\text{if }0<y\leq 1/r.\end{cases}

We first give some basic estimates needed for the proof.
For all
t\in[0,1]
we have
t\leq e^{t}-1\leq et
, for all
t\in[0,3\pi/2]
we have
1-e^{-t}\leq t
, and for
all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
we have
|1-e^{it}|\leq|t|
.
Then, for all
r\in(0,1/2]
, we have

(7.1)

|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}|\leq|e^{-3\pi r}-1|+|1-e^{\pi ri}|\leq 3\pi r+\pi r=4\pi r,

and

(7.2)

|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}|\geq|\operatorname{Im}(e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri})|=\sin(\pi r)\geq\pi r/2.

Now we consider two cases. First assume that
1\leq y\leq 1/r
. We note that

\displaystyle\big|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry}\big|

\displaystyle\leq|e^{-3\pi r}-1|+|1-e^{-\pi ri}|+|e^{-\pi ri}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry}|

\displaystyle\leq 3\pi r+\pi r+ery=4\pi r+ery,

and

\big|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry}\big|\geq|e^{\pi ri}e^{ry}|-|e^{-3\pi r}|\geq e^{ry}-1\geq ry.

These imply that

\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry}}{y(e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri})}\Big|\leq\frac{4\pi r+ery}{y\pi r/2}\leq\frac{4\pi/y+e}{\pi/2}\leq\frac{4\pi+e}{\pi/2}\leq e^{\pi},

and

\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry}}{y(e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri})}\Big|\geq\frac{ry}{y(4\pi r)}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\geq e^{-\pi}.

For
1\leq y\leq 1/r
, we have

2\pi\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy/(2\pi))-h_{r}^{-1}(y)=\log\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\Big|-\log y=\log\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry}}{y(e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri})}\Big|.

Combining these, one obtains the inequality in the proposition when
1\leq y\leq 1/r
.

Now assume that
y\geq 1/r
.
First note that

|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry}|\leq|e^{-3\pi r}|+|e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry}|\leq 1+e^{ry}\leq 2e^{ry},

and

|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry}|\geq|e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry}|-|e^{-3\pi r}|\geq e^{ry}-1\geq e^{ry}/2.

For
y\geq 1/r
, we have

\displaystyle 2\pi\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy/(2\pi))-h_{r}^{-1}(y)

\displaystyle=\log\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\Big|-ry-\log r^{-1}+1

\displaystyle=\log\Big|\frac{r(e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry})}{e^{ry}(e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri})}\Big|+1

\displaystyle=\log\Big|\frac{r}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\Big|+\log\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{ry}}{e^{ry}}\Big|+1.

Using
\log(2/\pi)+\log 2+1\leq\pi
and
-\log(4\pi)-\log 2+1\geq-\pi
, these imply the desired inequality in
the proposition when
y\geq 1/r
.
∎

Compare the item (iii) in the following lemma to the second functional equation in
(
6.12
).

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
r\in(0,1/2]
. We have

(i)

for all
y\geq 0
and all
x_{0}\in[0,1/r]
,

\max_{x\in x_{0}+{\mathbb{Z}}}\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(x+iy)=\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(x^{\prime}+iy),

where
x^{\prime}\in(x_{0}+{\mathbb{Z}})\cap[1/(2r)-1,1/(2r)]
;

(ii)

for all
y\geq 0
, and all
x\in[1/(2r)-1,1/(2r)]
we have

2\pi ry+\log(1/r)-4\leq 2\pi\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(x+iy)\leq 2\pi ry+\log(1/r)+2.

Proof.

(i): Note that

\displaystyle\max_{x\in x_{0}+{\mathbb{Z}}}\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(x+iy)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\max_{x\in x_{0}+{\mathbb{Z}}}\log\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi rix}e^{2\pi ry}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\Big|.

The maximum of
|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi ri(x_{0}+k)}e^{2\pi ry}|
occurs when

e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi ri(x_{0}+k)}
is closest to the negative real axis.
This happens when
|\pi ri+2\pi ri(x_{0}+k)-\pi i|\leq\pi r
, which implies that
|(x_{0}+k)-(1/(2r)-1/2)|\leq 1/2
.

(ii): For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\displaystyle 2\pi\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(x+iy)-2\pi ry-\log\frac{1}{r}

\displaystyle=\log\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi rix}e^{2\pi ry}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\cdot re^{-2\pi ry}\Big|.

Assume that
2\pi ry\geq 1
. By Equation (
7.2
),

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi rix}e^{2\pi ry}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\cdot re^{-2\pi ry}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|e^{-3\pi r}|+|e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi rix}e^{2\pi ry}|}{|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}|}\cdot re^{-2\pi ry}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2e^{2\pi ry}}{\pi r/2}\cdot re^{-2\pi ry}=\frac{4}{\pi}\leq e^{2}.

On the other hand, by Equation (
7.1
),

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi rix}e^{2\pi ry}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\cdot re^{-2\pi ry}\Big|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{|e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi rix}e^{2\pi ry}|-|e^{-3\pi r}|}{|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}|}\cdot re^{-2\pi ry}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{e^{2\pi ry}-1}{|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}|}\cdot re^{-2\pi ry}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{e^{2\pi ry}/2}{4\pi r}\cdot re^{-2\pi ry}=\frac{1}{8\pi}\geq e^{-4}.

Now assume that
0\leq 2\pi ry\leq 1
.
For all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, by Equation (
7.2
),

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi rix}e^{2\pi ry}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\cdot re^{-2\pi ry}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}+e^{2\pi ry}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\Big|\cdot re^{-2\pi ry}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3\pi r+e2\pi ry}{\pi r/2}\cdot re^{-2\pi ry}

\displaystyle=(6r+e4ry)e^{-2\pi ry}.

Hence, using
y\leq 1/(2\pi r)
and
r\in(0,1/2]
, we obtain

(6r+e4ry)e^{-2\pi ry}\leq(3+\frac{2e}{\pi})e^{-2\pi ry}\leq 3+\frac{2e}{\pi}\leq e^{2}.

On the other hand, for
x\in[1/(2r)-1,1/(2r)]
,
we have

|e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi rix}e^{2\pi ry}|\geq e^{2\pi ry}.

Hence, by Equation (
7.1
),

\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi rix}e^{2\pi ry}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\cdot re^{-2\pi ry}\Big|\geq\frac{e^{2\pi ry}}{4\pi r}\cdot re^{-2\pi ry}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\geq e^{-4}.

These imply the desired inequality in part (iii).
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

For all
r\in(0,1/2]
, we have

(i)

for all
x\in[0,1/r]
and all
y\geq-1
,

\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(x+iy)\geq\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy)-1/(2\pi);

(ii)

for all
x\in[0,1/r]
and all
y_{1}\geq y_{2}\geq-1
,

\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(x+iy_{1})-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(x+iy_{2})\leq\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{1})-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{2})+1/(2\pi);

(iii)

for all
y_{1}\geq y_{2}\geq-1
and
y\geq 0
,

\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy+iy_{1})-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy+iy_{2})\leq\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{1})-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{2})+1/(4\pi);

(iv)

for all
y_{1}\geq y_{2}\geq-1
and
y\in[0,5/\pi]
,

\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{1})-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{2})\leq\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy+iy_{1})-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy+iy_{2})+5/\pi.

Proof.

Part (i): Recall from the proof of Lemma
2.2
that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial Y_{r}}{\partial s}(w)=\frac{\partial Y_{r}}{\partial w}(w)+\frac{\partial Y_{r}}{\partial\overline{w}}(w)=r+\frac{ir\operatorname{Im}\xi}{|\xi-1|^{2}},

where
w=s+iy
and
\xi=e^{-3\pi r}e^{\pi ri}e^{2\pi riw}
.
We note that when
s\in[0,1/(2r)-1/2]\cup[1/r-1/2,1/r]
,
\operatorname{Im}\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)/\partial s\geq 0
,
and when
s\in[1/(2r)-1/2,1/r-1/2]
,
\operatorname{Im}\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)/\partial s\leq 0
.
Moreover, when
s\in[1/r-1,1/r-1/2]
, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}}{\partial s}(s+iy)\Big|=\frac{r|\operatorname{Im}\xi|}{|\xi-1|^{2}}\leq\frac{r|e^{-3\pi r}e^{2\pi r}\sin(\pi r+2\pi rs)|}{(1-e^{-\pi r})^{2}}\leq\frac{re^{-\pi r}\sin{(\pi r)}}{1+e^{-2\pi r}-2e^{-\pi r}}\leq\frac{1}{\pi}.

To see the last inequality, it is enough to show that
g(u)=u\sin u-e^{u}-e^{-u}+2\leq 0
for all
u=\pi r\in[0,\pi/2]
.
By Taylor’s remainder theorem, for all
u\in[0,\pi/2]
, there is
u_{0}\in[0,u]
such that

g(u)=g(0)+ug^{\prime}(0)+u^{2}g^{\prime\prime}(0)/2+u_{0}^{3}g^{(3)}(u_{0})/6=u_{0}^{3}(-3\sin u_{0}-u_{0}\cos u_{0}-e^{u_{0}}+e^{-u_{0}})/6\leq 0.

For
x\in[0,1/r]
, we use the formula

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(x+iy)-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy)=\int_{0}^{x}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds

to obtain a lower bound. We consider four cases:

\bullet
When
x\in[0,1/(2r)-1/2]
, the integrand is non-negative, and hence

\int_{0}^{x}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds\geq 0.

\bullet
When
x\in[1/(2r)-1/2,1/r-1]
, then
\frac{1}{r}-1-x\in[0,1/(2r)-1/2]
and by the previous case,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1/r-1-x}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds+\int_{1/r-1-x}^{x}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds

\displaystyle\geq\int_{1/r-1-x}^{x}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{1/r-1-x}^{x}\frac{r\sin(\pi r+2\pi rs)}{1+e^{-6\pi r-4\pi ry}-2e^{-3\pi r-2\pi ry}\cos(\pi r+2\pi rs)}\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{2\pi-\pi r-2\pi rx}^{\pi r+2\pi rx}\frac{r\sin(\theta)}{1+e^{-6\pi r-4\pi ry}-2e^{-3\pi r-2\pi ry}\cos\theta}\,\frac{d\theta}{2\pi r}=0.

\bullet
When
x\in[1/r-1,1/r-1/2]
, by the previous case and the above estimate,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1/r-1}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds+\int_{1/r-1}^{1/r-1/2}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds

\displaystyle\geq\int_{1/r-1}^{1/r-1/2}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds\geq\frac{-1}{\pi}\cdot\frac{1}{2}=\frac{-1}{2\pi}.

\bullet
When
x\in[1/r-1/2,1/r]
, by the previous case,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1/r-1/2}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds+\int_{1/r-1/2}^{x}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds

\displaystyle\geq\frac{-1}{2\pi}+\int_{1/r-1/2}^{x}\operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial Y_{r}(s+iy)}{\partial s}\,ds\geq\frac{-1}{2\pi}.

This completes the proof of Part (i).

Part (ii): Let

g(\theta,t_{1},t_{2})=\frac{1+e^{2t_{1}}-2e^{t_{1}}\cos\theta}{1+e^{2t_{2}}-2e^{t_{2}}\cos\theta},\quad\theta\in{\mathbb{R}},t_{1}\geq t_{2}>0.

Since
1+e^{2t_{2}}-2e^{t_{2}}\cos\theta\geq 1+e^{2t_{2}}-2e^{t_{2}}=(e^{t_{2}}-1)^{2}>0
,
g
is well-defined,
and is positive.
Moreover,

\partial_{\theta}g(\theta,t_{1},t_{2})=\frac{-2\sin\theta(e^{t_{1}}-e^{t_{2}})(e^{t_{1}+t_{2}}-1)}{(1+e^{2t_{2}}-2e^{t_{2}}\cos\theta)^{2}}.

Thus,
g
is a decreasing function of
\theta
on
[0,\pi]+2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
, and increasing on
[\pi,2\pi]+2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
.
This implies that
g(\theta,t_{1},t_{2})\leq g(0,t_{1},t_{2})
, for all
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Applying this inequality with
\theta=-\pi r-2\pi rx
,
t_{1}=3\pi r+2\pi ry_{1}
, and
t_{2}=3\pi r+2\pi ry_{2}
, one obtains

\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi rix}e^{2\pi ry_{1}}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi rix}e^{2\pi ry_{2}}}\Big|^{2}=g(\theta,t_{1},t_{2})\leq g(0,t_{1},t_{2})=\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{2\pi ry_{1}}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{2\pi ry_{2}}}\Big|^{2}.

Therefore, for all
y_{1}\geq y_{2}\geq-1
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(x+iy_{1})-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(x+iy_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi rix}e^{2\pi ry_{1}}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{-2\pi rix}e^{2\pi ry_{2}}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{2\pi ry_{1}}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{2\pi ry_{2}}}\Big|

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(1/r-1/2+iy_{1})-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(1/r-1/2+iy_{2}).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(1/r-1/2+iy_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{2\pi ry_{1}}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{2\pi ry_{1}}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}}\Big|=\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{1}),

and by the inequality in Part (i),

\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(1/r-1/2+iy_{2})\geq\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{2})-1/(2\pi).

Combining the above inequalities together, we conclude Part (ii) of the lemma.

Part (iii):
If
y_{1}=y_{2}
, the inequality trivially holds. Below we assume that
y_{1}>y_{2}
.

By the definition of
Y_{r}
, it is enough to prove that for all
r\in(0,1/2]
,
y_{1}\geq y_{2}\geq 0
, and
y\geq 0

we have

\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{2\pi r(y+y_{1})}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{2\pi r(y+y_{2})}}\Big|\leq\sqrt{2}\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{2\pi ry_{1}}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{2\pi ry_{2}}}\Big|.

Let us fix
y_{1}>y_{2}
, and consider the Möbius transformations

M(z)=\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{2\pi ry_{1}}z}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{2\pi ry_{2}}z},\quad M_{1}(z)=\frac{e^{-3\pi r}+e^{2\pi ry_{1}}z}{e^{-3\pi r}+e^{2\pi ry_{2}}z}.

We have
M(0)=M_{1}(0)=1
, and
M(\infty)=M_{1}(\infty)=e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})}
.
Then, there is a Möbius transformation
M_{2}
with
M_{2}(1)=1
,
M_{2}(e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})})=e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})}
,
and
M=M_{2}\circ M_{1}
. The precise form of
M_{2}
is not relevant here.

An elementary algebraic calculation shows that

M_{1}(1)\geq(1+e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})})/2.

Since
M_{1}
maps the real interval
[0,+\infty]
to
[1,e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})}]
, we must have

M_{1}(e^{2\pi ry})\geq M_{1}(1)\geq(1+e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})})/2.

That is,
M_{1}(1)
and
M_{1}(e^{2\pi ry})
belong to the right hand of the middle point of the interval

[1,e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})}]
.

Note that
|M_{2}^{\prime}(1)|=|M^{\prime}(0)/M_{1}^{\prime}(0)|=1
. Thus,
M_{2}
preserves the line
\operatorname{Re}z=(1+e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})})/2
,
and maps the real interval
[(1+e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})})/2,e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})}]
to an arc
\gamma
of a circle
whose center
B
lies on the vertical line
\operatorname{Re}z=(1+e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})})/2
.
By the above paragraph,
M(e^{2\pi ry})
and
M(1)
belong to
\gamma
.
By looking at
\arg M_{2}^{\prime}(1)
, one can see that
\gamma
meets the line
\operatorname{Im}z=0
at
e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})}

with asymptotic
angle
+\pi r\leq\pi/2
. That is,
\gamma
and
B
lie in the same component of
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
.
See Figure
5
for an illustration of the following argument.

0,1)(8,4.4)

.8)1-5390

Figure 5.
Illustration of the inequality in Lemma
7.3
-(iii). The curve
\gamma
is the arc on the dotted circle.

Let
C
be the point on
\gamma
where
|C|
achieves its maximum, and let
O
denote the origin.
Then, the line from
O
to
C
must pass through
B
.
Let
A
denote the point where the arc
\gamma
meets the line
\operatorname{Re}z=(1+e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})})/2
.
In the triangle
OAB
, the angle
\theta
at the vertex
B
is at least
\pi/2
.
By the cosine formula,

|OA|^{2}=|OB|^{2}+|AB|^{2}-2|OB||AB|\cos\theta\geq|OB|^{2}+|AB|^{2}.

Using the inequality
|OB|^{2}+|AB|^{2}\geq 2|OB||AB|
, we conclude that

2|OA|^{2}\geq|OB|^{2}+|AB|^{2}+2|OB||AB|=(|OB|+|AB|)^{2}.

By the above discussion,
M(1)
and
M(e^{2\pi ry})
belong to
\gamma
, with
M(1)
lying between
A
and

M(e^{2\pi ry})
.
Now, we consider two cases. If
M(1)
lies between
C
and
e^{2\pi r(y_{1}-y_{2})}
, then we must have

M(e^{2\pi ry})/M(1)\leq 1
.
If
M(1)
lies between
A
and
C
, by the above inequality, we obtain

\frac{|M(e^{2\pi ry})|}{|M(1)|}\leq\frac{|OC|}{|OA|}=\frac{|OB|+|BA|}{|OA|}\leq\sqrt{2}.

This completes the proof of the desired inequality.

Part (iv): Recall that
Y_{r}(0)=0
and hence by Lemma
2.2
,
\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(-i)\geq-9/10
.
Moreover,
\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy+iy_{1})\geq\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{1})
.
By rearranging terms and using the inequality in Part (iii), we note that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{1})-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{2})

\displaystyle-(\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy+iy_{1})-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy+iy_{2}))

\displaystyle=\big(\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy+iy_{2})-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{2})\big)+\big(\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{1})-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy+iy_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy+iy_{2})-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(iy_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(i(y-1))-\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(-i)+1/(4\pi)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{Im}Y_{r}(i(5-\pi)/\pi)+9/10+1/(4\pi).

On the other hand, by Equation (
7.2
), for all
r\in(0,1/2]
, we have

\displaystyle Y_{r}(i(5-\pi)/\pi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\Big|\frac{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{2r(5-\pi)}}{e^{-3\pi r}-e^{\pi ri}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\frac{|e^{-3\pi r}-1|+|1-e^{-\pi ri}|+|e^{-\pi ri}-e^{-\pi ri}e^{2r(5-\pi)}|}{\pi r/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\frac{3\pi r+\pi r+(e^{2r(5-\pi)}-1)}{\pi r/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\frac{3\pi r+\pi r+(e^{3}-8)\pi r/2}{\pi r/2}=\frac{3}{2\pi}.

Using the inequality
9/10\leq 3/\pi
, we obtain the desired estimate in Part (iv).
∎

8.
Topology of the sets
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}

8.1.
Cantor bouquets and hairy Jordan curves

In this section we describe the topology of the sets
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
in terms of the arithmetic properties of
\alpha
.
In particular, here we will prove Theorem
B
.
Let us start by presenting the definition of the two main topological objects which appear in the theorem.

A
Cantor bouquet
is any subset of the plane which is ambiently homeomorphic to a set of the form

\{re^{2\pi i\theta}\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid 0\leq\theta\leq 1,0\leq r\leq R(\theta)\}

where
R:{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}\to[0,1]
satisfies the following:

(a)

R=0
on a dense subset of
{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
R>0
on a dense subset of
{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
,

(b)

for each
\theta_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
we have

\limsup_{\theta\to\theta_{0}^{+}}R(\theta)=R(\theta_{0})=\limsup_{\theta\to\theta_{0}^{-}}R(\theta).

A
one-sided hairy Jordan curve
is any subset of the plane which is ambiently homeomorphic to a set
of the form

\{re^{2\pi i\theta}\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid 0\leq\theta\leq 1,1\leq r\leq 1+R(\theta)\}

where
R:{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}\to[0,1]
satisfies properties (a) and (b) in the above definition.

The Cantor bouquet and one-sided hairy Jordan curve enjoy similar topological feature as the standard Cantor set.
Under an additional mild condition (topological smoothness) they are uniquely characterised by some
topological axioms, see
[
AO93
]
.

To study the topology of the sets
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
(and
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
), it is convenient to work with the sets

I_{n}^{j}
and
I_{n}
from Section
3.3
. That is because each of the sets
I_{n}^{j}
and
I_{n}
is the region
above the graph of a function. Since the sets
I_{n}^{j}
, for
j\geq 0
, forms a nest, one is led to an increasing
collection of functions on a fixed domain.
Since the nest may shrink to an empty set along a fixed vertical line, we are led to considering functions which attain

+\infty
at some points.
It turns out that there are two main collections of functions required to capture the topological features of these sets.
In the next section we build these functions and study their properties.

8.2.
Height functions

8
8

8

The letter
b
stands for “base” and “p” for “pinnacle”; the reason for these will become clear in a moment.

Recall that the sets
I_{n}^{j}
and
I_{n}
consist of closed half-infinite vertical lines.
For
n\geq-1
, and
j\geq 0
, define
b_{n}^{j}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to[-1,+\infty)
as

(8.1)

b_{n}^{j}(x)=\min\{y\mid x+iy\in I_{n}^{j}\}.

Since each
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
preserves vertical lines, it follows that

I_{n}^{j}=\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid 0\leq\operatorname{Re}w\leq 1/\alpha_{n},\operatorname{Im}w\geq b_{n}^{j}(\operatorname{Re}w)\}.

By the definition of the sets
I_{n}^{j}
and the functional equations (
3.8
)–(
3.9
),
one can see that for all
n\geq-1
and
j\geq 0
,
b_{n}^{j}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to[-1,+\infty)
is continuous.
Moreover, by Equation (
3.16
), we must have
b_{n}^{j+1}\geq b_{n}^{j}
on
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
.
For
n\geq-1
, we define
b_{n}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to[-1,+\infty]
as

b_{n}(x)=\lim_{j\to+\infty}b_{n}^{j}(x)=\sup_{j\geq 1}b_{n}^{j}(x).

Note that
b_{n}
is allowed to take
+\infty
. The function
b_{n}
describes the set
I_{n}
, that is,

(8.2)

I_{n}=\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid 0\leq\operatorname{Re}w\leq 1/\alpha_{n},\operatorname{Im}w\geq b_{n}(\operatorname{Re}w)\}.

By Lemma
3.2
,
b_{n}^{j}(0)=b_{n}^{j}(1/\alpha_{n})
, and
b_{n}^{j}(x+1)=b_{n}^{j}(x)
for all
x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}-1]
.
Taking limits as
j\to+\infty
, we obtain

(8.3)

b_{n}(0)=b_{n}(1/\alpha_{n}),\qquad b_{n}(x+1)=b_{n}(x),\text{ for all }x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}-1]\text{ and }n\geq-1.

Only when
\alpha
is a Brjuno number, for
n\geq-1
and
j\geq 0
we inductively define the functions

p_{n}^{j}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to[-1,+\infty).

For all
n\geq-1
, we set
p_{n}^{0}\equiv({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{n+1})+5\pi)/(2\pi)
.
Assume that
p_{n}^{j}
is defined for some
j\geq 0
and all
n\geq-1
.
We define
p_{n}^{j+1}
on
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
as follows.
For
x_{n}\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
, we find
x_{n+1}\in[0,1/\alpha_{n+1}]
and
l_{n}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

-\varepsilon_{n+1}\alpha_{n+1}x_{n+1}=x_{n}-l_{n}
, and define

p_{n}^{j+1}(x_{n})=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}\big(x_{n+1}+ip_{n+1}^{j}(x_{n+1})\big).

In other words, the graph of
p_{n}^{j+1}
is obtained from applying
\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}
to the graph of
p_{n+1}^{j}
,
and then applying suitable translations by integers.

Evidently, for all
n\geq-1
and all
j\geq 0
, we have

p_{n}^{j+1}(x+1)=p_{n}^{j+1}(x)
, for
x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}-1]
.
Moreover, it follows from (
3.8
) and (
3.9
) that each

p_{n}^{j}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to{\mathbb{R}}
is continuous, and
p_{n}^{j}(0)=p_{n}^{j}(1/\alpha_{n})
.

Note that for every
n\geq-1
, by Lemma
7.2
-(i)-(ii) and Equation (
6.12
),
we have

\displaystyle p_{n}^{1}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n+1}]}\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}\big(x+i({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{n+2})+5\pi)/(2\pi)\big)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{n+1}\frac{{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{n+2})+5\pi}{2\pi}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\frac{1}{\alpha_{n+1}}+\frac{1}{\pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\big(\alpha_{n+1}{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{n+2})+\log\frac{1}{\alpha_{n+1}}\big)+\frac{\alpha_{n+1}5\pi}{2\pi}+\frac{1}{\pi}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{n+1})+\frac{5}{4}+\frac{1}{\pi}\leq p_{n}^{0}.

Using an induction argument, starting with
p_{n}^{1}\leq p_{n}^{0}
, for every
n\geq-1
, one may show that for all

n\geq-1
and
j\geq 0
we have

p_{n}^{j+1}(x)\leq p_{n}^{j}(x),\quad\forall x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}].

Therefore, we may define the functions

p_{n}(x)=\lim_{j\to+\infty}p_{n}^{j}(x),\quad\forall x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}].

It follows that

(8.4)

p_{n}(0)=p_{n}(1/\alpha_{n}),\quad p_{n}(x)=p_{n}(x+1),\forall x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}-1].

On the other hand, by definition,
p_{n}^{0}\geq b_{n}^{0}
, for all
n\geq-1
. Since the graphs of
b_{n+1}^{0}

and
p_{n+1}^{0}
are mapped to the graphs of
b_{n}^{1}
and
p_{n}^{1}
, respectively, by
\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}

and its integer translations, we must have
p_{n}^{1}\geq b_{n}^{1}
, for all
n\geq-1
.
By induction, this implies that for all
n\geq-1
and all
j\geq 0
,

(8.5)

p_{n}^{j}(x)\geq b_{n}^{j}(x),\quad\forall x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}].

In particular,
p_{n}\geq b_{n}
on
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
.

8.3.
Accumulation of the hairs

Proposition 8.1
.

For all
n\geq-1
, we have
9
9

9

To be clear, the notation
s\to x^{+}
means that
s\to x
and
s>x
. Similarly,

s\to x^{-}
means that
s\to x
and
s<x
.

(i)

for all
x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n})
,
\liminf_{s\to x^{+}}b_{n}(s)=b_{n}(x)
;

(ii)

for all
x\in(0,1/\alpha_{n}]
,
\liminf_{s\to x^{-}}b_{n}(s)=b_{n}(x)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1
, the sets
I_{n}^{j}
are closed. Thus, their intersection
I_{n}
is also a closed set.
Since
I_{n}
is the set above the graph of
b_{n}
, this implies that

\displaystyle\forall x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}),\liminf_{s\to x^{+}}b_{n}(s)\geq b_{n}(x),\quad\text{ and }\quad\forall x\in(0,1/\alpha_{n}],\liminf_{s\to x^{-}}b_{n}(s)\geq b_{n}(x).

So we need to show that the equality holds in both cases. If
b_{n}(x)=+\infty
, then we automatically have the
equalities. Indeed,
\liminf
can be replaced by
\lim
in that case.
Below we assume that
b_{n}(x)\neq\infty
.

Fix
x_{n}\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
, and let
e_{n}\in\{+1,-1\}
be arbitrary, except that
e_{n}=+1
if
x_{n}=0
, and
e_{n}=-1

if
x_{n}=1/\alpha_{n}
.
Define the sequence
\{e_{m}\}_{m\geq n}
according to
e_{m+1}=-\varepsilon_{m+1}e_{m}
.

We use
e_{n}
to deal with both statements at once.
That is, to prove the desired equalities, it is enough to show that for every
\delta>0
there is
x_{n}^{\prime}
strictly
between
x_{n}
and
x_{n}+\delta e_{n}
such that
b_{n}(x_{n}^{\prime})\leq b_{n}(x_{n})+\delta
.
The idea of the proof is rather elementary.
We map
x_{n}+ib_{n}(x_{n})\in I_{n}
to
z_{m}\in I_{m}
using the maps
\mathbb{Y}_{j}^{-1}
, for large
m\geq n
.
Then, using Equation (
8.3
), we find
z_{m}^{\prime}=z_{m}+e_{m}\in I_{m}
, which may be mapped to

z^{\prime}_{n}\in I_{n}
using
\mathbb{Y}_{j}
.
It follows that
\sign(\operatorname{Re}z^{\prime}_{n}-x_{n})=e_{n}
, and by the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{j}
,

z^{\prime}_{n}
is close to
x_{n}+ib_{n}(x_{n})
.
However, there are some technical difficulties due to
\varepsilon_{j}=\pm 1
and
z^{\prime}_{m}=z_{m}+e_{m}\notin I_{m}
.
We present the details in several steps.

Step 1.
There is an infinite sequence
\{(l_{m},x_{m+1})\}_{m\geq n}
, with
l_{m}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and

x_{m+1}\in[0,1/\alpha_{m+1}]
such that
\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(x_{m+1})+l_{m}=x_{m}
for all
m\geq n
.
Moreover, if
x_{m}=0
for some
m\geq n
, then
e_{m}=+1
.

We define the sequence inductively. Assume that
x_{m}\in[0,1/\alpha_{m}]
is defined for some
m\geq n
.
To define
l_{m}
and
x_{m+1}
we proceed as follows:

(a)

if
x_{m}\in[0,1/\alpha_{m}]\cap{\mathbb{Z}}
, we let
l_{m}=x_{m}+(e_{m}+\varepsilon_{m+1})/2
and define

x_{m+1}=\frac{1}{-\varepsilon_{m+1}\alpha_{m+1}}\cdot(x_{m}-l_{m})=\frac{1+\varepsilon_{m+1}e_{m}}{2\alpha_{m+1}}.

(b)

if
x_{m}\in[0,1/\alpha_{m}]\setminus{\mathbb{Z}}
we choose
l_{m}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

x_{m}-l_{m}\in((-1-\varepsilon_{m+1})/2,(1-\varepsilon_{m+1})/2)
, and define

x_{m+1}=\frac{x_{m}-l_{m}}{-\varepsilon_{m+1}\alpha_{m+1}}.

In part (a),
x_{m+1}\in\{0,1/\alpha_{m+1}\}
, depending on the sign of
\varepsilon_{m+1}e_{m}
.
In part (b), if
\varepsilon_{m+1}=-1
, then
x_{m}-l_{m}\in(0,1)
and therefore
x_{m+1}\in(0,1/\alpha_{m+1})
.
If
\varepsilon_{m+1}=+1
, then
x_{m}-l_{m}\in(-1,0)
and therefore
x_{m+1}\in(0,1/\alpha_{m+1})
.
Thus, in both cases
x_{m+1}
belongs to
[0,1/\alpha_{m+1}]
.

By Equation (
3.5
),
\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(x_{m+1})=-\varepsilon_{m+1}\alpha_{m+1}x_{m+1}
.
Thus,
\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(x_{m+1})+l_{m}=x_{m}
.

To prove the latter part of Step 1, note that if
j=n
then
e_{j}=+1
by the definition of
e_{n}
at the beginning of
the proof. If
j>n
, by the definition of the sequence
(x_{m},l_{m})
,
x_{j}
must be generated in part (a), since part (b)
produces values in
(0,1/\alpha_{m})
.
But, part (a) produces
x_{j}=0
only if
\varepsilon_{j}e_{j-1}=-1
. Then,
e_{j}=-\varepsilon_{j}e_{j-1}=+1
.

For all
m\geq n
we have

(8.6)

(1+\varepsilon_{m+1})/2\leq l_{m}\leq a_{m}+\varepsilon_{m+1}.

That is because, if
l_{m}
is produced in (b) and
\varepsilon_{m+1}=-1
then the largest integer in

[0,1/\alpha_{m}]
is
a_{m}-1
and there is
0\leq l_{m}\leq a_{m}-1
with
x_{m}-l_{m}\in(0,1)
.
If
l_{m}
is produced in (b) and
\varepsilon_{m+1}=+1
then the largest integer in
[0,1/\alpha_{m}]
is
a_{m}
and there is

1\leq l_{m}\leq a_{m}+1
with
x_{m}-l_{m}\in(-1,0)
.
If
l_{m}
is produced in (a), then
x_{m}\leq a_{m}+(\varepsilon_{m+1}-1)/2
and therefore

l_{m}\leq a_{m}+\varepsilon_{m+1}+e_{m}/2-1/2\leq a_{m}+\varepsilon_{m+1}
. On the other hand, in (a)

l_{m}=x_{m}+(e_{m}+\varepsilon_{m+1})/2\geq(1+\varepsilon_{m+1})/2
, since, by the above paragraph, if
x_{m}=0
then

e_{m}=+1
.
We are done with Step 1.

Let us say that some level
m\geq n
is
eligible
, if one of the following four cases occurs:

(E_{1})

(e_{m},\varepsilon_{m+1})=(+1,-1)
and
x_{m}
belongs to

[0,1/\alpha_{m}-2]\cup[a_{m}-2-\alpha_{m+1},a_{m}-2]\cup[a_{m}-1-\alpha_{m+1},a_{m}-1];

(E_{2})

(e_{m},\varepsilon_{m+1})=(+1,+1)
and
x_{m}
belongs to

[0,1/\alpha_{m}-2]\cup[a_{m}-1-\alpha_{m+1},a_{m}-1]\cup[a_{m}-\alpha_{m+1},a_{m}];

(E_{3})

(e_{m},\varepsilon_{m+1})=(-1,-1)
and
x_{m}
belongs to

[1,1/\alpha_{m}-1]\cup[1-\alpha_{m+1},1]\cup[a_{m}-1,a_{m}-1+\alpha_{m+1}];

(E_{4})

(e_{m},\varepsilon_{m+1})=(-1,+1)
and
x_{m}
belongs to

[1,1/\alpha_{m}-1]\cup[\alpha_{m+1},2\alpha_{m+1}]\cup[a_{m},a_{m}+\alpha_{m+1}].

Recall the numbers
\beta_{j}
introduced in Section
6.2
.

Step 2.
Let
k\geq j\geq n
be integers. Either there is an eligible
m\in[j,k]
, or

(8.7)

x_{j}\in\begin{cases}[0,\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]\cup[1/\alpha_{j}-1,1/\alpha_{j}-1+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]&\text{if }e_{j}=-1\\
[1/\alpha_{j}-1-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},1/\alpha_{j}-1]\cup[1/\alpha_{j}-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},1/\alpha_{j}]&\text{if }e_{j}=+1.\end{cases}

We prove this by induction on
k-j
.
Assume that
k-j=0
.
If
e_{j}=+1
then either
x_{j}\in[0,1/\alpha_{j}-2]
and
j
is eligible as in (
E_{1}
) and
(E_{2})
,
or
x_{j}\in[1/\alpha_{j}-2,1/\alpha_{j}-1]\cup[1/\alpha_{j}-1,1/\alpha_{j}]
and (
8.7
) holds.
If
e_{j}=-1
then either
x_{j}\in[1,1/\alpha_{j}-1]
and
j
is eligible as in (
E_{3}
) and
(E_{4})
, or

x_{j}\in[0,1]\cup[1/\alpha_{j}-1,1/\alpha_{j}]
and (
8.7
) holds.

Now assume that the statement holds for integers
k
and
j
with
k-j=t\geq 0
.
We aim to prove it for integers
k
and
j
with
k-j=t+1
.
By the induction hypotheses applied to the pair
j+1
and
k
we conclude that either there is an eligible
m\in[j+1,k]
,
or

(8.8)

x_{j+1}\in\begin{cases}[0,\beta_{k}/\beta_{j+1}]\cup[1/\alpha_{j+1}-1,1/\alpha_{j+1}-1+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j+1}]&\text{if }e_{j+1}=-1\\
[1/\alpha_{j+1}-1-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j+1},1/\alpha_{j+1}-1]\cup[1/\alpha_{j+1}-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j+1},1/\alpha_{j+1}]&\text{if }e_{j+1}=+1.\end{cases}

If there is an eligible
m\in[j+1,k]\subset[j,k]
then we are done. We show that if
(
8.8
) holds, either
j
is eligible or (
8.7
) holds.
To prove this, we consider four cases based on the values of
e_{j}
and
\varepsilon_{j+1}
.

I) If
(e_{j},\varepsilon_{j+1})=(+1,-1)
,
e_{j+1}=-\varepsilon_{j+1}e_{j}=+1
, and by Equation (
8.8
),

x_{j+1}\in[1/\alpha_{j+1}-1-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j+1},1/\alpha_{j+1}-1]\cup[1/\alpha_{j+1}-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j+1},1/\alpha_{j+1}].

Since
x_{m}-l_{m}=-\varepsilon_{j+1}\alpha_{j+1}x_{j+1}=\alpha_{j+1}x_{j+1}
, this implies that

x_{j}\in\big([1-\alpha_{j+1}-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},1-\alpha_{j+1}]\cup[1-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},1]\big)+{\mathbb{Z}}.

If
x_{j}\in[0,1/\alpha_{j}-2]
then
j
is eligible through (
E_{1}
).
If
x_{j}\in[1/\alpha_{j}-2,1/\alpha_{j}]
, using
1/\alpha_{j}=a_{j}-\alpha_{j+1}
,
x_{j}
must belong to one of the intervals

\displaystyle[a_{j}-2-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},a_{j}-2],\;[a_{j}-1-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},a_{j}-1]

\displaystyle[1/\alpha_{j}-1-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},1/\alpha_{j}-1],\;[1/\alpha_{j}-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},1/\alpha_{j}].

If
x_{j}
belongs to one of the bottom two intervals then (
8.7
) holds.
If
x_{j}
belongs to one of the top two interval, then
j
is eligible though (
E_{1}
), since
[a_{j}-2-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},a_{j}-2]

is contained in
[a_{j}-2-\alpha_{j+1},a_{j}-2]
and
[a_{j}-1-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},a_{j}-1]
is contained in
[a_{j}-1-\alpha_{j+1},a_{j}-1]
.
Here we use
\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}\leq\alpha_{j+1}
, which is valid due to
k\geq j+1
.

II) If
(e_{j},\varepsilon_{j+1})=(+1,+1)
,
e_{j+1}=-\varepsilon_{j+1}e_{j}=-1
, and by Equation (
8.8
),

x_{j+1}\in[0,\beta_{k}/\beta_{j+1}]\cup[1/\alpha_{j+1}-1,1/\alpha_{j+1}-1+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j+1}].

Since
x_{j}-l_{j}=-\varepsilon_{j+1}\alpha_{j+1}x_{j+1}=-\alpha_{j+1}x_{j+1}
, this implies that

x_{j}\in\big([-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},0]\cup[-1+\alpha_{j+1}-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},-1+\alpha_{j+1}]\big)+{\mathbb{Z}}.

If
x_{j}\in[0,1/\alpha_{j}-2]
then
j
is eligible though (
E_{2}
).
If
x_{j}\in[1/\alpha_{j}-2,1/\alpha_{j}]
, using
1/\alpha_{j}=a_{j}+\alpha_{j+1}
,
x_{j}
must belong to one of the intervals

\displaystyle[a_{j}-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},a_{j}],\;[a_{j}-1-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},a_{j}-1]

\displaystyle[1/\alpha_{j}-1-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},1/\alpha_{j}-1],\;[1/\alpha_{j}-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},1/\alpha_{j}].

If
x_{j}
belongs to one of the bottom two intervals, then (
8.7
) holds.
If
x_{j}
belongs to one of the top two intervals then
j
is eligible as in (
E_{2}
), since
[a_{j}-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},a_{j}]

is contained in
[a_{j}-\alpha_{j+1},a_{j}]
and
[a_{j}-1-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},a_{j}-1]
is contained in
[a_{j}-1-\alpha_{j+1},a_{j}-1]
.

III) If
(e_{j},\varepsilon_{j+1})=(-1,-1)
,
e_{j+1}=-\varepsilon_{j+1}e_{j}=-1
, and by Equation (
8.8
),

x_{j+1}\in[0,\beta_{k}/\beta_{j+1}]\cup[1/\alpha_{j+1}-1,1/\alpha_{j+1}-1+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j+1}].

Since
x_{j}-l_{j}=-\varepsilon_{j+1}\alpha_{j+1}x_{j+1}=\alpha_{j+1}x_{j+1}
, this implies that

x_{j}\in\big([0,\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]\cup[1-\alpha_{j+1},1-\alpha_{j+1}+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]\big)+{\mathbb{Z}}.

If
x_{j}\in[1,1/\alpha_{j}-1]
then
j
is eligible through (
E_{3}
).
If
