x_{j}\in[0,1]\cup[1/\alpha_{j}-1,1/\alpha_{j}]
, using
1/\alpha_{j}=a_{j}-\alpha_{j+1}
,
x_{j}
must belong to one of the intervals

\displaystyle[0,\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}],[1/\alpha_{j}-1,1/\alpha_{j}-1+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]

\displaystyle[1-\alpha_{j+1},1-\alpha_{j+1}+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}],[a_{j}-1,a_{j}-1+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}].

If
x_{j}
belongs to one of the top two intervals, then (
8.7
) holds.
If
x_{j}
belongs to one of the bottom two interval then
j
is eligible through (
E_{3}
) since

[1-\alpha_{j+1},1-\alpha_{j+1}+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]
is contained in
[1-\alpha_{j+1},1]

and
[a_{j}-1,a_{j}-1+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]
is contained in
[a_{j}-1,a_{j}-1+\alpha_{j+1}]
.

IV) If
(e_{j},\varepsilon_{j+1})=(-1,+1)
,
e_{j+1}=-\varepsilon_{j+1}e_{j}=+1
, and by Equation (
8.8
),

x_{j+1}\in[1/\alpha_{j+1}-1-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j+1},1/\alpha_{j+1}-1]\cup[1/\alpha_{j+1}-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j+1},1/\alpha_{j+1}].

Since
x_{j}-l_{j}=-\varepsilon_{j+1}\alpha_{j+1}x_{j+1}=-\alpha_{j+1}x_{j+1}
, this implies that

x_{j}\in\big([-1+\alpha_{j+1},-1+\alpha_{j}+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]\cup[-1,-1+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]\big)+{\mathbb{Z}}.

If
x_{j}\in[1,1/\alpha_{j}-1]
then
j
is eligible through (
E_{4}
).
If
x_{j}\in[0,1]\cup[1/\alpha_{j}-1,1/\alpha_{j}]
, using
1/\alpha_{j}=a_{j}+\alpha_{j+1}
,
x_{j}
must belong to one of the intervals

\displaystyle[\alpha_{j+1},\alpha_{j+1}+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}],\;[a_{j},a_{j}+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]

\displaystyle[0,\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}],\;[1/\alpha_{j}-1,1/\alpha_{j}-1+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]

If
x_{j}
belongs to one of the bottom two intervals, then (
8.7
) holds.
If
x_{j}
belongs to one of the top two intervals then
j
is eligible through (
E_{4}
),
since
[\alpha_{j+1},\alpha_{j+1}+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]
is contained in
[\alpha_{j+1},2\alpha_{j+1}]
and
[a_{j},a_{j}+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]

is contained in
[a_{j},a_{j}+\alpha_{j+1}]
.

Step 3.
Either there is
j\geq n
such that for all
m\geq j
we have
x_{m}=1/\alpha_{m}-1
, or there are arbitrarily
large eligible
m\geq n
.

If there is
j\geq n
such that
x_{j}\in\{1/\alpha_{j}-1,1/\alpha_{j}\}
, then for all
m\geq j+1
we have
x_{m}=1/\alpha_{m}-1
.
That is because, by the definition of the sequence
\{(x_{m},l_{m})\}
, if
x_{j}\in\{1/\alpha_{j}-1,1/\alpha_{j}\}
then

x_{j+1}\in\{1/\alpha_{j+1}-1\}
.
Therefore, for all
m\geq j+1
we have
x_{m}\in\{1/\alpha_{m}-1\}
.

Assume that there is
j\geq n
such that
x_{j}=0
.
Recall from Step 1 that whenever
x_{m}=0
,
e_{m}=+1
.
By the definition of the sequence
(x_{m},l_{m})
, if some
x_{m}=0
then either

\varepsilon_{m+1}=-1
and hence
x_{m+1}=0
, or
\varepsilon_{m+1}=+1
and hence
x_{m+1}=1/\alpha_{m+1}
.
By the above paragraph, it follows that either eventually
x_{m}=0
, or eventually
x_{m}=1/\alpha_{m}-1
.
When eventually
x_{m}=0
, all sufficiently large
m
becomes eligible through (
E_{1}
) and (
E_{2}
).

By the above paragraphs, if there is
j\geq n
with
x_{j}\in\{0,1/\alpha_{j}-1,1/\alpha_{j}\}
, we are done.
Below we assume that there are no such
j
.

Fix an arbitrary
j\geq n
. Since
\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, there is
k\geq j
such that

x_{j}\notin[0,\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]\cup[1/\alpha_{j}-1-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j},1/\alpha_{j}-1+\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]\cup[1/\alpha_{j}-\beta_{k}/\beta_{j}]
.
It follows from Step 2 that there must be an eligible
j^{\prime}\in[j,k]
.
This proves that there are arbitrarily large eligible
m\geq n
.

Step 4.
For every
\delta>0
there is
x_{n}^{\prime}
strictly between
x_{n}
and
x_{n}+\delta e_{n}
such that

b_{n}(x_{n}^{\prime})\leq b_{n}(x_{n})+\delta
.

For
m\geq n
, let
z_{m}=x_{m}+ib_{m}(x_{m})\in I_{m}
, which is the lowest point on
I_{m}
with real part
equal to
x_{m}
.
By Step 1, for
m\geq n
, we have
\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(x_{m+1})+l_{m}=x_{m}
.
It follows from the definition of the sets
I_{n}^{j}
and
I_{n}
that
\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1})+l_{m}=z_{m}
.

For
m\geq 0
, let
K_{m}=\cap_{j\geq 1}K_{m}^{j}
and
J_{m}=\cap_{j\geq 1}J_{m}^{j}
.

Assume that
m\geq n
, and either
x_{m}=1/\alpha_{m}-1
or
m
is eligible.
We claim that there is
z_{m}^{\prime}\in I_{m}
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

\sign(\operatorname{Re}z^{\prime}_{m}-\operatorname{Re}z_{m})=e_{m}
,

(ii)

|z^{\prime}_{m}-z_{m}|\leq 2
,

(iii)

either both
z^{\prime}_{m}
and
z_{m}
belong to
K_{m}
, or both
z^{\prime}_{m}
and
z_{m}
belong to
J_{m}
.

If
x_{m}=1/\alpha_{m}-1
we simply let
z^{\prime}_{m}=z_{m}+e_{m}
. By Equation (
8.3
),
z^{\prime}_{m}\in I_{m}
.
Moreover,
\{z_{m},z^{\prime}_{m}\}\subset K_{m}
if
e_{m}=-1
and
\{z_{m},z^{\prime}_{m}\}\subset J_{m}
if
e_{m}=+1
.
If
m
is eligible, we show this by looking at cases
(E_{1})
through
(E_{4})
in the definition of eligibility.
The main tool for each of those cases is the periodic property of
I_{m}
and
I_{m+1}
in Equation (
8.3
).

(E_{1})
: If
x_{m}\in[0,1/\alpha_{m}-2]
we let
z^{\prime}_{m}=z_{m}+1=z_{m}+e_{m}
. Here
\{z_{m},z^{\prime}_{m}\}\subset K_{m}
.

If
x_{m}\in[a_{m}-2-\alpha_{m+1},a_{m}-2]=[1/\alpha_{m}-2,a_{m}-2]
, then
x_{m+1}\in[1/\alpha_{m+1}-1,1/\alpha_{m+1}]
.
Choose an integer
k_{m+1}
with
x_{m+1}-k_{m+1}\in[0,1]
and define

z^{\prime}_{m}=\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1}-k_{m+1})+a_{m}-2
.
Note that
\operatorname{Re}z_{m}\leq a_{m}-2\leq\operatorname{Re}z^{\prime}_{m}\leq a_{m}-2+\alpha_{m+1}\leq 1/\alpha_{m}-1
.
This implies that
\sign(\operatorname{Re}z_{m}^{\prime}-\operatorname{Re}z_{m})=+1=e_{m}
, and
\{z_{m},z^{\prime}_{m}\}\subset K_{m}
.
Here,
a_{m}=1/\alpha_{m}+\alpha_{m+1}\geq 2+\alpha_{m+1}
, so
a_{m}\geq 3
.
Using Equation (
3.8
) with
e_{m}=+1
and Equation (
3.7
),

\displaystyle|z_{m}-z^{\prime}_{m}|

\displaystyle=|(\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1})+a_{m}-3)-(\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1}-k_{m+1})+a_{m}-2)|

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1})-1-\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1}-k_{m+1})|

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1}-1/\alpha_{m+1})-\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1}-k_{m+1})|

\displaystyle\leq 0.9|(z_{m+1}-1/\alpha_{m+1})-(z_{m+1}-k_{m+1})|

\displaystyle=0.9|1/\alpha_{m+1}-k_{m+1}|\leq 0.9\cdot 2\leq 2.

If
x_{m}\in[a_{m}-1-\alpha_{m+1},a_{m}-1]=[1/\alpha_{m}-1,a_{m}-1]
we let
z^{\prime}_{m}=\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1}-k_{m+1})+a_{m}-1
.
In this case,
\operatorname{Re}z_{m}\leq a_{m}-1\leq\operatorname{Re}z^{\prime}_{m}\leq a_{m}-1+\alpha_{m+1}\leq 1/\alpha_{m}
.
Hence,
\sign(\operatorname{Re}z_{m}^{\prime}-\operatorname{Re}z_{m})=+1=e_{m}
, and
\{z_{m},z^{\prime}_{m}\}\subset J_{m}
.
As in the previous case,
|z_{m}-z^{\prime}_{m}|\leq 2
.

(E_{2})
: If
x_{m}\in[0,1/\alpha_{m}-2]
we let
z^{\prime}_{m}=z_{m}+1=z_{m}+e_{m}
.
If
x_{m}
belongs to
[a_{m}-1-\alpha_{m+1},a_{m}-1]
, then
x_{m+1}\in[0,1]
.
Choose an integer
k_{m+1}
with
x_{m+1}+k_{m+1}\in[1/\alpha_{m+1}-1,1/\alpha_{m+1}]
and define

z^{\prime}_{m}=\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1}+k_{m+1})+a_{m}
.
If
x_{m}
belongs to
[a_{m}-\alpha_{m+1},a_{m}]
, we let
z^{\prime}_{m}=\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1}+k_{m+1})+a_{m}+1
.
As in the previous case, one can see that
z^{\prime}_{m}
enjoys the desired properties.

(E_{3})
: If
x_{m}
belongs to
[1,1/\alpha_{m}-1]
, we let
z^{\prime}_{m}=z_{m}-1=z_{m}+e_{m}
.
If
x_{m}
belongs to
[1-\alpha_{m+1},1]
, then
x_{m+1}\in[1/\alpha_{m+1}-1,1/\alpha_{m+1}]
.
We define
z^{\prime}_{m}=\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1}-1)
.
If
x_{m}
belongs to
[a_{m}-1,a_{m}-1+\alpha_{m+1}]
then
x_{m+1}\in[0,1]
.
We choose an integer
k_{m+1}
with
x_{m+1}+k_{m+1}\in[1/\alpha_{m+1}-1,1/\alpha_{m+1}]
and define

z^{\prime}_{m}=\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1}+k_{m+1})+a_{m}-2
.
One can see that
z^{\prime}_{m}
enjoys the desired properties.

(E_{4})
: If
x_{m}
belongs to
[1,1/\alpha_{m}-1]
, we let
z^{\prime}_{m}=z_{m}-1=z_{m}+e_{m}
.
If
x_{m}
belongs to
[\alpha_{m+1},2\alpha_{m+1}]
then
x_{m+1}\in[1/\alpha_{m+1}-2,1/\alpha_{m+1}-1]
.
We define
z^{\prime}_{m}=\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1}+1)+1
.
If
x_{m}
belongs to
[a_{m},a_{m}+\alpha_{m+1}]=[a_{m},1/\alpha_{m}]
, then
x_{m+1}\in[1/\alpha_{m+1}-1,1/\alpha_{m+1}]
.
Choose an integer
k_{m+1}
with
x_{m+1}-k_{m+1}\in[0,1]
and define

z^{\prime}_{m}=\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(z_{m+1}-k_{m+1})+a_{m}
.
One can see that
z^{\prime}_{m}
enjoys the desired properties.
This completes the proof of the existence of
z^{\prime}_{m}
.

By the definition of the domains
I_{n}^{j}
, and Equation (
8.6
), for all
m\geq n

•

either
\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(K_{m+1})+l_{m}\subset K_{m}
or
\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(K_{m+1})+l_{m}\subset J_{m}
,

•

either
\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(J_{m+1})+l_{m}\subset K_{m}
or
\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}(J_{m+1})+l_{m}\subset J_{m}
.

It follows from the above properties that any composition of the form
(\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}+l_{n})\circ\dots\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}+l_{m})

is defined and continuous on both
K_{m}
and
J_{m}
.

Fix an arbitrary
\delta>0
and choose
n^{\prime}\geq n
such that
2\cdot 0.9^{(n^{\prime}-n)}<\delta
.
By Step 3 there is
m\geq n^{\prime}
such that either
x_{m}=1/\alpha_{m}-1
or
m
is eligible.
Then, by the above argument, there is
z^{\prime}_{m}
satisfying the three items listed above.
Let us define
z_{n}^{m}=(\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}+l_{n})\circ\dots\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{m+1}+l_{m})(z^{\prime}_{m})\in I_{n}
.
By Equation (
3.7
), we have
|z_{n}^{m}-z_{n}|\leq 2\cdot 0.9^{m-n}<\delta
.
Let
x^{\prime}_{n}=x_{n}^{m}=\operatorname{Re}z_{n}^{m}
. We have
|x^{\prime}_{n}-x_{n}|=|\operatorname{Re}z_{n}^{m}-\operatorname{Re}z_{n}|<\delta
and

b_{n}(x_{n}^{m})-b_{n}(x_{n})\leq\operatorname{Im}z_{n}^{m}-\operatorname{Im}z_{n}<\delta
.
Moreover, the relations
\sign(\operatorname{Re}z^{\prime}_{m}-\operatorname{Re}z_{m})=e_{m}
,
e_{m-1}=-\varepsilon_{m}e_{m}
, and

\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{Y}_{m}(x)=-\varepsilon_{m}\alpha_{m}x
imply that
\sign(x_{n}^{m}-x_{n})=\sign(\operatorname{Re}x_{n}^{m}-\operatorname{Re}z_{n})=e_{n}
.
∎

8.4.
The Brjuno condition in the renormalisation tower

Proposition 8.2
.

For all
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}
and all
n\geq-1
we have

\Big|2\pi\sup_{x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]}b_{n}(x)-{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{n+1})\Big|\leq 5\pi.

Proof.

For
n\geq-1
and
j\geq 0
we define

D_{n}^{j}=\max\{b_{n}^{j}(x)\mid x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\}.

We first show that the numbers
D_{n}^{j}
nearly satisfy the recursive relation for the
Brjuno function, see Equation (
6.12
). That is, for all
n\geq 0
and all
j\geq 1
, we have

(8.9)

\big|2\pi D_{n-1}^{j}-2\pi\alpha_{n}D_{n}^{j-1}-\log(1/\alpha_{n})\big|\leq 4.

Since
b_{n-1}^{j}
and
b_{n}^{j-1}
are periodic of period
+1
, we may choose

x_{n-1}\in[1/(2\alpha_{n-1})-1,1/(2\alpha_{n-1})]
and
x_{n}\in[1/(2\alpha_{n})-1,1/(2\alpha_{n})]
such that

b_{n-1}^{j}(x_{n-1})=D_{n-1}^{j}
and
b_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n})=D_{n}^{j-1}
.
Choose
x_{n}^{\prime}\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
such that
-\varepsilon_{n}\alpha_{n}x^{\prime}_{n}\in x_{n-1}+{\mathbb{Z}}
.
By Lemma
7.2
-(i), we must have
x_{n}^{\prime}\in[1/(2\alpha_{n})-1,1/(2\alpha_{n})]
.
We apply
7.2
-(ii) with
y=b_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n}^{\prime})
and
x=x_{n}^{\prime}
, to obtain

\displaystyle 2\pi\alpha_{n}D_{n}^{j-1}+\log 1/\alpha_{n}=2\pi\alpha_{n}b_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n})+\log 1/\alpha_{n}

\displaystyle\geq 2\pi\alpha_{n}b_{n}^{j-1}(x^{\prime}_{n})+\log 1/\alpha_{n}

\displaystyle\geq 2\pi\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(x_{n}^{\prime}+ib_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n}^{\prime}))-2

\displaystyle=2\pi b_{n-1}^{j}(x_{n-1})-2=2\pi D_{n-1}^{j}-2.

Similarly,

\displaystyle 2\pi\alpha_{n}D_{n}^{j-1}+\log 1/\alpha_{n}=2\pi\alpha_{n}b_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n})+\log 1/\alpha_{n}

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\operatorname{Im}(x_{n}+ib_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n}))+4

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi b_{n-1}^{j}(x_{n-1})+4=2\pi D_{n-1}^{j}+4.

This completes the proof of inequality (
8.9
).

Fix an arbitrary
n\geq-1
and
j\geq 1
. Let us define
\beta_{n+i}(\alpha_{n+1})=\prod_{l=1}^{i}\alpha_{n+l}
for
i\geq 1
,
and
\beta_{n}(\alpha_{n+1})=1
.
Then for integers
k\in[0,j]
define the numbers

X_{k}=2\pi\beta_{n+k}(\alpha_{n+1})D_{n+k}^{j-k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta_{n+i-1}(\alpha_{n+1})\log 1/\alpha_{n+i}.

We have
X_{0}=2\pi D_{n}^{j}
.
With this notation, we form a telescoping sum

2\pi D_{n}^{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}(X_{k}-X_{k+1})+X_{j}.

By Equation (
8.9
),

|X_{k}-X_{k+1}|=\big|\beta_{n+k}(\alpha_{n+1})\big(2\pi D_{n+k}^{j-k}-2\pi\alpha_{n+k+1}D_{n+k+1}^{j-k-1}-\log(1/\alpha_{n+k+1})\big)\big|\leq\beta_{n+k}(\alpha_{n+1})4.

On the other hand, since
D_{n+j}^{0}=-1
, we have

\big|X_{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{j}\beta_{n+i-1}(\alpha_{n+1})\log 1/\alpha_{n+i}\big|=|2\pi\beta_{n+j}(\alpha_{n+1})D_{n+j}^{0}|\leq 2\pi.

Combining the above inequalities, and using Equation (
3.1
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\Big|2\pi D_{n}^{j}-\sum_{i=1}^{j}\beta_{n+i-1}(\alpha_{n+1})\log 1/\alpha_{n+i}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\beta_{n+k}(\alpha_{n+1})4+2\pi

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}2^{-k}4+2\pi\leq 8+2\pi.

By Equation (
3.16
),
b_{n}^{j}\geq b_{n}^{j-1}
, which implies that
D_{n}^{j}\geq D_{n}^{j-1}
.
Hence, for each
n\geq-1
,
D_{n}^{j}
forms an increasing sequence. Combining with the above inequality,

\Big|2\pi\lim_{j\to+\infty}D_{n}^{j}-{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{n+1})\Big|\leq 8+2\pi.

Note that because
b_{n}^{j}\leq b_{n}
and
b_{n}^{j}\to b_{n}
point-wise, we must have

\sup_{x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]}b_{n}=\lim_{j\to+\infty}D_{n}^{j}
.
∎

8.5.
The Herman condition in the renormalisation tower

In this section we establish an equivalent criterion for the arithmetic class
{\mathscr{H}}
in terms of the maps
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
.
The key idea here is that the equivalent criterion in Proposition
6.5
is stable under uniform changes
to the maps
h_{\alpha_{n}}
. That is, if one replaces
h_{\alpha_{n}}
by uniformly nearby maps, say
\mathbb{Y}_{n}^{-1}
,
the corresponding set of rotation numbers stays the same.
See Proposition
7.1
.

Note that for arbitrary
m>n\geq 0
and
y\geq 0
, the compositions

h_{\alpha_{n}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m}}^{-1}(y)
may not be defined. This happens when an intermediate
iterate falls into
(-\infty,0]
.

Lemma 8.3
.

Assume that for some integers
m>n\geq 0
, and
y\in(1,+\infty)
, the composition

h_{\alpha_{n}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m}}^{-1}(y)
is defined and is positive. Then,

\big|2\pi\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathbb{Y}_{n}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m}(iy/(2\pi))\right)-h_{\alpha_{n}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m}}^{-1}(y)\big|\leq 10\pi.

Proof.

For integers
j
with
n\leq j\leq m-1
, we may introduce

\displaystyle G_{m,j-1}(y)=h_{\alpha_{j}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m}}^{-1}(y),G_{m,m-1}(y)=h_{\alpha_{m}}^{-1}(y),G_{m,m}(y)=y.

By the assumptions in the lemma, all the above values are positive.
Also, for integers
j
with
n+1\leq j\leq m
, and
t\geq-1
, we may introduce the maps

\displaystyle\Psi_{j,n-1}(t)=2\pi\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{j}(it/(2\pi)),\Psi_{n,n-1}(t)=2\pi\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(it/(2\pi)),\Psi_{n-1,n-1}(t)=t.

With the above notations, we may form a telescoping sum, to obtain

\displaystyle\big|h_{\alpha_{n}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m}}^{-1}(y)

\displaystyle-2\pi\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m}(iy/(2\pi))\big|

\displaystyle=\big|G_{m,n-1}(y)-\Psi_{m,n-1}(y)\big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{j=n}^{m}\Psi_{j-1,n-1}(G_{m,j-1}(y))-\Psi_{j,n-1}(G_{m,j}(y))\Big|

By Equation (
3.7
), for all
s
and
t
in
(0,+\infty)
,

|\Psi_{j-1,n-1}(s)-\Psi_{j-1,n-1}(t)\big|\leq 0.9^{(j-n)}\cdot|s-t|
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\big|\Psi_{j-1,n-1}(G_{m,j-1}(y))-

\displaystyle\Psi_{j,n-1}(G_{m,j}(y))\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\Psi_{j-1,n-1}(G_{m,j-1}(y))-\Psi_{j-1,n-1}(-2\pi i\mathbb{Y}_{j}(iG_{m,j}(y)/(2\pi)))\big|

\displaystyle\leq 0.9^{(j-n)}\big|G_{m,j-1}(y)+2\pi i\mathbb{Y}_{j}(iG_{m,j}(y)/(2\pi))\big|

On the other hand, by Proposition
7.1
and Equation (
3.5
), for all
j\geq 0
and all
t\geq 1
, we have

|h_{\alpha_{j}}^{-1}(t)-2\pi\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{j}(it/(2\pi))|\leq\pi.

Also, note that since
G_{m,n-1}(y)>0
and
h_{\alpha_{n}}(0)=1
,
G_{m,j}(y)>1
, for
n\leq j\leq m
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\big|G_{m,j-1}(y)+2\pi i\mathbb{Y}_{j}(iG_{m,j}(y)/(2\pi))\big|=\big|h_{\alpha_{j}}^{-1}(G_{m,j}(y))-2\pi\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{j}(iG_{m,j}(y)/(2\pi))\big|\leq\pi.

Combining the above inequalities together, we obtain

\displaystyle\big|h_{\alpha_{n}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m}}^{-1}(y)

\displaystyle-2\pi\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m}(iy/(2\pi))\big|\leq\sum_{j=n}^{m}0.9^{(j-n)}\pi\leq 10\pi.\qed

Proposition 8.4
.

An irrational number
\alpha
belongs to
{\mathscr{H}}
, if and only if, for all
x>0
there is
m\geq 1
such that

\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))\leq x.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary
\alpha\in{\mathscr{H}}
and
x>0
.
Choose
n\geq 0
such that
0.9^{n}(10\pi+1)/(2\pi)\leq x
.
By the criterion in Proposition
6.5
, there is
m\geq n
such that

(8.10)

h_{\alpha_{m-1}}\circ\cdots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0)\geq{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m}).

Now we consider two cases. First assume that
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})\leq 1
.
Recall that
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})>0
. By (
3.6
) and (
3.7
),

\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))\leq 0.9^{m}\cdot 1/(2\pi)\leq 0.9^{n}/(2\pi)\leq x.

Thus, we have the desired inequality in the proposition in this case.

Now assume that
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})>1
. The composition of the maps in Equation (
8.10
) is understood
as the identity map when
m=n
, and as the map
h_{\alpha_{n}}
when
m=n+1
. Also recall that
h_{\alpha_{n}}(0)=1
.
Then, in this case, we must have
n<m-1
.
Let
k\in[n,m-1]
be the smallest integer such that

h_{\alpha_{k}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m}))

is defined and is positive. By Equation (
8.10
),
k
exists and
k>n
. Moreover, by the minimality of

k
, we must have
h_{\alpha_{k}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m}))\leq+1
.
Now, we may use Lemma
8.3
with
y={\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})
and

h_{\alpha_{k}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m}))
to conclude that

\displaystyle|2\pi\operatorname{Im}

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{Y}_{k}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq|2\pi\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{k}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))-h_{\alpha_{k}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m}))|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+|h_{\alpha_{k}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m}))|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 10\pi+1.

Then, by (
3.6
) and (
3.7
), we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{k-1}\circ\mathbb{Y}_{k}\circ\cdots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{k-1}((10\pi+1)/(2\pi))

\displaystyle\leq 0.9^{k}\frac{10\pi+1}{2\pi}\leq 0.9^{n}\frac{10\pi+1}{2\pi}\leq x.

Thus, the desired inequality in the proposition also holds in this case.

To prove the other direction of the proposition, fix an arbitrary
n\geq 0
.
We shall prove that there is
m\geq n
satisfying the inequality in Proposition
6.5
.

First note that for all
j\geq 0
and
y\geq 0
,
h_{\alpha_{j}}^{-1}(y)\leq y-1
. This implies that there are
k>l\geq n

such that

(8.11)

h_{\alpha_{l}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{k}}^{-1}(12\pi)\leq 0.

In particular, the composition in the above equation is defined. Note that in general one cannot choose
l=n
.
Now, choose
x>0
such that

(8.12)

x<\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{k}(i),

and

(8.13)

x<\min\big\{\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{j}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{j+1})/(2\pi))\mid\forall j\in[0,k-1]\cap\mathbb{Z}\big\},

By the hypothesis in the proposition, there is
m\geq 1
such that

(8.14)

\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))\leq x.

Note that by Equation (
8.13
) we must have
m\geq k+1
. In particular,
m\geq n
.

By Equation (
8.12
), we must have

(8.15)

\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{k+1}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))\leq 1.

Otherwise, by the injectivity of the maps
\mathbb{Y}_{j}
and Equation (
3.6
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{k}(\mathbb{Y}_{k+1}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))

\displaystyle>\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{k}(i)>x,

which contradicts Equation (
8.14
).

Now we consider two cases.
First assume that
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})>1
and
h_{\alpha_{k+1}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m}))

is defined. Here, we may apply Lemma
8.3
to this composition with
y={\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})
, and use
Equation (
8.15
), to get

\displaystyle h_{\alpha_{k+1}}^{-1}\circ\dots

\displaystyle\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m}))

\displaystyle\leq|h_{\alpha_{k+1}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m}))-2\pi\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{k+1}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+|2\pi\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{k+1}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))|

\displaystyle\leq 10\pi+2\pi.

Combining this with Equation (
8.11
), and using the monotonicity of the maps
h_{\alpha_{j}}
, we conclude that
there is
l^{\prime}\in[l,k]
such that

\displaystyle h_{\alpha_{l^{\prime}}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m}))

\displaystyle=h_{\alpha_{l^{\prime}}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{k}}^{-1}(h_{\alpha_{k+1}}^{-1}\circ\cdots\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})))

\displaystyle\leq h_{\alpha_{l^{\prime}}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{k}}^{-1}(12\pi)<0.

Here we need to choose
l^{\prime}\geq l
so that the compositions in the above equation are defined.
The above inequality implies that
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})<h_{\alpha_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{l^{\prime}}}(0)
.
Note that
l^{\prime}\geq l\geq n
.
On the other hand, since
h_{\alpha_{j}}(y)\geq y+1
for all
j
and
y>0
, we must have

h_{\alpha_{l^{\prime}-1}}\circ\cdots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0)>0
. Therefore,

{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})<h_{\alpha_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{l^{\prime}}}(0)<h_{\alpha_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0).

This completes the argument in this case.

Now assume that either
{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})\leq 1
, or

h_{\alpha_{k+1}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m}))
is not defined.
These imply that there is
j
in
[k+2,m-1]
such that

h_{\alpha_{j}}^{-1}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{m-1}}^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m}))\leq 0
, and hence

{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})\leq h_{\alpha_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{j}}(0)
.
Note that
j\geq k+2\geq n+2
.
As in the previous case,
h_{\alpha_{j-1}}\circ\cdots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0)>0
. Therefore,

{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})<h_{\alpha_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{j}}(0)<h_{\alpha_{m-1}}\circ\dots\circ h_{\alpha_{n}}(0).

This completes the argument in this case.
∎

Proposition 8.5
.

Assume that
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}
. Then,
\alpha\in{\mathscr{H}}
if and only if
p_{n}(0)=b_{n}(0)=0
for all
n\geq-1
.

Proof.

Recall that
p_{n}(0)=p_{n}(1/\alpha_{n})
and
b_{n}(0)=b_{n}(1/\alpha_{n})
, for all
n\geq-1
. Also,
\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}
maps the graphs of

p_{n+1}
and
b_{n+1}
to the graphs of
p_{n}
and
b_{n}
, respectively. These imply that
p_{n+1}(0)=b_{n+1}(0)

if and only if
p_{n}(0)=b_{n}(0)
, for all
n\geq-1
.
Therefore, to prove the proposition, it is enough to show that
\alpha\in{\mathscr{H}}
if and only if
p_{-1}(0)=b_{-1}(0)=0
.

Assume that
\alpha\in{\mathscr{H}}
. Fix an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
. Choose
m_{0}\geq 1
satisfying
5\cdot 0.9^{m_{0}}\leq\varepsilon
.
Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon/2
be a positive constant. We may apply Proposition
8.4
with
x=\varepsilon^{\prime}
and obtain

m\geq 1
satisfying the inequality in that proposition.
By making
\varepsilon^{\prime}
small enough, we may make
m\geq m_{0}
.
Now, using the uniform contraction of the maps
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
in Equation (
3.7
),

\displaystyle p_{-1}(0)

\displaystyle\leq p_{-1}^{m}(0)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(ip_{m-1}^{0}(0))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi)+5/2)-\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))

\displaystyle\leq 0.9^{m}5/2+\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon.

That is,
b_{-1}(0)=0\leq p_{-1}(0)\leq\varepsilon
, for all
\varepsilon>0
. This implies that
b_{-1}(0)=p_{-1}(0)
.

Now assume that
b_{-1}(0)=p_{-1}(0)=0
. Fix
x>0
. Since
p_{-1}^{m}(0)\to p_{-1}(0)
, as
m\to+\infty
, there
is
m\geq 1
such that
p_{-1}^{m}(0)<x
.
Then, by the monotonicity of the maps
t\mapsto\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{l}(it)
,

\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi))\leq\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{0}\circ\dots\circ\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}(i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{m})/(2\pi)+5/2)=p_{-1}^{m}(0)\leq x.

By Proposition
8.4
, this implies that
\alpha\in{\mathscr{H}}
.
∎

8.6.
Hairs, or no hairs

Proposition 8.6
.

For every
n\geq-1
, the following properties hold.

(i)

If
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}\setminus{\mathscr{H}}
,
b_{n}=p_{n}
on a dense subset of
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
and

b_{n}<p_{n}
on a dense subset of
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
;

(ii)

If
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
,
b_{n}=+\infty
on a dense subset of
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]

and
b_{n}<+\infty
on a dense subset of
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
.

Proof.

Each of the strict inequalities in items (i) and (ii) hold at least at one point. That is because when

\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}\setminus{\mathscr{H}}
, by Proposition
8.5
, we have
p_{n}(0)\neq b_{n}(0)
, and
when
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
,
b_{n}(0)=0<+\infty
.
The main task is to show that each of the equalities in (i) and (ii) hold at least at one point.
To that end we show that there is
x_{n}\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
such that if
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}\setminus{\mathscr{H}}
we have

p_{n}(x_{n})=b_{n}(x_{n})
and if
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
we have
b_{n}(x_{n})=+\infty
.
There is an algorithm to identify the points
x_{n}
, as we explain below.

There are
x_{n}\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
and
l_{n}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, for
n\geq-1
, such that

x_{n+1}\in[1/(2\alpha_{n+1})-1,1/(2\alpha_{n+1})],\quad-\varepsilon_{n+1}\alpha_{n+1}x_{n+1}\in x_{n}-l_{n}.

By Lemma
7.2
-(ii), for all
n\geq-1
and all
j\geq 0
we have

|2\pi b_{n-1}^{j}(x_{n-1})-2\pi\alpha_{n}b_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n})-\log(1/\alpha_{n})|\leq 4.

One may literally repeat the latter part of the proof of Proposition
8.2
with
D_{n}^{j}=b_{n}^{j}(x_{n})
to conclude that
for all
n\geq-1
,

(8.16)

\big|2\pi b_{n}(x_{n})-{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{n+1})\big|\leq 8+2\pi.

Now, if
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
we must have
b_{n}(x_{n})=+\infty
.
Assume that
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}
. We have

\displaystyle p_{n}^{j}(x_{n})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{Y}_{n}+l_{n})\circ\dots\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{n+j}(x_{n+j}+ip_{n+j}^{0}(x_{n+j}))+l_{n+j-1})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{Y}_{n}+l_{n})\circ\dots\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{n+j}(x_{n+j}+i{\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{n+j+1})/(2\pi)+5/2)+l_{n+j-1}),

and

\displaystyle b_{n}(x_{n})=\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{Y}_{n}+l_{n})\circ\dots\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{n+j}(x_{n+j}+ib_{n+j}(x_{n+j}))+l_{n+j-1}).

Therefore, by (
3.7
) and (
8.16
),

\displaystyle p_{n}^{j}(

\displaystyle x_{n})-b_{n}(x_{n})\leq 0.9^{j}\Big(\frac{5}{2}+\frac{8+2\pi}{2\pi}\Big).

Since
p_{n}\geq b_{n}
on
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
, we conclude that
p_{n}(x_{n})=\lim_{j\to+\infty}p_{n}^{j}(x_{n})=b_{n}(x_{n})
.

To discuss items (i) and (ii) in the proposition at once, let us define
p_{n}\equiv+\infty
, for all
n\geq-1
,
when
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
.

Recall that the graphs of
p_{n}
and
b_{n}
are obtained from the graphs of
p_{n+1}
and
b_{n+1}
,
respectively, using the map
\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}
and its integer translations.
Thus, if
p_{n}=b_{n}
at some
y\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
, then
p_{n-1}=b_{n-1}
at
-\varepsilon_{n}\alpha_{n}y+(1+\varepsilon_{n})/2
.
Similarly, if
p_{n}\neq b_{n}
at some
y\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
, then
p_{n-1}\neq b_{n-1}
at
-\varepsilon_{n}\alpha_{n}y+(1+\varepsilon_{n})/2
.
On the other hand,
p_{n}
and
b_{n}
are periodic of period
+1
, for all
n\geq-1
.
One infers from these properties, and the first part of the proof, that
p_{n}=b_{n}
on a dense subset of
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
,
and
p_{n}\neq b_{n}
on a dense subset of
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
, for all
n\geq-1
.
∎

Proposition 8.7
.

Assume that
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}
. For all
n\geq-1
,
p_{n}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to[1,+\infty)
is continuous.
Moreover, if
\alpha\in{\mathscr{H}}
then
p_{n}=b_{n}
on
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
, for all
n\geq-1
.

Proof.

We aim to show that
p_{n}^{j}
uniformly converges to
p_{n}
on
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
. By the continuity of the maps
p_{n}^{j}
,
this implies the first part of the proposition. At the same time, we show that
p_{n}^{j}
uniformly converges to
b_{n}
, when

\alpha\in{\mathscr{H}}
, which implies the latter part of the proposition. We present the details in several steps.

Step 1.
For all
n\geq-1
, all
j\geq 0
, and all
x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
,

p_{n}^{j}(x)\geq p_{n}^{j}(0)-5/\pi,\quad b_{n}^{j}(x)\geq b_{n}^{j}(0)-5/\pi.

We prove this by induction on
j
.
When
j=0
,
p_{n}^{0}\equiv({\mathcal{B}}(\alpha_{n+1})+5\pi)/(2\pi)
and
b_{n}^{0}\equiv-1
.
Therefore,
p_{n}^{0}(x)\geq p_{n}^{0}(0)-5/\pi
and
b_{n}^{0}(x)\geq b_{n}^{0}(0)-5/\pi
.
Now assume that both inequalities hold for some
j-1\geq 0
and all
n\geq-1
.
Below we prove them for
j
and all
n\geq-1
.

Fix an arbitrary
n\geq 0
, and let
x_{n-1}\in[0,1/\alpha_{n-1}]
be arbitrary. Choose
x_{n}\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]

and
l_{n-1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
-\varepsilon_{n}\alpha_{n}x_{n}=x_{n-1}+l_{n-1}
.

If
p_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n})\geq p_{n}^{j-1}(0)
, by the monotonicity of
y\mapsto\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(x_{n}+iy)
, we have

\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(x_{n}+ip_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n}))-\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(x_{n}+ip_{n}^{j-1}(0))\geq 0\geq-9/(2\pi).

If
p_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n})\leq p_{n}^{j-1}(0)
, by the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
in Equation (
3.7
)
and the induction hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(x_{n}+ip_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n}))-\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(x_{n}+ip_{n}^{j-1}(0))

\displaystyle\geq 0.9(p_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n})-p_{n}^{j-1}(0))

\displaystyle\geq 0.9(-5/\pi)=-9/(2\pi).

On the other hand, by Lemma
7.3
-(i),

\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(x_{n}+ip_{n}^{j-1}(0))\geq\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(ip_{n}^{j-1}(0))-1/(2\pi)=p_{n-1}^{j}(0)-1/(2\pi).

Therefore, by the above inequalities and the definition of
p_{n-1}^{j}
, we have

\displaystyle p_{n-1}^{j}(x_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(x_{n}+ip_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n}))

\displaystyle=\big(\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(x_{n}+ip_{n}^{j-1}(x_{n}))-\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(x_{n}+ip_{n}^{j-1}(0))\big)+\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(x_{n}+ip_{n}^{j-1}(0))

\displaystyle\geq-9/(2\pi)+p_{n-1}^{j}(0)-1/(2\pi)=p_{n-1}^{j}(0)-5/\pi.

The same argument applies to the map
b_{n-1}^{j}
.

As
j\to+\infty
,
p_{n}^{j}\to p_{n}
and
b_{n}^{j}\to b_{n}
point-wise on
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
.
These lead to

(8.17)

p_{n}(x)\geq p_{n}(0)-5/\pi,\quad b_{n}(x)\geq b_{n}(0)-5/\pi.

Step 2.
For all
n\geq-1
and
j\geq 0
we have

p_{n}^{j}(x)-p_{n}(x)\leq p_{n}^{j}(0)-p_{n}(0)+55/\pi,\quad\text{ and }\quad p_{n}^{j}(x)-b_{n}^{j}(x)\leq p_{n}^{j}(0)-b_{n}^{j}(0)+55/\pi.

We shall prove these by induction on
j
. If
j=0
, by Equation (
8.17
),

p_{n}^{0}(x)-p_{n}(x)=p_{n}^{0}(0)-p_{n}(x)\leq p_{n}^{0}(0)-p_{n}(0)+5/\pi,

and

p_{n}^{0}(x)-b_{n}^{0}(x)=p_{n}^{0}(0)-b_{n}^{0}(0)\leq p_{n}^{0}(0)-b_{n}^{0}(0)+5/\pi.

Now assume that both inequalities in Step 2 hold for some
j\geq 0
and all
n\geq-1
.
We aim to prove it for
j+1
and all
n\geq-1
.
Fix an arbitrary
x_{n-1}\in[0,1/\alpha_{n-1}]
. Choose
x_{n}\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
and
l_{n-1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

-\varepsilon_{n}\alpha_{n}x_{n}=x_{n-1}+l_{n-1}
.
Then, using Lemma
7.3
-(ii),

\displaystyle p_{n-1}^{j+1}(x_{n-1})-p_{n-1}(x_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(x_{n}+ip_{n}^{j}(x_{n}))-\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(x_{n}+ip_{n}(x_{n}))

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(ip_{n}^{j}(x_{n}))-\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(ip_{n}(x_{n}))+1/(2\pi).

We consider two cases.

(1) Assume that
p_{n}(x_{n})\geq p_{n}(0)
. Using Lemma
7.3
-(iii), the induction hypothesis, and
Equation (
3.7
), respectively, we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(ip_{n}^{j}(x_{n}))

\displaystyle-\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(ip_{n}(x_{n}))

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(i(p_{n}^{j}(x_{n})-p_{n}(x_{n})+p_{n}(0))-\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(ip_{n}(0))+1/(4\pi)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(i(p_{n}^{j}(0)+55/\pi)-p_{n-1}(0)+1/(4\pi)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(ip_{n}^{j}(0))+(9/10)\cdot(55/\pi)-p_{n-1}(0)+1/(4\pi)

\displaystyle=p_{n-1}^{j+1}(0)-p_{n-1}(0)+199/(4\pi).

(2) Assume that
p_{n}(x_{n})<p_{n}(0)
. By Equation (
8.17
), we must have
p_{n}(0)-p_{n}(x_{n})\in[0,5/\pi]
.
Then we may apply Lemma
7.3
-(iv), and the induction hypothesis, to obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(ip_{n}^{j}(x_{n}))

\displaystyle-\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(ip_{n}(x_{n}))

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(i(p_{n}^{j}(x_{n})-p_{n}(x_{n})+p_{n}(0))-\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(ip_{n}(0))+5/\pi

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(ip_{n}^{j}(0)+55/\pi)-p_{n-1}(0)+5/\pi

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n}(ip_{n}^{j}(0))+0.9\cdot 55/\pi-p_{n-1}(0)+5/\pi

\displaystyle=p_{n-1}^{j+1}(0)-p_{n-1}(0)+109/(2\pi).

Combining the above inequalities, we obtain the first inequality in Step 2 for
j+1
.
The same argument applies to the difference
p_{n-1}^{j+1}(x_{n-1})-b_{n-1}^{j+1}(x_{n-1})
.

Step 3.
For every
n\geq-1
,
p_{n}^{j}
uniformly converges to
p_{n}
on
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
, as
j\to+\infty
.

Fix
n\geq-1
, and let
\varepsilon>0
be arbitrary. Choose
m\geq n
such that
0.9^{m-n}(1+55/\pi)<\varepsilon
.
Since
p_{m}^{j}(0)\to p_{m}(0)
, as
j\to+\infty
, there is
j_{0}>0
such that for all
j\geq j_{0}
we have

|p_{m}^{j}(0)-p_{m}(0)|<1
. By Step 2, this implies that for all
x^{\prime}\in[0,1/\alpha_{m}]
we have

|p_{m}^{j}(x^{\prime})-p_{m}(x^{\prime})|\leq 1+55/\pi
.
By the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{l}
in Equation (
3.7
), and since
\mathbb{Y}_{l}
maps the graph of
p_{l}
to

p_{l-1}
and the graph of
p_{l}^{k}
to the graph of
p_{l-1}^{k+1}
, we conclude that

|p_{n}^{j+m-n}(x)-p_{n}(x)|\leq 0.9^{m-n}(1+55/\pi)<\varepsilon
.

Step 4.
If
\alpha\in{\mathscr{H}}
, for every
n\geq-1
,
p_{n}^{j}\to b_{n}
on
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
.

By Proposition
8.5
, if
\alpha\in{\mathscr{H}}
, we have
b_{n}(0)=p_{n}(0)
for all
n\geq-1
.
Taking limits as
j\to+\infty
in the second inequality in Step 2, we conclude that for all
n\geq-1
we have

|p_{n}(x)-b_{n}(x)|\leq 55/\pi
.
By the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{l}
, and equivariant property of the graphs of these functions,
we conclude that
p_{n}\equiv b_{n}
, for all
n\geq-1
.
∎

8.7.
Proof of Theorem
B

In this section we combine the results from the previous sections to characterise the topology of the model

\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
.

Note that
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\partial\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
satisfies the relations

(8.18)

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\left\{s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in I_{-1},\operatorname{Im}w\leq p_{-1}(\operatorname{Re}w)\right\},\quad\text{if }\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}},\\
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\left\{s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in I_{-1}\right\}\cup\left\{0\right\},\quad\text{if }\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}.\end{gathered}

Proof of Theorem
B
.

Recall that
I_{-1}
is the set above the graph of
b_{-1}
, and
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is obtained from
I_{-1}

through a projection, see (
8.2
) and (
8.18
).
By Lemma
3.2
and Equation (
8.3
) we may extend
I_{-1}
and
b_{-1}

periodically with period
+1
. That is,
b_{-1}
is defined on
{\mathbb{R}}
and is +1-periodic, and
I_{-1}
becomes

I_{-1}+{\mathbb{Z}}
.
When
\alpha
is a Brjuno number, we also have the function
p_{-1}
, which is
+1
-periodic by Equation (
8.4
).
We may extend this function
+1
-periodically onto
{\mathbb{R}}
as well.

Part (i)

By Proposition
8.7
,
b_{-1}\equiv p_{-1}
on
[0,1]
, and hence
b_{-1}
is a continuous function.
It follows that
\partial I_{-1}
is equal to the graph of
b_{-1}
.
The map
e^{2\pi ix}\mapsto e^{-2\pi ix}e^{-2\pi b_{-1}(x)}
from the unit circle to
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is continuous
and injective.

Part (ii)

By Proposition
8.2
,
b_{-1}(x)
is finite for every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and by Equation (
8.5
),
b_{-1}\leq p_{-1}
.
Consider the function
R_{\alpha}:{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}\to[1,+\infty)
,
R_{\alpha}(x)=e^{2\pi(p_{-1}(x)-b_{-1}(x))}
, and then the set

A^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\{re^{2\pi ix}\mid 1\leq r\leq R_{\alpha}(x)\}.

From Proposition
8.6
we infer that each of
R_{\alpha}(x)=1
and
R_{\alpha}(x)\neq 1
hold on
a dense subset of
{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Using the continuity of
p_{-1}
in Proposition
8.7
, and Proposition
8.1
, we note that
for every
x\in{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
, we have

\limsup_{s\to x^{+}}R_{\alpha}(s)=\exp\big(2\pi p_{-1}(x)-2\pi\liminf_{s\to x^{+}}b_{-1}(s)\big)=R_{\alpha}(x).

Similarly,
\limsup_{s\to x^{-}}R_{\alpha}(s)=R_{\alpha}(x)
.
Therefore, by the definition in the introduction,
A^{\prime}_{\alpha}
is a one-sided hairy Jordan curve.

Since
I_{-1}
is a closed set,
\partial I_{-1}\subset I_{-1}
.
On the other hand,
p_{-1}
is continuous, and
p_{-1}=b_{-1}
on a dense subset of
{\mathbb{R}}
.
It follows that

\partial I_{-1}=\{x+iy\mid x\in{\mathbb{R}},b_{-1}(x)\leq y\leq p_{-1}(x)\}.

Hence,

\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\{e^{2\pi ix}e^{-2\pi y}\mid x\in{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}},b_{-1}(x)\leq y\leq p_{-1}(x)\}.

The map
re^{2\pi ix}\mapsto re^{-2\pi ix}e^{-2\pi p_{-1}(x)}
from
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}^{\prime}
to
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is a homeomorphism.

Part (iii)

Consider the set

A^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\{re^{2\pi ix}\mid 0\leq r\leq e^{-2\pi b_{-1}(x)}\}.

By virtue of propositions
8.1
and
8.6
,
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}^{\prime}
is a Cantor bouquet.
Here,
I_{-1}
is the set above the graph of
b_{-1}
, and
b_{-1}
takes
+\infty
on a dense subset of
{\mathbb{R}}
,
see Proposition
8.6
.
It follows that

I_{-1}=\{x+iy\mid x\in{\mathbb{R}},b_{-1}(x)\leq y\},

and hence,

\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}=\{re^{2\pi ix}\mid 0\leq r\leq e^{-2\pi b_{-1}(x)}\}.

Here,
re^{2\pi ix}\mapsto re^{-2\pi ix}
provides a homeomorphism from
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}^{\prime}
to
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
.
Thus,
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is a Cantor bouquet.
∎

Corollary 8.8
.

For every
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}
, we have the following properties:

•

\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
contains the ball of radius
e^{-\mathcal{B}(\alpha)-5\pi}
about
0
,

•

\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
does not contain any ball of radius more than
e^{-\mathcal{B}(\alpha)+5\pi}
about
0
.

Proof.

Recall that any point
w
with
\operatorname{Re}w\in[0,1]
and
\operatorname{Im}w\geq p_{-1}(\operatorname{Re}w)
belongs to

I_{-1}
.
Recall that
P_{-1}^{j}
is a decreasing sequence of functions converging to
p_{-1}
,
and
p_{-1}^{0}\equiv(\mathcal{B}(\alpha)+5\pi)/(2\pi)
.
Through projection
w\mapsto s(e^{2\pi iw})
, and adding
0
, we obtain the desired ball.
On the other hand, by Proposition
8.2
,
\sup_{x\in[0,1]}b_{-1}(x)
is at least
(\mathcal{B}(\alpha)-5\pi)/(2\pi)
.
With the project, we obtain a point with modulus at most
e^{-\mathcal{B}(\alpha)+5\pi}
outside
\mathbb{M}_{\alpha}
.
∎

9.
Dynamics of
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}

Here, we study the dynamics of
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
on
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
and
classify the closed invariant subset of this model.
In particular, we prove all parts of Theorem
C
in this section.

9.1.
Topological recurrence

Recall that a map
f:X\to X
, of a topological space
X
, is called
topologically recurrent
,
if for every
x\in X
there is a strictly increasing sequence of positive integers
(m_{i})_{i\geq 0}
such
that
f^{\circ{m_{i}}}(x)\to x
as
i\to+\infty
.

Proposition 9.1
.

For every
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
,
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is
topologically recurrent.

Proof.

Evidently, it is enough to show that
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}\to I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}
is topologically recurrent.
To that end, fix an arbitrary
w_{-1}\in I_{-1}
, and assume that
(w_{i};l_{i})_{i\geq-1}
denotes the trajectory
of
w_{-1}
.
We consider two cases:

(i)

there are arbitrarily large integers
m
with
w_{m}\in K_{m}
,

(ii)

there is an integer
m\geq-1
such that for all
i\geq m
we have
w_{i}\in I_{i}\setminus K_{i}
.

Let us first assume that (i) holds. Fix an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
. There is
m\geq 4
such that
w_{m}\in K_{m}
and

(0.9)^{m}\sqrt{2}\leq\varepsilon
.
If
w_{m}+1\in K_{m}
, then we note that

(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(\varepsilon_{0}+1)/2)\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{1}+l_{0})\circ\dots\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+l_{m-1})(w_{m}+1)

is defined and belongs to
I_{-1}
.
It follows from an induction argument that there is an integer
n\geq 0
such that the above point is equal to

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{n}}(w_{-1})
.
Then, by Equation (
4.2
) and the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{j}
in
Lemma
2.2
,
we have
|\tilde{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{n}}(w_{-1})-w_{-1}|\leq(0.9)^{m+1}\cdot 1\leq\varepsilon
.

If
w_{m}+1\notin K_{m}
, we may not directly apply the above argument, since
\mathbb{Y}_{m}+l_{m-1}(w_{m}+1)

may not belong to
I_{m-1}
.
However, there is an integer
l^{\prime}\geq(\varepsilon_{m}+1)/2
such that

|\operatorname{Re}(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+l^{\prime})(w_{m}+1)-\operatorname{Re}w_{m-1}|\leq 1,

and either both
(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+l^{\prime})(w_{m}+1)
and
w_{m-1}
belong to
K_{m-1}
, or both

(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+l^{\prime})(w_{m}+1)
and
w_{m-1}
belong to
I_{m-1}\setminus K_{m-1}
.
Note that, by Lemma
2.2
, we have

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+l^{\prime})(w_{m}+1)-\operatorname{Im}w_{m-1}|

\displaystyle=|\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+l^{\prime})(w_{m}+1)-\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+l_{m-1})(w_{m})|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathbb{Y}_{m}(w_{m}+1)-\mathbb{Y}_{m}(w_{m})|

\displaystyle\leq 0.9\cdot 1\leq 1.

Combining with the upper bound on the difference of the real parts, we obtain

|(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+l^{\prime})(w_{m}+1)-w_{m-1}|\leq\sqrt{2}.

Now we consider the point

(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(\varepsilon_{0}+1)/2)\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{1}+l_{0})\circ\dots\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}+l_{m-2})\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+l^{\prime})(w_{m}+1),

which is defined and belongs to
I_{-1}
.
In the same fashion, there is an integer
n\geq 0
such that the above point is equal to
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{n}}(w_{-1})
.
Using the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{j}
again, and the above bound on

|(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+l^{\prime})(w_{m}+1)-w_{m-1}|
, we conclude that

|\tilde{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{n}}(w_{-1})-w_{-1}|\leq(0.9)^{m}\cdot\sqrt{2}\leq\varepsilon
.

Now assume that case (ii) holds.
Given
\varepsilon>0
, we may choose
m\geq 4
such that for all
i\geq m
we have
w_{i}\in I_{i}\setminus K_{i}
, and

0.9^{m+1}\cdot 31\leq\varepsilon
.

There is
w^{\prime}_{-1}
in
I_{-1}
whose trajectory
(w^{\prime}_{i};l_{i}^{\prime})_{i\geq-1}
satisfies
w_{i}\in I_{i}\setminus K_{i}
,
for
0\leq i\leq m
, and
w^{\prime}_{m}=w_{m}
. It follows that there is an integer
n_{1}\geq 0
such that

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{n_{1}}}(w_{-1})=w^{\prime}_{-1}
.

The point
w^{\prime}_{-1}
belongs to
V^{\infty}
, and
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
at
w^{\prime}_{-1}
is defined using the second case
in the definition of
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
. Recall the map
E_{n}
defined in Equation (
4.8
).
Let us choose the integer
l\geq(\varepsilon_{m+1}+1)/2
such that
E_{m}(w_{m})+l\in I_{m}\setminus K_{m}
.
It follows that
|\operatorname{Re}E_{m}(w_{m})+l-\operatorname{Re}w_{m}|\leq 1
.
By Equation (
4.9
), we have
|\operatorname{Im}E_{m}(w_{m})+l-\operatorname{Im}w_{m}|\leq 30
.
Thus,
|E_{m}(w_{m})+l-w_{m}|\leq 31
.

Consider the point

(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+(\varepsilon_{0}+1)/2)\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{1}+l_{0})\circ\dots\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{m-1}+l_{m-2})\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{m}+l)(E_{m}(w_{m})),

which is defined and belongs to
I_{-1}
.
The above point is equal to
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{n_{2}}}(\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w^{\prime}_{-1}))
.
By the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{j}
, we conclude that

|\tilde{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{n_{2}}}(\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w^{\prime}_{-1}))-\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w_{-1}^{\prime})|\leq 0.9^{m+1}\cdot 31
.
By the above paragraph, this implies that

|\tilde{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{n_{2}+1+n_{1}}}(w_{-1})-\tilde{T}_{\alpha}^{n_{1}+1}(w_{-1})|\leq 0.9^{m+1}\cdot 31\leq\varepsilon
.
As
n_{1}
is independent of
\varepsilon
, this completes the proof in case (ii).
∎

Recall that a set
K\subset\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is called
forward invariant
under
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
,
if
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}(K)\subseteq K
.
The set
K
is called
invariant
, or
fully invariant
, under
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
,
if
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}^{-1}(K)=K
.

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
. If
K\subset\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is closed and forward invariant under

\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
, then
K
is fully invariant under
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Fix an arbitrary
z\in K
.
By Proposition
9.1
, there is an increasing sequence of positive integers
m_{i}

such that
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{m_{i}}}(z)\to z
as
i\to\infty
.
Since,
K
is closed, the sequence
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}^{\circ{m_{i}-1}}(z)
has a convergence
subsequence, which converges to some
z^{\prime}
in
K
. Evidently,
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}(z^{\prime})=z
, and hence

z^{\prime}=\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}^{-1}(z)\in K
.
∎

9.2.
Closed invariant subsets

In this section we build a family of closed invariant sets for
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
,
parametrised on a closed interval in
{\mathbb{R}}
.
The process is in analogy with how the set
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
is built in Section
3.3
.

Fix an arbitrary
y\geq 0
, and inductively define
y_{n}\geq 0
, for
n\geq-1
, according to

(9.1)

y_{-1}=y,\qquad y_{n+1}=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}^{-1}(iy_{n}).

For
n\geq 0
, let

(9.2)

\begin{gathered}\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{0}=\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}],\operatorname{Im}w\geq y_{n}-1\},\\
\prescript{y}{}{J}_{n}^{0}=\{w\in\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{0}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[1/\alpha_{n}-1,1/\alpha_{n}]\},\quad\prescript{y}{}{K}_{n}^{0}=\{w\in\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{0}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}-1]\}.\end{gathered}

As in Section
3.3
, we inductively defined the sets
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{j}
,
\prescript{y}{}{J}_{n}^{j}
, and

\prescript{y}{}{K}_{n}^{j}
, for
j\geq 1
and
n\geq 0
.
Assume that
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{j}
,
\prescript{y}{}{J}_{n}^{j}
, and
\prescript{y}{}{K}_{n}^{j}
are defined for some

j
and all
n\geq 0
.
When
\varepsilon_{n+1}=-1
, we let

(9.3)

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{j+1}=\bigcup_{l=0}^{a_{n}-2}\big(\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n+1}^{j})+l\big)\bigcup\big(\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(\prescript{y}{}{K}_{n+1}^{j})+a_{n}-1\big).

When
\varepsilon_{n+1}=+1
, we let

(9.4)

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{j+1}=\bigcup_{l=1}^{a_{n}}\big(\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n+1}^{j})+l\big)\bigcup\big(\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(\prescript{y}{}{J}_{n+1}^{j})+a_{n}+1\big).

Then, define

\prescript{y}{}{J}_{n}^{j+1}=\{w\in\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{j+1}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[1/\alpha_{n}-1,1/\alpha_{n}]\},\;\prescript{y}{}{K}_{n}^{j+1}=\{w\in\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{j+1}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}-1]\}.

Let

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}^{0}=\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid\operatorname{Re}w\in[0,1/\alpha_{-1}],\operatorname{Im}w\geq y_{-1}-1\},

and for
j\geq 1
, consider the sets

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}^{j}=\mathbb{Y}_{0}(\prescript{y}{}{I}_{0}^{j-1})+(\varepsilon_{0}+1)/2.

By the latter part of Lemma
2.1
,
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{1}\subset\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{0}
, for
n\geq-1
.
This implies that for all
n\geq-1
and all
j\geq 0
,

(9.5)

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{j+1}\subset\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{j}.

For
n\geq-1
, we define the closed sets

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}=\bigcap_{j\geq 0}\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{j}.

Evidently, when
y=0
, we have
y_{n}=0
, for all
n\geq 0
, and hence,
\prescript{0}{}{I}_{n}=I_{n}
, for all
n\geq-1
.

Note that,
iy\in\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
, for any
y\geq 0
, and

(9.6)

\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1}\subsetneq\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1},\qquad\text{if }x>y\geq 0.

Moreover, by the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
in Lemma
2.2
,

(9.7)

iy^{\prime}\notin\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1},\quad\text{if }y^{\prime}<y.

Recall that for all
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
,
\max(\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\cap{\mathbb{R}})=+1
.
We define
r_{\alpha}\geq 0
according to

[r_{\alpha},1]=\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\cap[0,+\infty).

If
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
,
r_{\alpha}=0
, and if
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}
,
r_{\alpha}=e^{-2\pi p_{-1}(0)}
.
When
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
and
t\in(0,1]
, choose
y\geq 0
so that
t=e^{-2\pi y}
and define

\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}=\left\{s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}\right\}\cup\left\{0\right\}.

We extend this notation by setting
\prescript{0}{}{\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}}=\{0\}
.
When
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}
and
t\in[r_{\alpha},1]
, choose
y\geq 0
so that
t=e^{-2\pi y}
, and define

\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}=\left\{s(e^{2\pi iw})\mid w\in\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1},\operatorname{Im}w\leq p_{-1}(\operatorname{Re}w)\right\}.

For all
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
,
\prescript{1}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}=\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
.
By (
9.6
) and (
9.7
), for every
r_{\alpha}\leq s<t\leq 1
,
we have

(9.8)

\prescript{s}{}{\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}}\subsetneq\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}},\quad\text{ and }\quad t\notin\prescript{s}{}{\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}}.

Proposition 9.3
.

For any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
and any
t\in[r_{\alpha},1]
,
\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}
is fully
invariant under
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
9.2
, it is enough to show that
\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}
is forward invariant.
By the definition of
\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}
, it is enough to show that for all
y\geq 0
,

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
is forward invariant under
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
. Let us fix
y\geq 0
.

Recall the decomposition
I_{-1}=\cup_{n\geq 0}W^{n}\cup V^{\infty}
in Equation (
4.5
).
Let
w\in\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}\cap W^{n}
, for some
n\geq 0
.
Let
(w_{j},l_{j})_{j\geq-1}
denote the trajectory of
w
.
By the definition of
W^{n}
,
w_{n}\in K_{n}
, and by the definition of

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
,
w_{n}\in\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}
.
Since,
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}
is translation invariant,
w_{n}+1\in\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}
.
Then, it follows from the definitions of
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
and
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
that

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}(w)\in\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
.

By the above paragraph,
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
maps
\cup_{n\geq 0}W^{n}\cap\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
into

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
.
Since
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}:I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}\to I_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}
is continuous and
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}
is closed,

\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
maps the closure of
(\cup_{n\geq 0}W^{n}\cap\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1})/{\mathbb{Z}}
into

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}
.
The closure of
(\cup_{n\geq 0}W^{n}\cap\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1})/{\mathbb{Z}}
is equal to
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}/{\mathbb{Z}}
.
∎

9.3.
Closures of orbits

Lemma 9.4
.

For every
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
, the following hold:

(i)

if
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
, then for all
y\geq 0
, the orbit of
iy
under
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}

is dense in
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
;

(ii)

if
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}
, then for all
y
with
0\leq y\leq p_{-1}(0)
, the orbit of
iy

under
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
is dense in

\{w\in\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}\mid\operatorname{Im}w\leq p_{-1}(\operatorname{Re}w)\}.

Proof.

To simplify the notations, let us first consider the orbit of
y=0
.

When
\alpha\in{\mathscr{H}}
, the set in item (ii) becomes the graph of the function
b_{-1}=p_{-1}
.
Then, the statement follows from the continuity of
b_{-1}
in Proposition
8.7
.
The non-trivial case is to prove the statement when
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{H}}
.

Let
\langle x\rangle
denote the fractional part of
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that is,
\langle x\rangle\in[0,1)
and

x\in\langle x\rangle+{\mathbb{Z}}
.
By Equation (
8.18
), in order to prove the proposition, it is enough to show that the set

\Big\{\langle-m\varepsilon_{0}\alpha_{0}+(1+\varepsilon_{0})/2\rangle+ib_{-1}(\langle-m\varepsilon_{0}\alpha_{0}+(1+\varepsilon_{0})/2\rangle)\Bigm|m\in{\mathbb{N}}\Big\}

is dense in
\partial I_{-1}
. This is because
s(e^{2\pi i(-m\varepsilon_{0}\alpha_{0})})=e^{2\pi im\alpha}
.

It is possible to prove both items in the proposition at once.
Assume that
z
is an arbitrary point, such that either
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
and
z\in I_{-1}
,
or
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}
and
z\in I_{-1}
with
\operatorname{Im}z\leq p_{-1}(\operatorname{Re}z)
.
Also, fix an arbitrary
\delta>0
.
We aim to identify an element of the orbit of
y
in the
\delta
neighbourhood of
z
.

Recall that
I_{-1}=\cap_{j\geq 1}I_{-1}^{j}
.
Choose
j_{0}\geq 1
such that
(9/10)^{j_{0}}\sqrt{2}\leq\delta/2
.
There are
j\geq j_{0}
and
z^{\prime}\in\partial I_{-1}^{j+1}
such that
|z^{\prime}-z|\leq\delta/2
and
\operatorname{Re}z^{\prime}\in(0,1)
.
By the definition of
I_{-1}^{j+1}
, there must be
w_{j}\in\partial I_{j}^{0}
and integers
l_{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
for
-1\leq k\leq j
, such that

z^{\prime}=(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+l_{-1})\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{1}+l_{0})\circ\dots\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{j}+l_{j-1})(w_{j}).

There is an integer
l_{j}
with
0\leq l_{j}\leq 1/\alpha_{j}
such that
|l_{j}-w_{j}|\leq\sqrt{2}
.
Let

z^{\prime\prime}=(\mathbb{Y}_{0}+l_{-1})\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{1}+l_{1})\circ\dots\circ(\mathbb{Y}_{j}+l_{j-1})(l_{j}).

By the uniform contraction of the maps
\mathbb{Y}_{k}
in Equation (
3.7
),

|z^{\prime}-z^{\prime\prime}|\leq(9/10)^{j+1}\sqrt{2}\leq\delta/2
.
In particular,
|z^{\prime\prime}-z|\leq\delta
.

On the other hand, since
\operatorname{Re}\mathbb{Y}_{k}(x)=-\varepsilon_{k}\alpha_{k}\operatorname{Re}x
, one may verify that

\operatorname{Re}z^{\prime\prime}=\sum_{k=0}^{j}\Big(l_{k}\prod_{n=0}^{k}(-\varepsilon_{n}\alpha_{n})\Big)+l_{-1}.

Any value of the above form is equal to
-m\varepsilon_{0}\alpha_{0}+(1+\varepsilon_{0})/2
, for some
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, modulo
{\mathbb{Z}}
.
This may be proved by induction on
j
.
From the definition of
I_{-1}
we note that
l_{-1}=(1+\varepsilon_{0})/2
. This implies the statement for
j=0
.
Now assume that the statement is true for all integers less than
j
. To prove it for
j
, one uses the relation

\varepsilon_{j}\alpha_{j}=1/\alpha_{j-1}-a_{j-1}
to reduce the statement to
j-1
.

The proof for non-zero values of
y
is identical to the above one; one only needs to employ the translation
invariance of the sets
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{j}
.
∎

Proposition 9.5
.

For any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
, and any
t\in[r_{\alpha},1]
,
\omega(t)=\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}
.
In particular,

•

if
s>t
,
s\notin\omega(t)
,

•

the obit of
+1
is dense in
\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate corollary of Lemma
9.4
and the property in Equation (
9.7
).
∎

Proposition 9.6
.

Let
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
. For every non-empty closed invariant set
X
of

\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{A}_{\alpha}
, there is
t\in[r_{\alpha},1]
such that

X=\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

If
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
and
X=\{0\}
, we let
t=0
.
Otherwise, let
X_{-1}\subset I_{-1}
be the lift of
X\setminus\{0\}
, which is a closed set in
\mathbb{C}
.
We inductively define the closed sets
X_{n}
, for
n\geq 0
, according to

X_{n+1}=\{w\in I_{n+1}\mid\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(w)+(\varepsilon_{n+1}+1)/2\in X_{n}\}.

We consider two cases below.

Case (I)
There is
N\geq-1
such that for all
n\geq N
,
\min\operatorname{Im}X_{n}\geq 4
.

We define the functions
h_{n}^{j}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to[-1,+\infty)
, for
n\geq-1
and
j\geq N+1
, as follows.
For
n\geq N
, let

h_{n}^{0}(x)=\min\operatorname{Im}X_{n}-5.

Then, define
h_{n}^{j+1}
as the lift of
h_{n+1}^{j}
by
\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}
.
That is, for
x\in[0,1]
, let

h_{n}^{j+1}(x)=\operatorname{Im}\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}(-\varepsilon_{n+1}x/\alpha_{n+1}+ih_{n+1}^{j}(-\varepsilon_{n+1}x/\alpha_{n+1})),

and then extend
h_{n}^{j+1}
over
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
using
h_{n}^{j+1}(x)=h_{n}^{j+1}(x+1)
.

We claim that for all
n\geq-1
and all
j\geq N+1
,
h_{n}^{j+1}\geq h_{n}^{j}
.
We show this by induction on
j
.
Let
n\geq N
.
Since
X_{-1}
is invariant under
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
,
X_{n+1}
is
+1
-periodic.
This implies that every point on the graph of
h_{n+1}^{0}
is at distance at most
(5+1/2)
from some element of

X_{n+1}
.
Then, by the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{n+1}
in Lemma
2.2
, any point on the
graph of
h_{n}^{1}
is at distance at most
(1/2+5)\cdot 0.9\leq 5
from some element of
X_{n}
.
This implies that
h_{n}^{1}\geq\min\operatorname{Im}X_{n}-5=h_{n}^{0}
, for all
n\geq N
.
Now repeatedly applying the maps
\mathbb{Y}_{l}
, we obtain the desired property.

By the above paragraph,
h_{n}^{j}
converges to some function
h_{n}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to[-1,+\infty]
, as
j\to+\infty
.
Moreover, by the relations in (
3.8
) and (
3.9
), for any
n\geq-1
we have

h_{n}(0)=h_{n}(1/\alpha_{n}),\qquad h_{n}(x)=h_{n}(x+1),\quad\text{for }n\geq-1,x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}-1].

One may repeat the argument in Step 1 of the proof of Proposition
8.7
(replace
p_{n}^{j}
with
h_{n}^{j}
) to
conclude that for all
n\geq N
and all
x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
,
h_{n}(x)\geq h_{n}(0)-5/\pi
.
Since
X_{N}
is not empty, and lies above the graph of
h_{N}
, there must be
x\in[0,1/\alpha_{N}]
such that

h_{N}(x)<+\infty
.
In particular,
h_{N}(0)\neq+\infty
, and hence
h_{-1}(0)\neq+\infty
.
Let us introduce
y=h_{-1}(0)
.

We claim that
iy\in X_{-1}
.
To see this, fix an arbitrary
n\geq N
.
There is a net of points on
X_{n}-5i
, at distance one from one another, which lie on the graph of
h_{n}^{0}
.
Lifting these points using
\mathbb{Y}_{j}+l_{j}
, we obtain a net of points on the graph of
h_{-1}^{n}
,
which by the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{j}
in Lemma
2.2
must be at distances
at most
1\cdot 0.9^{n+1}
from one another.
Moreover, those points on the graph of
h_{-1}^{n}
lie at distances at most
5\cdot 0.9^{n+1}
from some elements
of
X_{-1}
.
As
h_{-1}^{n}\to h_{-1}(0)
, one may conclude that there is a sequence of points in
X_{-1}
which converges
to
ih_{-1}(0)=iy
.
Since
X_{-1}
is closed,
iy\in X_{-1}
.

Let us consider the set
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
and the associated function
\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}
.
We claim that
h_{n}\equiv\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}
, on
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
, for all
n\geq-1
.
Recall the values
y_{n}\geq 0
from Equation (
9.1
), and note that
h_{n}(0)=y_{n}
,
for all
n\geq-1
.
Also, recall the functions
\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{j}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to[-1,+\infty]
.
Since
X_{n}
lies above the graph of
h_{n}
, for
n\geq N
we have

h_{n}^{0}(x)=\min\operatorname{Im}X_{n}-5\geq\min_{x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]}h_{n}(x)-5\geq h_{n}(0)-5/\pi-5.

On the other hand,
h_{n}(0)\geq h_{n}^{0}(0)=h_{n}^{0}(x)
, for all
x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
.
Recall that
\prescript{y}{}{b}^{0}_{n}(x)\equiv y_{n}-1=h_{n}(0)-1
, for
x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
.
Therefore, for all
n\geq N
and all
x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
,
|h_{n}^{0}(x)-\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{0}(x)|\leq 6
.
By the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{l}
in Lemma
2.2
, for all
n\geq-1
,

x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
and
j\geq N+1
, we must have
|h_{n}^{j}(x)-\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{j}(x)|\leq 6\cdot 0.9^{j}
.
Taking limits as
j\to+\infty
, we obtain
h_{n}(x)\equiv\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}(x)
.

Since
X_{-1}
lies above the graph of
h_{-1}
, and
h_{-1}=\prescript{y}{}{b}_{-1}
, we must have

X_{-1}\subseteq\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1},\;\text{if }\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}},\quad\text{ and }\quad X_{-1}\subseteq\{w\in\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}\mid\operatorname{Im}w\leq p_{-1}(\operatorname{Re}w)\},\;\text{if }\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}.

On the other hand, as
iy\in X_{-1}
,
X_{-1}
is closed, and
X_{-1}
is invariant under
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
,
the closure of the orbit of
iy
under
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
must be contained in
X_{-1}
.
By virtue of Lemma
9.4
, we conclude that the inclusions in the above equation are equalities.

Case (II)
There are arbitrarily large
n
with
\min\operatorname{Im}X_{n}\leq 4
.

Here, we may introduce
h_{n}^{0}(x)\equiv-1
, for all
n\geq-1
, and define the functions
h_{n}^{j}
as in case (I).
We note that
h_{n}^{j}
is an increasing sequence of functions, which converges to some

h_{n}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to[-1,+\infty]
.
Moreover, these functions also enjoy the functional relations
h_{n}(x+1)=h_{n}(x)
, for
x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
, and

h_{n}(0)=h_{n}(1/\alpha_{n})
. Then, as in the above paragraphs, we must have
h_{n}(0)\leq h_{n}(x)+5/\pi
, for all
n\geq 0
.
Since
X_{n}
lies above the graph of
h_{n}
, combining with the hypothesis in this case, there must be arbitrarily
large
n
with
h_{n}(0)\leq 4+5/\pi
.
By the uniform contraction of
\mathbb{Y}_{l}
, we conclude that
h_{-1}(0)=0
.
Thus,
0\in X_{-1}
.
Since
X_{-1}
is closed, and invariant under
\tilde{T}_{\alpha}
, the closure of the orbit of
0
must be in
X_{-1}
.
Using Lemma
9.4
, we complete the proof in this case.
∎

9.4.
Topology of the closed invariant subsets

In order to explain the topological properties of the sets
\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}
, we use height functions
as in Section
8.2
to study the structure of the sets
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
.
Since each
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
preserves vertical lines, each of
I_{n}^{j}
and
I_{n}
, for
n\geq-1
and
j\geq 0
,
consists of closed half-infinite vertical lines.
For
n\geq-1
and
j\geq 0
, define
\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{j}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to[-1,+\infty)
according to

(9.9)

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{j}=\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid 0\leq\operatorname{Re}w\leq 1/\alpha_{n},\operatorname{Im}w\geq\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{j}(\operatorname{Re}w)\}.

By the relations in (
3.8
)–(
3.9
), for all
n\geq-1
and
j\geq 0
,

\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{j}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to[-1,+\infty)
is continuous.
Moreover, by (
9.5
) and (
9.9
),

\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{j+1}\geq\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{j}
on
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
.
Thus, for
n\geq-1
, we may define
\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to[-1,+\infty]
as

\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}(x)=\lim_{j\to+\infty}\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{j}(x)=\sup_{j\geq 0}\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{j}(x).

The function
\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}
describes the set
I_{n}
, that is,

(9.10)

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}=\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid 0\leq\operatorname{Re}w\leq 1/\alpha_{n},\operatorname{Im}w\geq\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}(\operatorname{Re}w)\}.

By the definition of the sets
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{n}^{j}
,
\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{j}(0)=\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{j}(1/\alpha_{n})
, and

\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{j}(x+1)=\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{j}(x)
for all
x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}-1]
.
Taking limits as
j\to+\infty
, we note that for all
n\geq-1
,

(9.11)

\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}(0)=\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}(1/\alpha_{n}),\quad\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}(x+1)=\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}(x),\quad\forall x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n}-1].

These are the key functional relations required to explain the topology of
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
.

From Section
8.2
, recall the height functions
p_{n}:[0,1/\alpha_{n}]\to[-1,+\infty)
, for
n\geq-1
.

Proposition 9.7
.

For every
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
the following hold:

(i)

if
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
, for every
t\in(r_{\alpha},1]
,
\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}
is a Cantor bouquet;

(ii)

if
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}\setminus{\mathscr{H}}
, for every
t\in(r_{\alpha},1]
,
\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}

is a one-sided hairy Jordan curve.

Proof.

One may repeat the proof of Proposition
8.1
, replacing
b_{n}
with
\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}
, and using the
relations in Equation (
9.11
) instead of the corresponding ones in Equation (
8.3
).
(Indeed, one only needs to verify the first and the last paragraphs in the proof of Proposition
8.1
.)
That leads to the properties

(a)

for all
x\in[0,1/\alpha_{n})
,
\liminf_{s\to x^{+}}\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}(s)=\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}(x)
;

(b)

for all
x\in(0,1/\alpha_{n}]
,
\liminf_{s\to x^{-}}\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}(s)=\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}(x)
;

for each
n\geq-1
and
y\geq 0
.
In the same way, as in the proof of Proposition
8.6
, we also note that for every
n\geq-1
,

(c)

if
\alpha\notin{\mathscr{B}}
, each of
\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}=+\infty
and
\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}<+\infty

hold on a dense subset of

[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
;

(d)

if
\alpha\in{\mathscr{B}}\setminus{\mathscr{H}}
and
y<p_{-1}(0)
,
each of
\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}=p_{n}
and
\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}<p_{n}
hold on a dense subset of
[0,1/\alpha_{n}]
.

Then, one may repeat the proof of Theorem
B
, using the above properties (a)–(d)
to establish the desired result.
∎

9.5.
Dependence on the parameter

Proposition 9.8
.

For every
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathbb{Q}}
, the map
t\mapsto\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}
,
t\in[r_{\alpha},1]
,
is continuous with respect to the Hausdorff metric on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

By the definition of
\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}
, it is enough to show that the map

y\mapsto\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
, for
y\geq 0
, is continuous with respect to the Hausdorff distance.
To that end, we need to show that for any
y\geq 0
and any
\varepsilon>0
, there is
\delta>0
such that
if
|x-y|\leq\delta
and
x\geq 0
, then
\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1}\subset B_{\varepsilon}(\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1})

and
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}\subset B_{\varepsilon}(\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1})
.
Here,
B_{\varepsilon}(\prescript{z}{}{I}_{-1})
denotes the
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood of
\prescript{z}{}{I}_{-1}
.
Fix an arbitrary
y\geq 0
and
\varepsilon>0
.

We claim that

(i)

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}=\overline{\cup_{x>y}\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1}}
,

(ii)

if
y>0
,
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}=\cap_{x<y}\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1}
.

To prove these, it is enough to show that for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\prescript{x}{}{b}_{-1}(t)\to\prescript{y}{}{b}_{-1}(t)
,
as
x\to y
.
Let
(x_{n})_{n\geq-1}
and
(y_{n})_{n\geq-1}
denote the sequences defined according to
Equation (
9.1
) for the values
x
and
y
respectively.
If
|x-y|
is small enough,
|x_{n}-y_{n}|\leq 1
.
The inequality
|\prescript{x}{}{b}_{n}^{0}-\prescript{y}{}{b}_{n}^{0}|\leq 1
and the uniform contraction in
Lemma
2.2
imply that

|\prescript{x}{}{b}_{-1}^{n+1}-\prescript{y}{}{b}_{-1}^{n+1}|\leq 0.9^{n+1}
.
Since
\prescript{x}{}{b}_{-1}^{n}(t)\to\prescript{x}{}{b}_{-1}(t)
and

\prescript{y}{}{b}_{-1}^{n}(t)\to\prescript{y}{}{b}_{-1}(t)
, as
n\to\infty
, one infers that

\prescript{x}{}{b}_{-1}(t)\to\prescript{y}{}{b}_{-1}(t)
, as
x\to y
.

By property (i) above,
\cup_{x>y}B_{\varepsilon}(\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1})
provides an open cover of
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
.
Note that for any
x>y
,
B_{\varepsilon}(\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1})
covers all points in
\prescript{0}{}{I}_{-1}

above some imaginary value.
It follows (choose a finite cover) that there is
x_{0}>y
such that if
y<x\leq x_{0}
,

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}\subset B_{\varepsilon}(\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1})
.
On the other hand, if
y>0
, property (ii) implies that there is
x_{1}<y
such that for all
x_{1}<x<y
,
we have
\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1}\subset B_{\varepsilon}(\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1})
.

Let
\delta=\min\{|x_{0}-y|,|x_{1}-y|\}
.
Below assume that
|x-y|\leq\delta
, for some
x\geq 0
.

If
x<y
, by Equation (
9.6
),
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}\subset\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1}
and hence

\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}\subset B_{\varepsilon}(\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1})
.
If
x>y
, by the above paragraph, we have
\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}\subset B_{\varepsilon}(\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1})
.

Similarly, if
x>y
, by Equation (
9.6
),
\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1}\subset\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1}
and hence

\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1}\subset B_{\varepsilon}(\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1})
.
If
x<y
, by the above paragraphs,
\prescript{x}{}{I}_{-1}\subset B_{\varepsilon}(\prescript{y}{}{I}_{-1})
.
∎

It would be interesting to know the modulus of continuity of the map

t\mapsto\prescript{t}{}{\mathbb{A}}_{\alpha}
,
t\in[r_{\alpha},1]
.

This completes the proof of Theorem
C
.
More precisely, part (i) is proved in Proposition
9.1
.
Part (ii) follows from Propositions
9.5
and
9.8
.
Part (iii) follows from Proposition
9.6
and Equation (
9.8
).
Parts (iv) and (v) follow from Propositions
9.6
and
9.7
.
Here, when
\alpha\in{\mathscr{H}}
, we have
r_{\alpha}=1
and there is nothing to prove.
